# Casse-Robot — architecture

This document describes the project for those who contribute to it: the commands, the architecture, the technical choices, the recipes for adding a game element, and the roadmap. The contribution guide is [`CONTRIBUTING.md`](../CONTRIBUTING.md), the map authors' guide is [`maps.md`](maps.md).

Prototype of a multiplayer game of destructible block robots (inspired by Robocraft), in the browser. The documentation is in English; the code comments are in French. The long-term goal is a clean multiplayer game: the architecture must stay tidy (see "Architecture rules").

## Commands

Node.js 20 or newer. Strict TypeScript, Vite, Vitest.

- `npm run dev`: dev server (http://localhost:5173, opens the browser). It does not watch the built bundles, and only looks for its dependencies from `index.html` (vite.config.ts): a working copy of the repository stored inside the folder (its tsconfig files) made it empty its cache in the middle of a match. If the arena is no longer drawn after a dependency re-optimization (only the sky, "'<' : syntax error" in the console, a shader that is the HTML page), the browser is mixing old Babylon chunks, kept in its permanent cache, with new ones: two copies of the shader store (`ShaderStore`). Clear the page's cache (developer tools open, right-click on "Reload": "Empty Cache and Hard Reload").
- `npm run check`: type checking + tests. **Run it after every change.** Continuous integration (`.github/workflows/check.yml`) runs it on every push and every *pull request*, along with `npm run map:check`. The code is licensed under AGPL-3.0-or-later (`LICENSE`, `package.json`); the contributors' guide is `CONTRIBUTING.md`. The sounds and music in `public/sounds/` (ElevenLabs) are not under this license: their terms forbid distributing them on their own, outside the game.
- `npm run typecheck`: three passes, `tsconfig.json` (the whole project, the `tools/` scripts included, except `src/server/main.ts`), `src/shared/tsconfig.json` (shared code **without the DOM**, to guarantee that it runs on the server) and `src/server/tsconfig.json` (server, with Node's types).
- `npm run server`: builds the game server (`vite build --ssr` into `dist-server/`) and starts it on port 8787 (`PORT` to change it); it keeps the players' progression in `casse-robot-progress.json` (`PROGRESS_FILE` to change it, ignored by git), and also serves the compiled game page (`dist/`, `CLIENT_DIR` to change it; its status at `/status`). It plays the game's arenas from their files in `content/arenas/` (`ARENAS_DIR` to change it; `ARENAS=valley,canyon`: only those in the rotation, in that order), read and built at startup (an arena whose file is missing or unreadable leaves the rotation, and the log says why; without the Depot, the default arena, the room isn't created). It loads the community maps from the `maps/` folder (`MAPS_DIR` to change it; map files, and map packages as `.zip` archives or folders, whose resources it serves at `/map-assets/<sha256>`; `MAPS_ONLY=1`: only those in the rotation; an invalid map is left out, and the log says why). The client connects to it through the "Multiplayer" menu (`defaultServer`, client/net/netClient.ts: in development or under `vite preview`, `ws://<machine de la page>:8787` (the page's host); otherwise the page comes from the game server, which is therefore at the same address, over `wss` if the page is on https).
- `npm run share` (`tools/share.mjs`): share the game over the Internet. Builds the page and the server (`--no-build` to skip that), starts the server, then a Cloudflare quick tunnel (`cloudflared tunnel --url`, no account needed; an `https://….trycloudflare.com` link that changes on every launch) and prints the link to send, along with the address on the local network (that of the network card that goes out to the Internet, found with a connected UDP socket that sends nothing). A single address for the page and the match: the tunnel lets the WebSocket through. The server serves the page compressed (gzip, kept in memory per file: the ~5.8 MB bundle arrives as ~2.2 MB), and the files in `assets/` with permanent caching (`staticFiles.ts`, pure logic, tested by `tests/staticFiles.test.ts`: nothing outside the client folder). Measured through a tunnel: page loaded in ~2.5 s, WebSocket connection in ~0.3 s, ~19 frames per second and ~180 KB/s per player.
- `npm test` (once) or `npm run test:watch`; a single file: `npx vitest run tests/simulation.test.ts`; a single test: `npx vitest run -t "Grue"`.
- `npm run bench`: cost of a simulation step during 20 s of combat with 12 robots (`BATTLE_ROSTER`), broken down by stage of `Simulation.step` (measured by `sim.profile`; `sim.measure(nom, travail)` times a stage, and a sub-stage such as "ia › conduite" (AI › driving) is not added to the total). About 3.2 ms, of which 1 ms of physics, 0.5 ms of wheels and 1.1 ms of AI. The same test (`tests/perf.test.ts`) runs in `npm test` with a generous budget (there it shares the machine with the other test files).
- `npm run battle`: eight battles between AIs (Battle mode, headless) and a balance report: match durations, kill causes (`botKilled.cause`), average cost of a step, share of the time ground robots are stuck (in an unsticking maneuver, or blocked for more than a second), and per robot: kills, deaths, lifetime, time flipped over or stunned, causes of its deaths. At the Depot; another arena via `BATTLE_ARENA` (`BATTLE_ARENA=canyon npm run battle`), or a community map from `maps/` by its file name (`BATTLE_ARENA=val-du-lac npm run battle`). `npm test` plays two short ones (`tests/battle.test.ts`) to check that a real match runs without errors: this is how accesses to already-freed Rapier bodies (`RuntimeError: unreachable`) get caught. `BATTLE_RECORD=<file>` also saves the report as JSON: the **reference battles** (`tests/reference/battles-depot.json`, seeds 1 to 8, 240 s each), against which a moved or rewritten simulation (in a Worker, in compiled code) is compared with the same seeds. The simulation is deterministic to the bit: two battles with the same seed give exactly the same world (`tests/determinism.test.ts`, a fingerprint of every pose, velocity and hit point after 12 s of combat).
- `npm run perf` (`tools/browser/perf.mjs`): **rendering measurement**. Builds the page, serves it, and plays the reference battle in headless Chrome on the machine's graphics card, with no frame-rate cap: the twelve robots of `BATTLE_ROSTER` (those of `npm run bench`), without a player, respawning (`ENDLESS_BATTLE`), the camera behind the first one, `Math.random` seeded. It reports frames per second (per 5 s window, to see contention), frame intervals (median, 95 %, 99 %, worst), the share of frames too long for 120 Hz (8.3 ms) and 60 Hz, the CPU time per frame and per stage (`ArenaGame.frameProfile`: commands, simulation, views, effects, lights, HUD, sound, and "rendu", Babylon preparing the frame), the simulation step by stage (`sim.profile`), the GPU time per frame (if the browser has the timer query; waits included), draw calls, the GPU used. Options: `--arena=cityNight`, `--size=2560x1440`, `--preset=high`, `--seconds=60`, `--render-only` (simulation frozen: what a network client renders), `--renderer=webgpu` (WebGL by default; under WebGPU, the draw calls of the render passes and Babylon's GPU timer, `timestamp-query`), `--eval="…"` (code run in the page once the battle has started: `--eval="__casse.game.setGlow(false)"`), `--cpuprofile=profile.cpuprofile` (the CPU profile of the page's thread, saved for Chrome's developer tools, and its 30 most expensive functions by self time; build without minification for readable names: `BASE_PATH= npx vite build --minify false`, then `--no-build`), `--json=before.json` then `--compare=before.json` (a before/after table), `--no-build`. The machine must be idle. Reference (November 11, 2026, GTX 1660 Ti Max-Q, 1920 × 1080, Medium, Depot): ~33 frames per second, 29 ms of CPU per frame, of which ~16 ms of Babylon, ~7.6 ms of simulation, ~3.8 ms of effects; the GPU ~12 ms (waits included).
- `npm run render:check` (`tools/browser/render-check.mjs`): **rendering check**, also run by continuous integration. In headless Chrome without a graphics card (SwiftShader; `--gpu`: the machine's), the built game goes through each screen (title, a day battle, a night battle, the arena picker with every arena file downloaded and the selected plan drawn, the garage, the map editor), survives a WebGL context loss (`WEBGL_lose_context`: Babylon rebuilds everything on its own, and a banner says so, `hud/gpuNotice.ts`), and fails on any console error or exception (a shader that does not compile is one), any failed request, or a screen that does not draw a real picture (the canvas alone, the HUD hidden for a moment, captured and decoded: `Page.canvasImage`, `decodePng` in chrome.mjs; its colors must vary). `--renderer=webgpu`: the same tour under WebGPU (it fails if the browser does not offer it, unless `--optional`: CI runs it that way, since its Chrome without a graphics card may have no WebGPU adapter; on Windows, Chrome has none without a GPU; with `--optional`, a software adapter only, a fallback adapter or one that renders on the CPU such as SwiftShader, skips it too: on Linux without a GPU, CI's Chrome has one, on which the game did not finish starting in two minutes; the adapter is printed first, and a game that does not start says where its loading screen stopped), where the context loss becomes a destroyed device (`GPUDevice.destroy()`): the banner offers to reload at once, and the reloaded game must draw again under WebGPU. Screenshots of each screen go to `artifacts/render-check/` (`artifacts/render-check/webgpu/`; uploaded by CI on failure). The pages are driven through the console (`__casse.screens`: `title`, `garage`, `editor`, `benchmark(arena)`, `arenas(mode)`, `online(preset)`, `pilot(preset, arena, mode)` (a piloted match), `settings(tab)`, `bindings()` (the keys screen); `__casse.graphics(preset)`, `__casse.settings(patch)` (changes settings as the settings screen does, and returns them)), not through the menus, whose labels change with the language; the shared helpers (build, serve, launch Chrome, CDP) are in `tools/browser/chrome.mjs` (`CHROME_PATH` to pick a browser).
- `MELEE_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/melee.test.ts --silent=false`: the measurements from the melee weapon tests (reaction, precession, imbalance, ricochet, hammer); with `MELEE_BATTLE=1` as well: six battles where each team has its Reaper and its Pounder, their per-robot report (kills, deaths, time flipped over, hits) and the cost of the "contact" stage (the benchmark, `BATTLE_ROSTER`, has no melee weapon). Likewise for the energy weapons: `ENERGY_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/meleeEnergy.test.ts --silent=false` (blade, whip, lance, field), and `ENERGY_BATTLE=1` (six battles with a Duelist and an Urchin per team; more than half an hour on a loaded machine). Likewise for the biting weapons: `MELEE_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/meleeBite.test.ts --silent=false` (equilibrium spin, damage depending on spin, breach, grip, ricochet, drill, roll, network), and `MELEE_BATTLE=1` (the Sawyer and the Borer on each side, and the time they spend biting). And for the mechanical strikes: `MELEE_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/meleeMechanics.test.ts --silent=false` (flipper, flail, jaw), `MELEE_BATTLE=1` (six battles with a Catapult, a Crusher and a Thresher per team; `MELEE_SEEDS=1,2,3`: those particular seeds, to split them across several processes). For the spinners and the cutting propeller: `MELEE_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/spinners.test.ts --silent=false`, and `SPINNER_BATTLE=1` (six battles with a Tumbler, a Drummer and a Whirlwind per team; three minutes on an idle machine, nearly an hour on a loaded one).
- `npm run build`: type checking then production build into `dist/`; `npm run preview` to test it.
- `npm run native` (`tools/native/build.mjs`): builds the **compiled simulation** (`crates/casse-sim`, Rust) into WebAssembly and embeds it in base64 in `src/shared/native/wasm.ts` (like Rapier's "compat" package: the same module serves the page, the solo Worker, the Node server and the tests, with no file to serve apart), with the hash of the Rust sources (`tools/native/source.mjs`). **Run it after changing anything under `crates/`**: `tests/native.test.ts` fails when the embedded module was not built from the sources of the repository. It needs Rust (rustup reads `rust-toolchain.toml`: the pinned version and the `wasm32-unknown-unknown` target); a contributor who does not touch `crates/` needs nothing: the built module is committed. `npm run native:test`: the Rust unit tests (`cargo test -p casse-sim --features sim`, Rapier included: ~1.5 minutes the first time). `npm run native -- --probe`: the probe (the crate with its `sim` feature: Rapier and the ported simulation that owns its world, see "Compiled simulation"; into `target/probe/`; nothing is embedded; ~13 minutes on a loaded machine), which `tests/nativePhysics.test.ts` and `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` load (skipped without it); `npm run native -- --probe --dev`: the same, built fast for iterating (the `probe-dev` Cargo profile: no LTO, `opt-level` 1, the same floating-point results; ~3.5 minutes the first time, ~15 s after a change), the tests taking the newer of the two builds (`tests/probe.ts`). CI builds the module again and the probe, and runs the parity tests against them (the `native` job).
- `npm run map:check -- [cartes…]`: checks community maps the same way as the game and the server (a map file, a map package as a folder or as an archive; without an argument: those in `maps/`; exit code 1 if one of them is not playable; `--lang en`); `npm run map:terrain -- <image.png|relief.r16> --cells N --step S --low L --high H [--mountains DEPUIS,HAUTEUR] [--into carte]` imports a terrain into a map (`DEPUIS,HAUTEUR`: from, height; `carte`: map); `npm run map:example` regenerates the example map (`maps/val-du-lac.casse-map.json` and its source terrain `tools/maps/val-du-lac.png`); `npm run map:export -- <arène…|all> [--into dossier]` writes the game's arenas as map files, from their code (`arène`: an arena id; `dossier`: a folder; `content/arenas/` by default, the files the game plays: see "The game's arenas as maps"; **run it after changing an arena**); `npm run map:pack -- <cartes…> [--into dossier] [--zip]` and `npm run map:unpack -- <paquets…> [--into dossier]` turn map files into map packages and back, and a package folder into its one-file archive (`.casse-map.zip`) and back (`cartes`: maps, `paquets`: packages; see "Map packages"). The tool (`tools/maps/cli.ts`) is compiled by `vite build --ssr` into `dist-tools/` (ignored by git). `MAP_PERF=1 npx vitest run tests/communityMaps.test.ts --silent=false`: the cost of a combat step on each map in `maps/`, compared with the Depot and the Valley. The authors' guide: `docs/maps.md`.
- `npm run schemas`: writes the JSON Schemas of the two open formats from the code, `schemas/map.schema.json` (a community map) and `schemas/robot.schema.json` (a robot design): the generator (`tools/schemas/`, compiled by `vite build --ssr` into `dist-tools/schemas/`) takes the enums from the catalogs and the bounds from the map reader. **Run it after changing a format field, a bound or a catalog entry** (a block, wheel or prop type, an arena…): `tests/schemas.test.ts` fails when the committed files are no longer what the generator makes. It also checks that the bundled maps, the guide's minimal map and the preset robots follow them, and that the map schema accepts and rejects like `readMap` (every field of a sample map that has them all, changed to another type, around each bound, to unknown choices). It also writes the schema of each family of **element files** (`content/schemas/<family>.schema.json`, the files the game reads: `content/blocks/`, `content/props/`, `content/wheels/`, `content/weapons/`, `content/melee/`) and the **content catalog**, the game's elements as open data: `content/catalog.json` and its schema `content/catalog.schema.json` (see "Content catalog").
- `npm run models`: checks the glTF models (`public/models/<family>/<type>.glb`: `props`, `blocks`, `heads` for turret heads, `wheels`; one per type, a binary glTF that holds everything) and writes their manifest, `src/client/render/modelFiles.ts` (the file and a hash of each, one constant per family), from which the game knows which pieces have a model; it prints each model's size next to what the game expects (a prop's collision shape, a block's cell, a head's muzzle, a wheel's radius, width and symmetry), and refuses (exit code 1) a file it could not read, an unknown type, a type that cannot have a model (a wing or a control surface, a track roller, the head of a block that is not a turret), a `.gltf` with separate files, an extension the game does not read. **Run it after adding, changing or removing a model**: `tests/models.test.ts` fails when the manifest is out of date. `npm run models -- example` also regenerates the example model shipped with the game, the crate (`tools/models/crate.ts`); `npm run models -- example all` also writes the other examples (an armor block, a cannon head, a wheel: `tools/models/parts.ts`), to try them in the game. The artists' guide: `docs/models.md`.
- `npm run sounds`: prepares the generated sounds (`tools/sounds/build.mjs`, with ffmpeg): for each sound in `tools/sounds/catalog.json`, its kept variants (`tools/sounds/raw/<son>/`, ignored by git) become `public/sounds/<son>-<n>.ogg` (see "Sound"). Redoing everything takes more than ten minutes: `npm run sounds -- <son> [<son>…]` prepares only those (the encoding is reproducible, `bitexact`: a sound rebuilt without changes gives the same file).
- In the browser console, `__casse` gives access to the match: `sim(n)` advances by `n` steps of 1/60 s, `pause(true)` freezes the AI, `spawnWave(["tank", "heli"])` spawns those robots, `bots`, `world`, `camera`, `scene`, `applyDamage(point, dir, WEAPONS.cannon)`, `fireAt(x, y)` (without an ammo limit), `game` (the whole `ArenaGame`), `garage` (the `Garage`: `garage.load({ name, color, design })` opens a design in it), `presets` (the preset robots, `PRESET_BOTS`), `maps` and `testMap(carte)` (the community maps) and `editor` (the map editor: `editor.map`, `editor.history`, `editor.lastRebuild` and `editor.lastCheck` in ms) and `arenas` (the game's arenas: `load(id)` downloads a file, `loaded(id)` gives the built arena, `ready()` those whose file is there, `times` what each download and reading cost, in ms).

## Technical choices already made

- **Babylon.js** for rendering, **Rapier** (`@dimforge/rapier3d-compat`) for physics. Rapier was preferred over Havok because it also runs in Node (server), it is deterministic and it allows snapshot/restore of the world.
- **Babylon imported piece by piece:** the client imports `* as B from "./babylon"` (`client/babylon.ts`, which re-exports only the classes in use and registers the side effects: ray casting, shadows, instances), never `@babylonjs/core` directly (checked by `tests/architecture.test.ts`). An extra class gets added there. The bundle went from 11.5 to 5.8 MB, of which 4.2 MB is Rapier. Babylon's glTF loader (`@babylonjs/loaders`, pinned at the same version as the core) is apart, in `client/babylonGltf.ts`, which only `render/gltfModel.ts` imports, itself loaded on demand (`import()`, render/gltfModels.ts): it stays out of the main bundle (its chunk, ~195 KB, and those of the PBR materials it builds before the game converts them, ~140 KB each, are only fetched when a prop, a block, a turret head or a wheel has a glTF model). The WebGPU engine is apart (`client/babylonGpu.ts`), loaded on demand: a WebGL game does not download it.
- **Renderer: WebGL 2 or WebGPU (`client/render/engine.ts`, `createEngine`):** the "Renderer" setting (Graphics tab, `Settings.renderer`: `webgl` by default, or `webgpu`) is taken when the page loads (`main.ts`: the engine is created before `ArenaGame`, which receives it); `?renderer=webgpu` or `?renderer=webgl` in the address forces one (`requestedRenderer`, settings.ts; the measuring tools use it). WebGPU only if the browser offers an adapter; if it fails along the way, the game falls back to WebGL on a fresh canvas (a canvas that took a WebGPU context takes no other). The rest of the client only sees a `B.AbstractEngine` (`backendOf`: `webgpu`, `webgl2`, `webgl1`): the scene, the materials (Babylon's own WGSL shaders), our GLSL shaders (converted to WGSL by Babylon through glslang and twgsl, served with the game from `@babylonjs/core/assets`, not from Babylon's CDN), shadows, glow, clustered lights and post-processes work on both. **WGSL rule:** a filtered texture read (`texture2D`, `textureSample` in WGSL) must not sit in a branch that depends on a varying or an instance (WebGPU refuses the shader: "must only be called from uniform control flow"): read the textures first, then branch (`fxSprite`, the flame and the fireball). **Lost device:** Babylon 9.28 rebuilds a lost WebGPU device (`initAsync`) but not all of its resources follow (the first shadow drawn after it fails in `getBindGroups`); the game does not try (`keepLostDevice`): the banner offers to reload the page at once (`restoresAfterLoss`, `hud.gpu.reset`). The settings screen says which renderer is drawing, that another choice takes effect on reload (with a button), or that the browser refused WebGPU. **Why WebGL stays the default** (measured November 12, 2026, `npm run perf`, Depot reference battle, 1920 × 1080, Medium, GTX 1660 Ti Max-Q, runs alternated on a machine loaded by other work): WebGL ~39 frames per second, 24 ms of CPU per frame, of which Babylon preparing the frame ~15.7 ms; WebGPU ~33 frames per second, 28 ms, of which ~19 ms. The graphics card works much less under WebGPU (~4 ms per frame by its timer, against 12 to 16 ms in WebGL waits included, not the same measure), but Babylon spends ~20% more CPU time issuing the same ~210 draws (bind groups, pipelines); its "non-compatibility" mode (`engine.compatibilityMode = false`, which stops rechecking each draw's bindings) only brought that back to ~17.4 ms, at the cost of changes that would no longer be seen, and was not kept. WebGPU will pay off with recorded draw lists (snapshot rendering) and work moved to the GPU (phase 4), not by itself. 
- Versions pinned exactly (Babylon 9.28, Rapier 0.21): Rapier's API changes from one version to the next (`castRay`, `timeOfImpact`, joints…). Check the `.d.ts` files before upgrading. Rapier's generic joints have neither motors nor limits in JS: a joint with two degrees of freedom is made with two joints and an intermediate body.
- **Compiled simulation (`crates/casse-sim`, phase 4 of the engine plan, started):** the simulation is being ported to Rust, compiled to WebAssembly, **module by module, from the leaves up** (what depends on nothing else first, up to the simulation step), each module checked against its TypeScript original: `tests/native.test.ts` feeds both the same inputs (drawn by a fixed LCG, and the real data of the arenas) and requires the same outputs, to the bit (`Object.is`). The Rust code follows the TypeScript operation by operation (same order of floating-point operations, same neighbor order, same heap, same tie-breaks), so that a ported module can replace the TypeScript one without changing a single match. The TypeScript of a ported module stays, renamed `…Ts` (the reference of the parity tests, and the fallback when the module is not loaded); the public function calls the compiled module when it is loaded (`initNative`, done by `initPhysics`; `nativeReady`). The interface (`crates/casse-sim/src/ffi.rs`, `src/shared/native/native.ts`) is plain `extern "C"` functions on numbers and pointers into the module's memory (no wasm-bindgen: nothing to install but Rust): the TypeScript reserves memory (`alloc`), copies its arrays in, calls, reads the result, gives it back (`dealloc`); long-lived data (a terrain, a nav grid) lives in the module, named by a number (`NativeTerrain`, `NativeGrid`; a nav grid copied once, freed with it through a `FinalizationRegistry`). Ported so far: the deterministic math (`det.rs`) and V8's `Math.hypot` and `Math.round` (`jsmath.rs`); the terrain (`terrainHeight`, `rayTerrain`; not yet called from TypeScript: a call into the module costs more than the interpolation itself) and the nav grid's searches (`findPath`, `approachPath`, `nearestOpenCell`, `keepLargestRegion`: the AI's paths on Volcanic Island, the Harbor and community maps now run in Rust). The module weighs ~54 KB. **Physics, to the bit:** `@dimforge/rapier3d-compat` 0.21.0 is rapier3d 0.36.0 and parry3d 0.31 (its `bindings/typescript` build, features `fem` among others, no `enhanced-determinism`, no SIMD); the same rapier3d compiled here to WebAssembly (`crates/casse-sim/src/probe.rs`, feature `physics`) and the game's Rapier, fed the same world (a ground, 30 boxes and balls thrown at each other, the game's gravity, step and solver iterations; bodies created in the same order, rotations given as exactly unit quaternions), give **the same poses to the bit** after 4 s of collisions (`tests/nativePhysics.test.ts`). So the ported simulation, which will own its Rapier world, can be checked to the bit against the TypeScript one, collisions included; the transcendental functions are settled too: the simulation's own (`dsin`, `dcos`, `dlog`, `dpow`, see "Deterministic math" in "Architecture rules"), and those of `Math` that V8 and the module compute alike. **The ported simulation owns its world (first milestone, "a robot that drives", to the bit):** in the probe (feature `sim`, `crates/casse-sim/src/sim/`), not yet in the game's module. It reads the catalogs from `content/catalog.json` (embedded with `include_str!`; `json.rs`, a small JSON reader: `str::parse::<f64>` rounds correctly, like `JSON.parse`, so it reads back exactly the numbers `JSON.stringify` wrote; the shapes' vertices, rounded in the catalog, are recomputed like `shapePieces`, `shapes.rs`); builds a world like `Simulation` (`world.rs`: gravity, step, solver iterations, the common ground, the arena's scenery boxes given by the caller: the Depot's, for now); spawns a preset robot from its design like `spawnBot` (`lifecycle.rs`, `robot.rs`, `colliders.rs`: one part, its merged colliders, plates, shaped blocks and turret hitboxes, its wheels, in the same creation order); runs the raycast wheels (`wheels.rs`: `stepWheels`, `placeWheels`) and the driving without AI (`drive.rs`: `thinkPlayer` on the ground, `drive`, `driveWheels`, the steering geometry, brake, boost, righting, energy). What makes it bit-exact: **Rapier is called exactly as the TypeScript calls the game's Rapier** (`physics.rs`, after the TypeScript binding, `bindings/typescript` of the Rapier repository at tag v0.36.0: every body gets all of `RigidBodyDesc`'s settings, zero additional mass properties included, every collider all of `ColliderDesc`'s; `setRotationWrtParent` normalizes the quaternion then goes through an axis-angle; a capsule is built from its two end points; numbers cross into Rapier as f32, rounded to nearest like `as f32`, and come back as f64), in the same order (bodies and colliders created, impulses applied, poses read), and the TypeScript's floating-point expressions are copied operation by operation (`vec.rs` is vec.ts; the JavaScript semantics of `Math.max`, `Math.min`, `len(a) || 1`, `%` are kept). `tests/nativeSim.test.ts`: 21 ground preset robots (fixed, steered, small and heavy wheels, tracks, oriented blocks, mine layers facing back, every ground turret, shaped blocks: the Bastion), each in three runs in the Depot (along the southern alley: settle, full throttle, turn, boost, brake; up the western ramp: climb, hold on the brake on the slope, back down; against the southern wall: a gentle push, Rapier's contact solver with the robot's merged colliders, then back off), give the same pose and velocity of their part and the same pose, steering, suspension travel, spin, load and motor voltage of every wheel, **to the bit**, every second (8 to 9 s, up to 540 steps; the first try was bit-exact); the 8 block shapes give the same convex pieces; the other robots are refused with what is not ported yet (flying robots, engines, supports, wings, melee weapons). In these runs nothing is hit faster than `SAFE_SPEED` (the TypeScript side counts no collision); the rest of the TypeScript step (detection, air, weapons, fire, repairs, props) does not act on ground robots driven in a Depot without props: the test checks by the TypeScript's events that none of it happened. Measured on purpose: the zero additional mass properties and the turret hitboxes' poses do not change these runs (parry skips adding zero mass properties; hitboxes touch nothing), but they stay mirrored (a shot will read the hitboxes). Cost of a step with one robot (`SIM_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/nativeSim.test.ts -t coût --silent=false`, best of four runs on a loaded machine): the TypeScript step 0.63 to 0.82 ms, of which the stages the Rust does (driving, wheels, physics) 0.42 to 0.57 ms; the Rust step 0.06 to 0.13 ms, 4 to 7 times less. The probe weighs 2.4 MB (Rapier included; the `probe-dev` build 2.8 MB). **Second milestone, "collisions and damage", to the bit:** the ported step now also records the parts' motion before the physics (`captureMotions`), collects the step's contact force events in the order the binding's event queue drains them (`physics.rs`: an event handler that queues them like its `ChannelEventCollector`), groups them per pair of bodies, measures each clash as the TypeScript reads it (`contactPair` through the narrow phase: the pair's first collider, each manifold's world normal, each point's impulse and position in that collider's frame, that collider's world pose) with the closing speed from before the step, and above `SAFE_SPEED` strikes both sides (`collisions.rs`: `resolveCollisions`, `measure`, `ownerOfBody`, `strikeImpact`, `targetAt`; `blockAt` in `colliders.rs`). Then the damage batch (`damage.rs`: `DamageBatch`, its targets kept in the insertion order of the TypeScript's `Map`s, `keepMax`, the resistances, the shield, `resolveDamage`, the structure rule `WRECK_INTEGRITY`), the weak links (`structure.rs`: `weakLinks`, Tarjan's bridges, recursive like the TypeScript, the detachable side in its traversal order; `stressLinks`), the destroyed blocks and their debris (`debris.rs`: bodies of the world, their kick drawn at random, `MAX_DEBRIS`, their end of life), the splits and the wheels (`lifecycle.rs`: `makePart`, `removePart`, `splitPart`, `refreshWheels`, `attachWheel`, `moveWheel`, `releaseWheel`, `discardWheel`, `expireWheelWrecks`) and the rebuilt colliders (`colliders.rs`: `rebuildColliders` removes the old ones without waking the body, then `rebuildDirtyParts`). All of them create and remove bodies and colliders at the same moments and in the same order as the TypeScript: Rapier reuses the slots it frees in the order of the calls, and those handles order its pairs and its constraints. A robot now has several parts (`Sim.parts` by number, `Sim.order` for the iteration order of `sim.parts`: the wheels are grouped by part like `mountedWheels`); only the wheels of the cockpit's part are driven, and a robot out of action no longer drives. The randomness is the same: `Math.random` replaced by mulberry32 with the same seed, as `seedRandom` does (`rng.rs`), drawn at the same places (each turret's starting cooldown at spawn, unused but drawn; eight draws per debris), and the test compares the number of draws. What would happen without being ported yet is counted (`Stats.unported`: a destroyed battery sets its neighbors on fire, until the third milestone ported fire); not ported: the breach of an anchored prop (no props yet: the third milestone), a robot thrown by a flipper (no melee weapons), the AI's reaction to a hit. `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` now compares, every second and to the bit, every part's pose and velocity, every wheel (mounted, torn off with its own body, or removed with a frozen pose: its pose, state, hit points and where it is), every block's hit points and part, the broken links, every debris body, and the counters (collisions, damage batches, destroyed blocks, broken links, robots out of action, wheels torn off and removed, random draws): for the 63 driving runs, and for 18 crash runs in the Depot, robots without repair modules, none of whose batteries gets destroyed, rushed at full throttle and boost into the southern wall (Centipede, Buggy, Sapper, Trike, Ram, Scout), into a pillar (Crane, Tank, Brawler, Bulwark), head-on into the Tank (Bastion, Stilt, Scarab), or into the flank of a stopped robot (the Tank into the Bulwark and the Scarab, the Bastion into the Centipede, the Ram into the Crane, and into the Scout followed by a 27 s wait); the TypeScript side checks by its events that nothing not ported happened. Across these runs: blocks destroyed, links broken (the Crane against the pillar breaks into 19 parts, rammed in its flank into 21), the Sapper out of action by the structure rule (a broken link leaves its cockpit with too few blocks), wheels torn off by a hit on the wheel itself or with their support block, and their wrecks removed after 30 s, a hit partly absorbed by the Bulwark's shield (its batteries pay for it); a start shifted by one micrometer, or another seed (other debris), makes the comparison fail. Here too, the first try was bit-exact. Cost of a step (`SIM_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/nativeSim.test.ts -t coût --silent=false`, best of four runs, release probe, on a machine shared with other work): one robot driving, the TypeScript step 0.26 to 0.38 ms, of which the stages the Rust does (driving, wheels, physics, collisions) 0.23 to 0.35 ms, the Rust step 0.04 to 0.08 ms, 4 to 6 times less; the Centipede rushed into the wall (it breaks into five parts during the 10 s measured), 0.33 ms against 0.12 ms, 2.8 times less. The probe now weighs 2.5 MB (the `probe-dev` build 2.9 MB). **Third milestone, "the props", to the bit:** the arena's props are given by the caller like its boxes, spot by spot (`sim_add_prop`, its rests with `sim_prop_rest`), and created like `resetProps` does, in the order of the spots and before the robots, as the game does (`props.rs`: `createProp`, the density, friction, `prop` group and contact force events of its collider, an anchored prop as a fixed body sunk by `SINK`; `propHalf`, `propVolume`, `propMass`, `propHp`, from the props of `content/catalog.json`, which `catalog.rs` now reads: shape, mass, resistances, explosion, fuse, vent, anchoring, toppling, wood). Their motion before the step is captured with the parts' (a fixed prop has none); a clash between a robot and a prop strikes both sides (`collisions.rs`: a prop as an owner and a target, its resistances in the damage batch), and the breach of an anchored prop is ported (`breachOf`, measured with the other clashes; `breakThrough` after the hit: the robot gets the speed it keeps, the fragments of what it broke leave ahead of it, `struck`, or the tree it felled falls in its direction, `fellProp`, in the `felled` group until `restoreFelled`, whose test of a robot still inside is the binding's `intersectionWithShape`: `intersect_shape` of the broad phase's query pipeline with the same predicate, of which only the existence of a hit counts). A prop that gives way (`damageProp`) lights its fuse, vents its gas (the side of its leak drawn at random), topples (a tree: `releaseProp`, the binding's `setBodyType`, then `toppleProp`) or breaks; each step `updateProps` burns the fuses, pushes the venting cylinders by their leak (`applyImpulseAtPoint`) and detonates what has burned down: the prop is removed (what rested on it falls, `dropUnsupported`), it breaks into fragments (`spawnFragment`, now shared with the blocks' debris: a crate in small cubes, a wall or a trunk in big pieces of random size) and explodes (`explosion.rs`: `explode`, the area damage on every living block, every mounted or torn-off wheel and every prop by its distance to the surface, `propDistance`; the blocks and the wood it sets alight at random, `Explosion.ignite`; the blast, `blast`, pushing every block of every part within reach, the torn-off wheels, the loose props at their nearest point, `propNearest`, and the debris, then the links it stresses), which sets the next barrel's fuse: a chain. Fire came with them (`fire.rs`: `ignite`, `extinguish`, `igniteProp`, `extinguishProp`, `updateFires` in 0.25 s slices: a burning block's damage and its spread to its linked neighbors, a burning prop's damage in a batch of its own with a random direction, its charring, its spread to nearby wood, `spreadToProps`, and to the blocks of the robots that brush against it, `scorchRobots`), so a destroyed battery now sets its neighbors on fire. Not ported then: the ground fire puddles (the fourth milestone), the projectiles (no mine to blow, no missile to shoot down) and the energy walls (nothing stops a blast); a car that burns while its long fuse runs is ported, but no run has one yet. `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` now also compares, every second and to the bit, every prop (present or not, pose and velocity, anchored, hit points, state and time left, the direction of its leak, its fire and charring, until when it lets robots through, the velocity of its fragments) and every block's fire, with the counters of explosions, removed and released props, blocks lit and put out, and wooden prop fire events (the earlier runs too), in 13 runs: the Depot's props (the Ram rushed into a pile of crates, the Scarab gently pushing a crate, the Tank and the Bulwark into a barrel, its shield absorbing part of the blast, the Ram into the three barrels at the foot of a pillar, two of which blow up in a chain, the Buggy into a gas cylinder that vents, flies off and explodes, the Centipede into a gas cylinder then into crates) and small arrangements laid on the Depot's ground (the Ram and the Buggy into a pine, which the breach fells, lets them through, becomes an obstacle again, then breaks; the Ram through a plank fence bearing a roof, which falls; the Tank into a barrel next to a pine, which the explosion topples; the Tank into a barrel pinned against the south wall between crates, which catch fire; the Lancer backing into such a barrel, whose blast destroys its three batteries, which set their neighbors alight). Across them, crates broken, barrels exploded and in a chain, a gas cylinder vented, blocks, batteries and wood on fire, a tree felled, a wall broken through, a roof fallen. A robot or a prop shifted by one micrometer makes the comparison fail, and so does a change of the explosion's damage by one part in 10^15 (a change of one unit in the last place of the blast's direction does not: it vanishes when the impulse crosses into Rapier as f32). The first try was bit-exact again. Cost of a step (`SIM_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/nativeSim.test.ts -t coût --silent=false`, best of four runs, release probe, two measurements on a machine heavily loaded by other work, where one robot driving cost the TypeScript three times what it did for the second milestone): the Tank rushed into a barrel of the Depot (its 23 props in the world; an explosion and burning blocks during the 10 s measured), the TypeScript step 1.8 to 2.0 ms, of which the stages the Rust does (driving, wheels, physics, collisions, fire, props) 1.5 to 1.9 ms, the Rust step 0.53 to 0.57 ms, about 3 times less; into the barrel pinned between crates, 1.5 to 1.7 ms against 0.54 to 0.60 ms, 2.7 to 2.9 times less (the share of Rapier, the same compiled Rust on both sides, grows with the bodies in the world). The probe weighs 2.5 MB (the `probe-dev` build 2.9 MB). **Fourth milestone, "repair and ground fire puddles", to the bit:** a repair module now works (`repair.rs`: `updateRepairs` in 0.25 s slices, right after the fires, as in the TypeScript step). On the cockpit's part, it waits `delay` s without a hit (`Bot::last_hit`, which every damage batch sets on every robot it touches, like `lastHit`), then spends `rate` × 0.25 points on the most damaged blocks and mounted wheels within its radius (sorted by the share of their hit points they have left, a stable sort like V8's), then regrows one lost block at most every `REBUILD_GAP`: `regrowable` takes, in the design's order, the blocks that are lost (`Bot::lost`: destroyed, not the cockpit) or still alive on another part, within reach and touching a block of the cockpit's part, the nearest first, and keeps the first whose cell is free (`cellFree`: the binding's `intersectionWithShape`, a cube slightly smaller than a cell turned like the cockpit's part, with the same predicate: neither debris nor the robot's own colliders); `rebuildBlock` puts it back on the cockpit's part with 30% of its hit points (a destroyed block comes back to life; a block left on a torn-off part leaves it, which splits in turn or is removed, `splitPart`), restores its broken links, has its wheels follow (`refreshWheels`) and rebuilds the colliders. Two orders the TypeScript reads became explicit: that of a robot's living blocks (`bot.blocks`, a `Map` in which a regrown block comes last: `Bot::live`, which the explosions, the wood that scorches robots, the puddles and the module go through, and the random draws with them), and that of the cockpit part's `Set`, which the module's loop goes through while a regrown block enters it (a regrown module works in the same slice). The ground fire puddles came with it (`fire.rs`: `GroundFire`; `groundBelow`, the binding's `castRay`, now mirrored in `physics.rs`; `spawnGroundFire`: none under the arena's water, `Sim::water`, given by the caller like the boxes, one already burning there revived instead, 40 at most; `burnGround` in `updateFires`: the puddles that have had their time go out, the others burn, each in a batch of its own, the blocks and mounted wheels standing in them, except those of the robot that lit them, set the blocks alight at random, and the wood they touch), and an explosion with a `puddle`, the fire mine's napalm, leaves one under it, credited to its attacker (`explode` takes one). Nothing ported lights a puddle on its own yet (the flamethrower and the fire mine are weapons, and no prop explodes into one), so the test lights them as the game does: `sim_explode`, the explosion of a weapon's projectile at a point, credited to a robot, as when a mine bursts, and `sim_puddle`, the puddle a weapon's projectile leaves where it lands (`groundBelow` then `spawnGroundFire`, like the flamethrower's jet, without the chance its projectile draws), the TypeScript side calling `explode`, `groundBelow` and `spawnGroundFire` the same way; `catalog.rs` now reads the repair modules and the weapons' explosions and puddles. `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` now also compares, every second and to the bit, whether each block is lost, each robot's last hit, next regrowth, battery energy and order of living blocks, and every puddle (center, radius, until when, its author), with the counters of regrown blocks, block repairs, and puddles lit and put out (in every run), in 15 new runs. Nine with repair: the Mechanic, the Truck and the Guardian rushed into the south wall or a pillar, or rammed in the flank (by the Ram, the Bulwark, the Tank), then left alone to mend their blocks and wheels and regrow their lost blocks; the Truck pressed at an angle against the wall, and two robots that stay pressed against a repairer (the Bulwark, the Centipede), whose lost blocks have their cell taken by the wall, by the other robot's blocks, by one of its wheels (checked on the TypeScript side with its own `regrowable`). Six with puddles: a fire mine bursting next to the Mechanic, the Guardian and the Truck (blocks set alight, a puddle under them, then the repairs, including blocks left on a part that had come off, which rejoin the cockpit's part), or between the Scarab that laid it and the Mechanic (63 of the Scarab's blocks stand in its own puddle, which does not burn them; the Mechanic dies); flamethrower puddles under robots (one under its own author), against crates (which catch fire), in the air (no ground, no puddle), 51 at once (40 lit, the others refused until the first go out), on a puddle already burning (revived); and water at 0.4 m (the puddles on the ground refused, those on a pillar and on the ramp lit). A robot, or the point where the test acts, shifted by one micrometer makes the comparison fail. The first try was bit-exact again. Cost of a step (`SIM_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/nativeSim.test.ts -t coût --silent=false`, best of four runs, release probe, two measurements on a machine shared with other work): the Mechanic rushed into the wall, then its module at work, the TypeScript step 0.26 to 0.52 ms, of which the stages the Rust does 0.25 to 0.47 ms, the Rust step 0.06 to 0.15 ms, 3.2 to 4.2 times less; the fire mine next to the Truck (its blocks burning, the puddle, then the module), 0.36 to 0.75 ms against 0.14 to 0.20 ms, 2.6 to 3.8 times less. The probe still weighs 2.5 MB (the `probe-dev` build 2.9 MB). Not ported: the repair beam and the nanites (projectiles that repair an ally), the stun that stops the modules (EMP), and the projectiles that light puddles in the game. **Fifth milestone, "the AI that drives", to the bit:** a robot can now be driven by the AI (`sim_spawn_ai`, in a team), as in the game modes without combat (`GameMode.combat` false, `sim.combat`: the shooting range and the garage trial), where the AI patrols and drives but neither plans a fight nor aims nor fires: its tactics (`planCombat`) will come with the turrets, since as soon as combat is on every turret draws an aiming error at random each step it has no target (`randomInBall`), draws the ported step would have to make too. `brain.rs` is `thinkBot` and `thinkGround` (`AiState`: mode, destination, timers, stuck and blocked counters, reversing side, and the avoidance rays with how long they hold, which the TypeScript keeps in a `WeakMap`): the patrol destinations drawn at random (`wander`; without a match, no zone to take), the three avoidance rays recast every 50 ms (`avoidObstacles`: the binding's `castRayAndGetNormal` with a predicate and no groups, now mirrored in `physics.rs` along with `castRay`'s), the corners no tighter than the robot's stability allows (`limitCornering`, `rolloverAccel`), the U-turn, reversing while counter-steering when it is stuck (a random side without steered wheels, `randSign`), the gyroscope's hop when it stays blocked (`unstick`), and the reaction to a hit (`onHit`: a clash between two robots, or an explosion, whose origin `inflict` and `explode` now pass along, `react`; it flees, or charges if its preset's `behavior` says so, which `catalog.rs` now reads). The ported pilot now writes its robot's AI mode as `thinkPlayer` does (flipped or patrol), and every robot records its last position (`lastPos`). `navigation.rs` is `navigate` in an arena without terrain: the obstacles (the scenery's boxes of the kinds that stop a robot, the boxes now carrying their kind and their pose as the arena gives it, `sim_add_box`; the standing anchored props, by their footprint, `footingOf`), the first one the straight line crosses (`entry`, `clip`) and its most convenient corner, and a destination on a platform reached by its most convenient ramp (`rampPath`). `detection.rs` is `updateDetection` (by sight, the binding's `castRay` with a predicate, or by radar, which a jammer limits; `catalog.rs` reads the radars and jammers): the AI without combat does not read it yet, but it is compared. The scenery can be replaced as `loadArena` does (`sim_clear_scenery`: a new `Simulation` has the Depot's, whose bodies are removed before the first step, and Rapier reuses their slots), and the arena's bounds are given (`sim_set_bounds`): the four small arenas, Depot, Fortress, Trenches and Construction Site, are played. Not ported: the nav grid and graph (no terrain yet), the city (`urban`), bridges and tall ramps (an arena that has any counts `Stats.unported` each time navigation runs), the buildings made of props, zones and matches, the AI's pause, lava, deep water, mines, boosters, supports and melee weapons. `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` now also compares, every second and to the bit, every robot's AI state (mode, destination, timers, counters, reversing side, last position), the point its navigation would drive it to from its last position to its destination (`navigate`, a pure query in these arenas, asked on both sides), and what each team has spotted, in every run (a piloted robot's AI state is written by `thinkPlayer` and by the hits), and in 27 new ones (AI robots: `Start.ai`, their team; another arena: `Run.arena`): each of the 21 ported robots patrolling alone in the Depot for 25 s; four against four for a minute in the Depot, the Fortress and the Construction Site, and three against three in the Trenches, with their props; a piloted Tank circling at full throttle among AI robots (the garage trial); a fire mine bursting among four AI robots. Across them (counted on the TypeScript side): 326 patrol destinations, 1,528 samples where navigation aims at a corner or a ramp rather than the destination, 682 steps on a Construction Site platform reached by a ramp, 127 reverse maneuvers, 5 hops to get free, 6 robots flipped and righted, 38 flights and 10 charges after a clash or an explosion, enemies spotted, and 98 times hidden by the scenery. A robot shifted by one micrometer, or another seed, makes the comparison fail. The AI's driving was bit-exact at the first try; the only difference found was the pilot's AI mode, which `thinkPlayer` rewrites every step. Cost of a step (`SIM_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/nativeSim.test.ts -t coût --silent=false`, best of four runs, release probe, two measurements on a machine shared with other work): four against four in the Depot with its props, the TypeScript step 1.36 to 1.47 ms, of which the stages the Rust does 1.32 to 1.43 ms (the "ia" stage 0.31 ms: driving 0.21 ms, of which the avoidance rays 0.07 ms), the Rust step 0.50 to 0.56 ms, 2.6 to 2.7 times less; six against six, 1.90 to 2.05 ms against 0.66 to 0.73 ms, 2.7 to 2.8 times less (the "ia" stage 0.46 ms; in a battle, with combat, the AI costs ~1.1 ms: its turrets and tactics). The probe weighs 2.6 MB (the `probe-dev` build 3.0 MB). **Porting a module (recipe):** its Rust in `crates/casse-sim/src/<module>.rs` (comments in French, like the TypeScript), following the TypeScript operation by operation; its `extern "C"` functions in ffi.rs; its wrapper in native.ts; the TypeScript renamed `…Ts` and the public function dispatching to the module; a parity test in tests/native.test.ts (random inputs and real game data, compared with `Object.is` or `toEqual`); `npm run native`, `npm run native:test`, `npm run check`. A part of the step that touches the physics goes in `crates/casse-sim/src/sim/` instead (Rapier called through `physics.rs`, never directly: one more binding call is mirrored there first), with its case in `tests/nativeSim.test.ts` (robots, piloted or driven by the AI, and the arena's props, a run, their state compared every second, what the TypeScript must not do on its side checked by its events, `PORTED_EVENTS`); the bodies and colliders it creates or removes are created or removed at the same moment and in the same order as in the TypeScript, its random draws (`sim.rng`) at the same places, and the `Map`s and `Set`s of the TypeScript it goes through keep their order (an entry removed then added again comes last; one added while a loop goes through it is still seen); `npm run native -- --probe --dev` to iterate, `npm run native -- --probe` to check with the release build.
- **Authoritative-server multiplayer (done, first version):** the clients send only their inputs (`PlayerInput`), the Node server simulates with Rapier at a fixed step and sends 20 frames per second (events + binary snapshot), the clients replay 100 ms behind, interpolating. Transport: WebSocket (`ws`). No client-side prediction or snapshot compression yet.

## Architecture

```
src/shared/   pure simulation: Rapier, no DOM, no Babylon (will also run on the server)
  config.ts         physical constants (fixed step, block size, wheels, energy, view…)
  modes.ts          game modes (GAME_MODES): piloted robot or free camera, match rules, team composition
  catalog/          data catalogs: blocks (engines, wings, turrets, supports, equipment), block shapes
                    (shapes.ts), wheels, weapons, melee weapons (melee.ts), props; reading the element files
                    of content/ (content.ts)
  design/           robot designs (RobotDesign, JSON), DesignGrid, validateDesign, preset robots,
                    build budget (budget.ts), garage robots (custom.ts), part clearance
                    (clearance.ts), track roller groups (tracks.ts)
  arena/            arenas as data: the types and the game's arenas played from their files (layout.ts: ARENA_IDS,
                    ARENAS, arenaById), the code that makes them, source of their files (sources.ts: the small
                    arenas and ARENA_SOURCES; the Valley: valley.ts; the Canyon: canyon.ts; the Volcanic Island:
                    island.ts; the City: city.ts; the Harbor: port.ts), building blocks (builders.ts; houses and
                    tenements made of props: buildings.ts), ground palettes (grounds.ts), terrain (terrain.ts), static
                    scenery (arena.ts), buildings and trees (structures.ts), props (props.ts), air gusts (air.ts),
                    ray casting on the scenery (ray.ts), lava, deep water, fumaroles and waterfalls (features.ts),
                    terrain-aware AI navigation: nav grid (navgrid.ts) or nav graph of points (navGraph.ts)
  sim/              Simulation (world + fixed step), entities, events, waves and teams (wave.ts),
                    detection (detection.ts), player's robot (player.ts), matches (match.ts), terrain hazards (hazards.ts)
  robot/            lifecycle, destruction, weak links (structure.ts), wheels (wheels.ts), steering,
                    propulsion, wheelless supports (supports.ts), energy (energy.ts), engine flame jets
                    (exhaust.ts: their geometry, without physics), wings and control surfaces in runs (surfaces.ts), queries
  ai/               AI: driving (brain), going around the scenery (navigation), combat tactics (tactics),
                    plane pilot (pilot), turret fire (gunner), melee weapons (brawl)
  combat/           projectiles, turrets, lobbed fire (ballistics), damage, collisions (collisions.ts), fire (burning,
                    fire), what engine jets burn (scorch), electromagnetic pulse (emp), smoke (smoke), ammo and heat (arsenal), repair,
                    energy walls (barriers), debris, melee weapons (melee: spin, hits, gyroscope, imbalance;
                    mechanics: flipper, flail, jaw)
  net/              multiplayer: protocol (messages, network events, frames), binary snapshots,
                    encoder (server), world replica (client)
  native/           the compiled simulation (crates/casse-sim) seen from TypeScript: loading (initNative), wrappers of
                    the ported modules (native.ts), the embedded module (wasm.ts, made by npm run native)
  map/              community maps: format (MapFile, format.ts), reading untrusted input (read.ts),
                    conversion into an arena (build.ts) and prefabs (prefabs.ts), validation (validate.ts), identifier
                    (key.ts), terrain in base64 (codec.ts), terrain import (heightmap.ts: PNG, RAW) and PNG (png.ts),
                    an arena written as a map (export.ts), the text of a map file (text.ts), map packages (pack.ts),
                    the game's arenas read from their files (gameArenas.ts);
                    for the editor: terrain brushes (brush.ts), editing operations (edit.ts), history
                    (history.ts), palette (palette.ts), fields of each kind of element (fields.ts)
  media/            files that come from elsewhere (a map package): what the game needs of a glTF binary model
                    (glb.ts, with GLTF_EXTENSIONS) and of an Ogg Opus sound (ogg.ts), zip archives (zip.ts),
                    SHA-256 (sha256.ts), UTF-8 without the DOM's types (utf8.ts)
src/server/   Node server: game room (room.ts, without transport, testable), WebSocket entry point (main.ts: also
              serves the page, and the resources of the maps it plays), what the HTTP side serves (staticFiles.ts)
src/client/   browser: Babylon, HUD, input, sound
  main.ts           entry point (initializes Rapier, creates the engine, ArenaGame and App)
  babylon.ts        Babylon, imported piece by piece; babylonGpu.ts: the WebGPU engine, loaded on demand
  app.ts            App: main menu, mode and robot selection, match, pause, end of match, garage, settings
  settings.ts       player settings (Settings, localStorage), lenient parsing (parseSettings), graphics presets,
                    the preset of the first launch (firstRunPreset: the lightest on a phone)
  menu/             Menu: HTML panel built from a description (MenuView: entries, tabs, setting rows,
                    free content); grids of cards to choose from (cardGrid.ts; moving in 2D: gridNav.ts);
                    robot picker (robotPicker.ts; cards, filters and sorting: robotCatalog.ts); arena picker
                    (arenaPicker.ts) and the top-down plan of an arena (arenaPreview.ts)
  game.ts           ArenaGame: brings together simulation, rendering, HUD, input, sound; fixed-step loop
  garage/           robot editor (its own scene), clearance shown in transparency (clearanceView.ts)
                    and saved robots (localStorage)
  net/              WebSocket link to the server (NetClient)
  maps/             the browser's community maps: library (library.ts: shipped, imported, received),
                    IndexedDB (db.ts: maps, editor draft, package resources), files (files.ts: import, export, a
                    package, terrain from an image, bounded (de)compression), package resources (assets.ts);
                    the game's arenas: the small ones in the bundle (smallArenas.ts), the large ones on demand (gameArenas.ts)
  editor/           map editor (its own scene): state and commands (editor.ts), drawing and overlay
                    (editorView.ts, draping.ts), HTML panels (editorUi.ts), tools (tools.ts), checking in a
                    Worker (checker.ts, checkWorker.ts), draft (drafts.ts)
  i18n/             languages: `t` and the message types (i18n.ts), displayed language (state.ts), numbers, percentages
                    and accent-insensitive search (format.ts), names of what comes from shared code and the server (names.ts),
                    page texts (dom.ts), message catalogs per language (locales/<langue>/, one file per part of the game)
  audio/            spatialized sounds triggered by SimEvents: recorded (samples.ts), otherwise synthesized (Web Audio);
                    music (music.ts: tracks streamed, chained, skipped; playlists with their history)
  babylonGltf.ts    Babylon's glTF loader, imported only by render/gltfModel.ts (loaded on demand), and the glTF
                    extensions the game reads (gltfExtensions.ts)
  render/           scene, materials, models, props (propModels.ts), glTF models of props, blocks, turret heads and
                    wheels (the reader: gltfModel.ts, read on demand; reading per scene and adopting: gltfModels.ts;
                    blocks, heads, wheels: partModels.ts; the manifest modelFiles.ts, written by npm run models),
                    arena scenery and terrain (ArenaView), zones (ZoneView),
                    effects (fx/: particles, streaks, ground marks and the systems that emit them; realistic smoke
                    and flames: sprites.ts, flames.ts, fxMaterial.ts, computed textures fxNoise.ts),
                    optional lighting (lighting.ts: glow, lights, heat haze), night and
                    clustered lights (nightLights.ts: the pool; lightSources.ts: what gives light;
                    lightChoice.ts: choosing the lights; lightBeams.ts: light cones in the air;
                    tracers.ts: tracers),
                    WorldView and ProjectileView (event-driven meshes), melee weapons (meleeView.ts;
                    their models: models/melee.ts, and models/meleeEnergy.ts for energy weapons and what glows),
                    LocomotionView (tracks, legs, pads, blades), smoke, effects, labels,
                    3D preview of a robot (robotPreview.ts) and its 2D thumbnail (robotSketch.ts), what sticks out
                    of blocks and wheels, motionless, outside the game (stillParts.ts), profile of wings and
                    control surfaces (surfaceModels.ts)
  hud/, input/      HTML HUD (markup in index.html): weapons and Tab scoreboard (hud.ts), match (matchHud),
                    minimap and map (minimap.ts), music player (nowPlaying.ts), playing by touch (touchHud.ts:
                    pause, notes), full screen (fullscreen.ts); keys (action table: bindings.ts), gamepad (gamepad.ts), keyboard,
                    mouse and touch (controls.ts, touchPad.ts; the camera by touch: touchLook.ts), camera
tools/sounds/  generated sounds: catalog (prompts, settings, kept variants) and preparation (build.mjs)
tools/maps/    command-line map tool (cli.ts: check, terrain, example, export, pack, unpack), the example map (example.ts),
               the game's arenas as map files (arenas.ts: their names in each language), a package's LICENSE.txt (packages.ts)
tools/schemas/ JSON Schema generator (schemas.ts: the schemas, from the code; catalog.ts: the content catalog and its schema;
               build.ts: writes them, npm run schemas)
tools/models/  glTF models (props, blocks, turret heads, wheels): manifest (manifest.ts), .glb writer and checker (glb.ts),
               the examples made by code (builder.ts: boxes and cylinders; crate.ts: the crate; parts.ts: an armor
               block, a cannon head, a wheel), npm run models (build.ts)
tools/native/  builds the compiled simulation into src/shared/native/wasm.ts (build.mjs, npm run native), hash of its sources (source.mjs)
crates/casse-sim/ the compiled simulation (Rust → WebAssembly): det.rs (deterministic math, as src/shared/math/det.ts),
               jsmath.rs (V8's Math.hypot and Math.round), terrain.rs, navgrid.rs (ported from src/shared/arena),
               ffi.rs (the module's functions), probe.rs (the physics probe, feature `physics`), sim/ (the ported
               simulation that owns its Rapier world, feature `sim`: catalog.rs and json.rs, the catalogs read from
               content/catalog.json; physics.rs, Rapier called the way the game calls it; world.rs, robot.rs,
               lifecycle.rs, colliders.rs, shapes.rs, wheels.rs, drive.rs; collisions.rs, damage.rs, structure.rs,
               debris.rs; props.rs, explosion.rs, fire.rs (the fire of blocks and wood, the ground fire puddles);
               repair.rs; brain.rs (the AI's driving), navigation.rs, detection.rs; rng.rs, vec.rs); Cargo.toml
               and rust-toolchain.toml at the root
schemas/       JSON Schemas of the open formats: a community map, a robot design (generated: npm run schemas)
content/       the game's elements as data: one file per element, which the game reads (blocks/, wheels/, weapons/, melee/, props/), the schema of
               each family (schemas/, generated), and catalog.json with its schema (generated: npm run schemas)
maps/          community maps shipped with the game and loaded by the server (.casse-map.json)
content/arenas/ the game's arenas written as map files, which the game, the solo Worker, the server and the tests play
               (generated from their code: npm run map:export -- all)
docs/          map authors' guide (maps.md), artists' guide to the 3D models (models.md)
public/sounds/ game sounds (Opus), served as is
public/models/ glTF models (props/, blocks/, heads/, wheels/<type>.glb), served as is (docs/models.md)
tests/        Vitest, in Node: designs, simulation, damage, collisions, effects, resources, vehicles, steering,
              locomotion, aircraft, combat, tactics, battles, matches, arenas (Valley, Canyon, Island, City, Harbor), community maps, garage, robot
              picker, network, settings, keys and gamepad, the camera by touch, brake and boost, night and lights, melee weapons (energy, biting, mechanical), community maps (format, free spawn lines, map editor, the game's arenas exported, packages), JSON Schemas of the open formats, glTF models (props, blocks, turret heads, wheels), architecture,
              performance, the compiled simulation against the TypeScript (native, nativePhysics, nativeSim; probe.ts:
              the probe to load)
```

- Tests that depend on randomness (AI, gusts) call `seedRandom(n)` (`tests/seed.ts`) to be reproducible.
- The tests play the game's arenas from their files, like the game and the server: `tests/arenaFiles.ts` (the `setupFiles` of vite.config.ts) reads them from `content/arenas/` on demand, so `ARENAS.valley` reads as before; the code that makes an arena is `ARENA_SOURCES` (arena/sources.ts).

### Languages (i18n)

The game speaks French and English; the player chooses in the settings (Profile tab, `Settings.language`, by default the browser's language: `detectLocale(navigator.languages)`, English as a fallback), and the change applies live. Only what the player sees is translated: code comments and messages for developers (console, errors, server logs, names of the profiler steps) stay in French; the documentation is in English.

- **Message catalogs (`client/i18n/locales/<langue>/`):** one file per part of the game (zone), the same in every language: `common` (units, teams, buttons), `catalog` (names of blocks, weapons, melee weapons, wheels, props, damage types), `world` (arenas, map places, game modes, AI modes, kill causes), `bots` (preset robots), `design` (design issues, clearance), `net` (server refusals, credits), `picker` (robot picker), `keys` (actions, keys, gamepad, controls help), `menu` (screens and settings, the music player and the track titles), `hud` (HUD and page), `garage`, `parts` (part info sheets), `maps` (community maps: their screen, `MapIssue`, terrain import errors). Each key starts with the name of its section (`menu.pause.resume`, `block.armorHeavy`) and is in only one file (`tests/i18n.test.ts` checks this). **French is the source language**: the `MessageKey` type is the set of its keys; each file of another language is checked against the French one (`as const satisfies ZoneOf<typeof fr>`: a missing or extra key is flagged right there), then the whole language in locales/index.ts (`complete`: extra keys, parameters that differ from the French ones; the offending keys show up in the TypeScript error). Each message is written as a single string: a concatenation "…" + "…" would lose the literal type, and with it the parameter check (`WidenedKeys`, rejected for French by `source` and for the other languages by `complete`). A section that is exhaustive over the identifiers of a game catalog is written `...section("block", { armor: "Blindage", … } as const satisfies Record<BlockType, Message>)` (locales/section.ts): a block type without a name is an error, like an exhaustive `Record` in the renderer; an optional section (not every action has a detail): `Partial<Record<…>>` and `tMaybe`.
- **`t(clé, paramètres)` (i18n.ts):** TypeScript checks the key and the parameters against the French message (a missing or extra parameter: an error). Parameters `{nom}`; a number passed as is gets written the language's way (at most two decimals, digit grouping above 9,999: a port stays "8787"). **Plurals:** a message is a text or its forms `{ one: "{n} pièce", other: "{n} pièces" }` (and `zero`, `two`, `few`, `many` for the languages that have them), chosen by the `n` parameter according to `Intl.PluralRules` (0 and 1 are singular in French, only 1 in English); a sentence with two counts is made of two messages. A text that agrees with a thing ("les mâchoires entreraient", "the jaws would go in") receives `n` = 2 if that thing is made of several parts (`Zone.plural`). An optional key built on the fly: `tMaybe("actionDetail.boost")`. `translate(langue, clé, …)` for a given language (tests).
- **Numbers (`format.ts`):** `num(x, décimales)` (at most, without useless zeros), `fixed` (exactly: the instruments that change every frame), `pct` ("85 %", "85%"), `signed` ("−1,5", a true minus sign), with `Intl` formats cached per language and per precision; `measure(x, unité, décimales)` (i18n.ts) for a measurement (`unit.<id>` in `common`: the symbol and its position, a non-breaking space: "0,5 m", "1200 tr/min", "1200 rpm", "40 PS", "40 HP"). No more `.replace(".", ",")`. `fold` (search ignoring accents and case) and `compareText` (the language's alphabetical order): the searches in the garage and the robot picker work on the displayed text, in the displayed language.
- **Fallback language:** a language that is being translated may have only some of the keys (`LocaleInfo.messages` is partial): what is missing is displayed in English, then in French; in development, each missing translation or absent parameter is reported once in the console (`report`). The shipped languages are complete (TypeScript, and `tests/i18n.test.ts`).
- **What comes from shared code and the server (`names.ts`):** `src/shared` (which also runs on the server) has no displayed text: the catalogs have only identifiers (a block no longer has a `label`, a preset robot no longer has a `name` or a `trait`, an arena no longer has a `name` or a `description`, a map place has a `PlaceId`), and what shared code tells the player is a code with its parameters, which the client translates: `validateDesign` returns `DesignIssue`s (`designIssueText`), clearance returns `ZonePart`s and `ConflictReason`s (`zonePartName`, `conflictText`, `conflictLine`), `matchReward` returns `RewardLine`s (`rewardLineText`), the server `Refusal`s (`refusalText`; `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 27), map validation `MapIssue`s (`mapIssueText`), and the list of unlocked parts comes as `Part`s. `botName(profile)`: the name a person gave (garage robot, player in multiplayer, `BotProfile.name`, optional) or that of the preset robot in the displayed language (`bot.<id>`); `botTrait`. A player without a name is called "Robot 3" (the server writes no text to translate). Zone letters (A, B, C) are not translated. What a person wrote for a community map (its name, its description, its named places) is displayed as is, or in the displayed language if the author wrote it in that language (`authored`): `arenaName` (an identifier, or the arena itself: a map received from a server is known only to the replica), `arenaInfo`, `placeLabel`.
- **The page (`dom.ts`):** an element of index.html carries the key of its text (`data-i18n`) or of its attributes (`data-i18n-title`, `data-i18n-aria-label`, `data-i18n-placeholder`); a text mixed with a child element (a key `<kbd data-key-action>`) is wrapped in a `<span data-i18n>`; the game's name carries `translate="no"`. `applyDom` at startup (main.ts: the saved language from the very first loading screen) and on every language change, along with the page's `lang`.
- **Live switching:** `setLocale` (state.ts) notifies those who listen (`onLocaleChange`): the page (`applyDom`), the HUD (weapon bar, key help, scoreboard, title, the robots' 3D labels, minimap), the garage. The settings screen redraws itself (`MenuView.id`: the same screen keeps its focus); the other screens are built when they open, in the language of the moment. Lines already shown in the kill feed stay in the old language (they fade out within a few seconds).
- **Translation choices (English):** American English (armor, color, gray), sentence case for labels ("Main menu"), a concise game tone, the informal French "tu" → "you". The names of the preset robots and arenas are translated: they are common nouns that say what the robot or the place is (Scarabée → Scarab, Bélier → Ram, Vallée → Valley, Île volcanique → Volcanic Island), and the code identifiers are already those words; "Casse-Robot" stays the game's name. Lexicon kept consistent from one screen to another: bloc → block; pièce → part; plan → design; blindage → armor; points de structure (PS) → hit points (HP); dégâts → damage; destruction (score, fil) → kill; partie → match; arme de contact → melee weapon; tourelle → turret; viseur → scope; réticule → crosshair; verrouillage → lock-on; toupie → spinner; régime → spin; leurres → flares; IEM → EMP; paralysé → stunned; réacteur → thruster; propulseur → booster; turboréacteur → turbojet; rotor orientable → tilt rotor; hélice → propeller; patin anti-gravité → hover pad; gouverne → control surface; roue directrice → steered wheel; roulette → caster; chenille → track; galet → roller; crédits → credits; débloquer → unlock; se saborder → self-destruct; réapparition → respawn; caméra libre → free camera; tableau → scoreboard; fil des destructions → kill feed; pilote automatique → autopilot; gaz → throttle; palonnier → rudder. Keys that type a character keep the name from the player's keyboard layout (`codeLabel`); the others have their own name in each language ("Espace", "Space").
- **Tests:** `tests/i18n.test.ts` (all languages have all the keys and the same parameters, one key per file, plurals and numbers in both languages, default language and setting, live switching, page keys); `tests/i18nSource.test.ts` rejects French hard-coded in the code and the page (heuristic in `tests/frenchText.ts`: accented letters, « » quotes, function words, common interface words; excluding comments, import paths, console and error messages, shaders); a string that is not displayed text but looks like French is marked `// i18n-ignore` on its line. `tests/keyMessages.test.ts`: no message names a key hard-coded (a `{key}` parameter, filled in from the chosen keys: see "Keys and gamepad").

### Simulation

- **Simulation → rendering contract:** the `Simulation` emits `SimEvent`s (`shared/sim/events.ts`) **synchronously**, at the exact moment the structure changes (`partAdded`, `blockAttached`, `blockDestroyed`, `wheelRemoved`, `debrisAdded`, `shot`, `fireStarted`, `smokeAdded`, `botKilled`…). `WorldView` creates or destroys its meshes only from these events, then `sync()` copies the body poses every frame. The blocks of a part are grouped by appearance (type, color) into batches of **thin instances** (`thinInstanceAdd`) parented to the part's node, each with **its own copy of the geometry** (Babylon stores the thin instances' color buffer on the geometry: batches sharing it would overwrite each other, and on a real GPU some would no longer be drawn): one matrix per batch per frame, and the instances only go back to the GPU when a block changes (removal by swapping with the last one, "dirty" batches flushed in `sync`). What follows a block (turret head, flame, fire) is attached to its frame (`anchor`), created on demand. **Wings and control surfaces** are not in the batches: a part's wings (per type) are a single piece, and each row of control surfaces another one, which pivots as a unit around its hinge (`SurfacePiece`, `buildSurfaces`); their merged cells (`mergeSegments`, render/surfaceModels.ts) each have a profile according to what touches them (`surfaceSides`, shared/robot/surfaces.ts: rounded leading edge, tapered trailing edge, wingtip rounded only at the ends; nothing is drawn between two cells that continue into each other, two wings of the same design or two cells of a row with the same profile; a flat face closes a side against a block, a control surface that moves separately or a cell with another profile, `CLOSED_*`: a hidden face flush with the skin striped it with a dotted line, the depth buffer hesitating between them), and their wear is a vertex color; their vertices are rounded to a hundredth of a millimeter, so that two neighboring cells have exactly the same edge. A change in the robot's structure (`blockAttached`, `blockDestroyed`, `blockRebuilt`) has them rebuilt at the start of the next frame, only those whose key (part, blocks and profiles, hinge, carrier row) has changed; `partRemoved` takes its own with it. A row of control surfaces just behind another (the flap of an aileron two rows deep in chord) is attached to it and only turns by the difference between their deflections. In the garage and in previews, one instance per cell, from the source of its profile (`Assets.surfaceSource`, from `designSurfaceSides`); a lone cell (debris, thumbnail, part to place) has all four sides shaped (`SIDES_ALONE`). A Plane: 7 pieces (before: one batch and 19 control-surface instances); rebuilding all of its pieces costs ~4 ms, checking that none has changed ~0.2 ms, pivoting the control surfaces of three planes ~0.01 ms (Chrome without GPU). Later, the server will send these same events. Any new structural change must emit its event; the `expectEventsInSync` test in `tests/simulation.test.ts` checks this.
- **Solver:** `SOLVER_ITERATIONS` = 4 (Rapier's default value; 8 cost 30% more physics without changing anything in the tests or the battles). The AI's avoidance rays are recast every 50 ms.
- **Step (`Simulation.step`):** detection → AI (or player commands) of each robot, and energy balance → air (lift, gusts) → wheels (`stepWheels`) → physics (`world.step`, which fills the contact force queue, then wheel placement: `placeWheels`) → collisions → melee weapons (`updateMelee`, "contact" stage) → projectiles → engine flame jets (`updateScorch`, "jets" stage) → fires, smoke, repairs → props, debris → match. Each stage is timed when `sim.profile` is active.
- **Arenas (`arena/layout.ts`):** an `ArenaLayout` describes each arena as data (the game's: Depot, Fortress, Trenches, Construction Site, Valley, Canyon, Volcanic Island, City, City by Night, Harbor, `ARENA_IDS`; made by their code, `ARENA_SOURCES`, and played from their files, `ARENAS`: see "The game's arenas, played from their files"): its dimensions (`ArenaBounds`: walls or foot of the mountains `half`, area where the AI roams `roam`, spawn lines `spawnLine`/`spawnSpread`, aircraft circuit `circuit`; the small arenas all have `SMALL`, ±36 m), scenery boxes (`StaticBox`: wall, ramp, pillar, platform, low wall, rock, container, bridge deck `deck` and roadway `road`, both drivable), props (`PropSpot`), capture zones, and optionally a terrain (`terrain`), a water level (drawn only: you drive through it), paths (`paths`, drawn; `surface`, `PathSurface`: dirt, grass (a lawn, the short grass of an airstrip), sand, gravel road that stays visible on rock, gravel lane, ballast, pavement) and a sky (`sky`: color, fog).
