Further options (arena/features.ts, all optional: an arena that doesn't declare them behaves as before; fixed data, nothing in the protocol): `deepWater` (beyond this depth, the water is real: you drown in it, the AI doesn't venture into it, hover pads glide over it, `waterBelow` in robot/supports.ts), `lava` (flows and lakes, `LavaShape`, `lavaAt`), `navGrid` (the AI's nav grid, see below) or `nav` (nav graph, `NavGraph`, arena/navGraph.ts: see "Getting around scenery"), `ground` (ground palette, `GroundStyle`: ground, dirt, sand, rock, summit, basalt around the lava, cliff strata, color patches, tint of slopes (a city's embankments), of lawns, tighter pattern; without it, the Valley's), `waterColor`, `vents` (fumaroles, `Vent`) and `falls` (waterfalls, `Waterfall`), and a box look (`StaticBox.look`, `BoxLook`: sandstone, timber framing, railway track, railcar, planks, basalt, rusted steel; for rendering only). In its code, a large arena can build its terrain, scenery, props and nav graph on first read (getters, like the Canyon: ~0.8 s); the game plays its file instead, read and built in 20 to 50 ms. Spawn lines stay clear. `sim.arena` is the current arena; `sim.loadArena(id)` replaces the static scenery and the terrain (the flat ground stays, underneath), resets the props and emits `arenaChanged` (to be done before placing the robots). Everything that used to read the scenery or the arena size (AI cover and destinations, spawns, aircraft pilot, camera) reads `sim.arena`. The client draws the scenery with `ArenaView.show`.
- **Terrain (`arena/terrain.ts`):** a square of heights at the vertices of a grid (`makeTerrain`, from a pure function; fixed noise `noise2`, without `rand`: it is data), which the physics turns into a Rapier heightfield (arena.ts, `static` group: wheels, projectiles, debris and sight see it as the ground), with `HeightFieldFlags.FIX_INTERNAL_EDGES` (without which a crate rubbing against the terrain snags on the edge between two triangles as on a step). A rock (`StaticBox` of kind `rock`) has as its collider the convex hull of the dented ball you see (`rockShape`, arena/rocks.ts, which the renderer also draws): a box let its corners stick out by more than half a meter, invisible obstacles against which the Dragster at full speed stopped dead. An invisible boundary (`bound`) closes off the Valley at the back of its mountains: not drawn, not seen by the camera, not on the map. `terrainHeight` interpolates like Rapier (each cell cut by the diagonal (ix+1, iz)–(ix, iz+1); checked by `tests/terrain.test.ts`), `rayTerrain` casts a ray on it, without physics (camera, also online). The terrain that the code of a game arena computes is rounded once to the 16-bit precision of the map format (`quantizeTerrain`, map/codec.ts: the encoding then the decoding; heights move by 0.4 mm at most), so that the arena of its file is the same to the bit, terrain included. `groundLevel(arena, x, z)`: ground height; robots spawn on it (`spawnHeight`, wave.ts), flying robots and planes count their altitude above it and above the terrain ahead of them (`flightLevel`, layout.ts; `Instruments.ground`, ai/pilot.ts).
- **The Valley (`arena/valley.ts`):** the large map, ±105 m of playing field within mountains (too steep to climb); a shallow river running diagonally, spanned by a stone bridge (zone C, taken on the deck); to the northeast the village (brick houses with tiled roofs, church and bell tower, zone B), to the east the fort hill (plateau at 9.5 m, concrete rampart, blockhouse, zone A), to the west the factory (warehouses, tanks, containers, chimney, zone E), to the southwest the airfield (runway, open hangar, control tower, zone D), a sawmill and a cabin in two woods, oaks and rocks in the fields. North = +z, west = +x (right-handed frame: a robot facing north has west on its left). The places are roughly symmetric about the center. The terrain is computed: swell, flattened sites (`FLATS`), fort hill (via a maximum: a flattened site at its foot doesn't cut into it as a cliff), riverbed, mountains; slopes of 31° at most in the playing area (the flanks of the fort). The buildings are made by `house` (wall panels, roof in strips resting on their walls, diamond-shaped gables), the trees placed by a fixed draw. A combat step costs as much there as in the small arenas (~3 ms), robots get stuck there just as rarely (~7% of the time, as at the Depot), and matches last longer (2 to 4 min in Battle).
- **The Canyon (`arena/canyon.ts`):** another large map (±105 m, sandstone mountains around it, invisible boundaries at ±140 like the Valley), a red desert: a plateau at 12 m cut from east to west by a winding canyon (centerline `canyonZ`, dry bed at 2.6 m, `floorHalf`: 19 m wide, a basin to the east, a gorge to the west; sheer walls 4 m wide). You can only get down into it by trails (`Trail`: a smooth curve through its points, `smoothed`, Catmull-Rom: at the bend of a polyline, the trail's height, that of the nearest segment, jumped from one segment to the next along its inner edge, a step of several decimeters where a robot cutting the bend got stuck; its height along it, `graded`: a constant slope eased to flat over `EASE` m at each end, with no crease where a low robot would catch its nose; cut into the slope on one side, built up on fill on the other): four in the walls, in an X on either side of the viaduct (one goes down where the other goes up: they don't narrow the bed by facing each other), and the two ravines of the oasis. You cross it by the railway's trestle viaduct (zone C, deck `rail`; in the bed, each bent is a timber post under the deck's axis and its cap under the two stringers: with two legs 4.6 m apart, too close for a robot to pass between and too far apart for the navigation to treat them as one obstacle, a robot coming along the bed aimed at a corner of one while brushing the other, and hit it; the Buggy lost a wheel there), or by the natural arch (zone A) that joins the two halves of the Mesa, an elliptical mesa at 24 m that you climb by a ledge trail on each side (8 m wide, `LEDGE_WIDTH`: cut into a cliff, it is flat only over its width less a meter or two, the terrain being sampled every meter; at 6.5 m, robots 3.5 to 4.4 m wide drove with a wheel or a track on its edge). The railway, leveled, goes through two buttes in tunnels (a cut in the terrain, a sandstone roof `platform` 5 m above the rails); to the northeast the wooden Wild West town (saloon with a false front, general store, sheriff's office, church and bell tower, station and its platform, water tower: tank `watertank` on stilts; zone B), to the southwest the mine (headframe, winch house, crusher shed, bunkhouse, spoil heaps, mine carts, tank; zone D), to the east the oasis at the bottom of the canyon (pond, oaks, adobe ruin; zone E), cacti (`cactus`), rocks and hoodoos. North = +z, west = +x; the map is symmetric about the center (town and mine, buttes, trails; the oasis and the Mesa face each other). Terrain at 1 m per vertex (at 1.375 m, Rapier rays falling exactly on certain lines of vertices went through the heightfield: a step that is a power of two), desert tints and cliff strata (`ground`), ambience: the Fortress wind (no desert ambience recorded yet). Measurements (`BATTLE_ARENA=canyon npm run battle`): see the roadmap.
- **The Volcanic Island (`arena/island.ts`):** an island of ±116 m surrounded by the sea (`deepWater` 1.1 m: beyond that, you drown; invisible boundaries at ±172 m, far enough out for planes), dominated at the center by a volcano (crater rim at 31 m, radius 15; floor at 26 m; foot at 52 m; gullied flanks too steep to climb). Two switchback roads (`spiralRoad`: 9 m wide, 250°, an even grade based on the distance traveled, a radial end in line with a breach in the rim; embankments of `BANK` m) climb from the north and the south to the crater, where a lava lake boils (zone A, on the other side of the line between the breaches). Two flows (`LAVA`: northeast and southwest) run down from fissures at the foot of the volcano to the sea, where they smoke (steam); each is cut by a ford of solidified basalt about a dozen meters wide (`FORD_WEST`). Two gorges (floor under deep water, steep walls, a spring cascading at their head), to the northwest and southeast, are crossed on wooden bridges (decks along the z axis, connected to the terrain by their abutments). The land rises gently toward the volcano (`RISE`). Places, symmetric about the center: the fishing village (zone B, northwest; plank huts, tile or sheet-metal roofs, three huts on stilts in the shallow lagoon, connected by pontoons) and the bunkers (zone C, southeast; concrete casemates, low walls, coastal gun), the grass airstrip (zone D, southwest; open hangar, shelter, tank, windsock) and the wreck beach (zone E, northeast; a rusty freighter lying on its side, its containers, straw huts); the spawn lines are on the northern and southern beaches. The jungle: 124 palms and kapok trees (`palm`, `kapok`: a trunk that topples and burns, a crown of fronds or an umbrella canopy with buttresses), in symmetric groves and along the coast, away from paths, roads, gorges, lava and places. The terrain is computed in ~0.3 s at load time (the polylines are stored in bundles of known footprint, `Polyline`, and the flattened sites are ruled out by their footprint). Measured (`ISLAND_REPORT=1 npx vitest run tests/island.test.ts --silent=false`, and a probe on the same loaded machine): a combat step costs as much as in the Valley (8.0 vs. 8.1 ms under load, driving 1.6 vs. 1.3 ms), robots get stuck there just as much (16 vs. 16% of samples stopped while pushing, in the same match), a Battle lasts 5 to 8 minutes, a Zone Capture under 2 minutes.
- **The City (`arena/city.ts`):** a city in ruins, ±94 m of streets (`urban`), enclosed by a continuous row of gutted apartment buildings (`StaticKind` `tower`: indestructible blocks, with windows) and rubble embankments, with more towers in the haze and invisible boundaries. A large square and its monument in the center (zone C), a central avenue and boulevards (spawn lines) 160 to 180 m long; to the east an elevated expressway (deck `deck` on piers, `underpass`: you can go under it; terrain embankments at both ends, taken from their foot, `approach`; concrete abutments under its ends; zone E on the deck); to the west the station (tracks `track` at the bottom of a 3 m cutting with gentle banks, ramps down at both ends, a platform domed into the terrain under a canopy, zone A); to the northeast the park (mound, bandstand, zone B), to the southwest a ruined tower of ~32 m and a multistory parking garage (platform and two tall ramps, zone D). The map is symmetric about the center (the expressway mirrors the station). The buildings (`building`) are facade panels (`facade`, `tenement`: a 3.6 m story, windows), each resting on the one below, with a flat roof in concrete strips; some have lost their top, two city blocks are piles of rubble. Cars (`car`: dynamic; worn down, it burns for its 5 s fuse then explodes, look `car`), anchored wrecks (`wreck`), lampposts that topple (`lamppost`), bus shelters, kiosks, barricades. Surfaces (`TerrainPath.surface`: lawns, walkways, ballast, pavements) and a city palette (`ArenaLayout.ground`: flagstones and cobbles in a tight pattern, grime, earth banks on slopes, rubble above 10.5 m, lawns), gray sky and dust in suspension (`ArenaSky.dust`, `dustFx`). The AI's planes fly above the tower (`airFloor` 34), its flying robots above the rooftops (`hoverFloor` 14). The ambience is the Trenches' (wind, distant cannon fire), for lack of its own. About 535 props; a combat step costs as much there as in the Valley.
- **The Harbor (`arena/port.ts`):** a large map of quays (±100 m) by the sea, to the east; low hills to the north, south and west, invisible boundaries at ±200 m (at ±135, planes drifting away on patrol crashed into them). Symmetric about the east-west axis (z → -z). In the middle, the basin where a freighter is moored (zone C on its deck, at 7.2 m, level with the top of its gangways: a platform in the water, reached by two gangways 7 m wide at 7.6°, one on each side; containers at the bow, hatch cover, superstructure and funnel at the stern), a channel spanned by a swing bridge (a deck between two lattice girders), a dock basin at the end (a barge, two tower cranes). On each side: two container gantry cranes (front legs against the parapet), a container terminal (stacks of one to three, aisles of 9 to 10 m, zone B or D in the middle, one stack you can climb by a ramp), two warehouses you can drive through (`HouseSpec.through`: the opening is repeated in the back panel), an oil depot (zone A or E between two rows of tightly packed tanks, which blow up in a chain), the railway (railcars on one track, well spaced); to the west the harbor office, three silos (round boxes, `StaticBox.round`: a cylinder for physics and rendering) and their gallery; to the east the fishermen's low quay (1.5 m lower, behind a retaining wall with a parapet, two ramps) and the lighthouse jetties. Quays at 6 m, water at 3 m, bottom at 0.2 m (`deepWater` 1.5: you drown in the basin, the dock basin, the sea, sim/hazards.ts): the water's edges fall on terrain vertices and a quay wall (`road`, look `quay`) hides the terrain slope; parapets (low walls for the AI) line the water, except at the foot of the gangways and at the root of the jetties. Roads as asphalt paths, rails (`track`) flush with the quay. The AI uses the nav grid (`navGrid`, 2 m cells) and goes around warehouses and containers as in a city (`urban`). Aircraft floor altitude: 28 m (the gantry booms pass 25 m above the water). Ambience: the Depot's, until it gets its own.
- **AI nav grid (`arena/navgrid.ts`, `ArenaLayout.navGrid`):** the arena in cells (2.5 m on the island), passable or not according to the slope (at the center of the cell and halfway to its neighbors: a cell at the foot of a wall is closed), lava (with a margin) and deep water; a deck connected to the ground at both ends (a bridge, `bridgeDecks`) is passable, even above a gorge, if it covers the whole cell; a raised deck (the floor of a hut on stilts) closes what is under it. What can be walked on whatever the terrain beneath (`walkways`) is open: a ramp (over the whole cell), and, above deep water, a roadway that plunges into it (a quay, a jetty) or a platform that rises from it (the deck of the Harbor's freighter, with its gangways). Only the largest connected region stays open (`keepLargestRegion`): every open cell is reachable. Computed once per arena, on first request. `navigate` (ai/navigation.ts) goes through it first (`gridWaypoint`): a closed destination becomes the nearest open cell; if the straight line (`clearLine`) crosses a closed cell, the robot follows a path (weighted A*, `GREED` 1.4, heap in typed arrays: 0.2 to 2 ms) and aims at the farthest of the next cells on it that it can see; one search per step in total (`SEARCHES_PER_STEP`), no more than one per second per robot (`MIN_REPLAN`), a new one when the destination has moved by 4 cells, when it has strayed from the path, or after 4 s. In combat (`engage`, `support`, `charge`), a path longer than 2.5 times the straight line plus 25 m isn't worth the detour: it just goes as close as it can among the neighboring cells (`approachPath`); otherwise a robot at the foot of the volcano went all the way around the mountain to reach an enemy on the other switchback of the road. A bridge that the route crosses is taken from one end and followed along its axis (`bridgeGoal`: near it, a point on the axis `PURSUIT` m further on; only in an arena with a grid, the Valley keeps its behavior). Also: random destinations (`wander`), those brought back into the arena (`clampToRoam`), landing destinations (`landing`) and spawn spots (`isClear`, wave.ts) are taken from open cells (`passable`, `nearestPassable`: true everywhere without a grid). Local avoidance (brain.ts) sees lava and deep water as an obstacle 1.5 m from their edge (`hazardDistance`, not under a bridge).
- **Terrain hazards (`sim/hazards.ts`, `updateHazards`, in the "fire, smoke, repair" stage, in 0.25 s slices):** lava burns blocks and wheels less than `LAVA_HEIGHT` (1.3 m) above it (`LAVA_DPS` 40, damage type `fire`), sets them alight, sets wood on fire and blows up a barrel; a robot whose cockpit plunges into deep water drowns (`DROWN_DPS` 160 to the cockpit, damage type `energy`: 5 s), and the water puts out the burning blocks it covers. Kill causes `lava` and `drown` (`KillCause`, "lava", "drowning" in the feed). An arena without lava or deep water has none of this.
- **Entities** (`shared/sim/entities.ts`): `Bot` → `Part` (a `RigidBody`) → `Block`. `bot.blocks` only contains living blocks. `sim.parts` lists all the parts in the world (including those of robots outside `sim.bots`).
- **Merged colliders (`robot/colliders.ts`):** a part doesn't have one collider per block: `rebuildColliders` merges neighboring cubes of the same type into large boxes (greedy merging), plates and shaped blocks keep their own (one convex hull per piece of the shape, `addShape`), and the density gives exactly the nominal mass. `attachBlock`/`detachBlock` only mark the part (`dirty`); the colliders are rebuilt at spawn and at the end of each damage batch (`rebuildDirtyParts`), then the mass is recomputed immediately (otherwise Rapier only removes the old one at the next step). `hitTargetOf` finds the block hit from the grid cell at the impact point. Rebuilt colliders are only visible to raycasts at the next step: this is why piercing goes through a robot by advancing through its grid (`pierceRobot`). **Hittable turret heads (`addHeads`):** a weapon's head and barrel stick out of its block, whose collisions are only the cube; each turret therefore has its hittable zone (`Part.heads`, `TurretHitbox`): a box for the head (`TurretSpec.head`, at `HITBOX_SHARE` of the drawn head) and a capsule for the barrel (`TurretSpec.caliber`, at least `MIN_CALIBER`, up to its muzzle; not for the bomb bay), massless (zero density) and without contact (`hitbox` group: a barrel grazing the ground doesn't snag on it). Shots and rays without a group filter see them (not the wheel rays); a hit on them strikes the turret's block (owner `head`, `hitTargetOf`, `ownerBot`). They follow the head's yaw and pitch, as the renderer draws it (`syncHeads`, every step after the AI, only when it has turned), and are rebuilt with the part's colliders. Without them, a shot at the weapon went through it and only hit the block below.
- **Collision groups (`physics/groups.ts`):** debris only collides with the static scenery; a mounted wheel only collides with what moves (robots, props: `wheel`), and its suspension ray only sees the static scenery (`groundRay`); a turret's hittable zone collides with nothing (`hitbox`). Every new collider must be given its group. Each entity has a unique `id` within its simulation, which serves as a key on the rendering side and later on the network side.
- **`sim.colliders`** (handle → `ColliderOwner`) links a ray impact to what the collider represents. It must be kept up to date on every collider creation or removal.

- **`World` (`sim/world.ts`):** what the renderer, the sound and the HUD read (robots, time, player, match, detection, projectiles and mines, events). The `Simulation` implements it, and so does the network replica: the views are typed `World`, never `Simulation`.

### Multiplayer (`shared/net/`, `server/`)

- **Room (`server/room.ts`):** an endless six-versus-six battle; a player who joins (`join`: name, preset robot or garage design, validated by the server) replaces an AI robot on the team with the fewest humans; they pilot through `Bot.input` (their commands, clamped on receipt, `readInput`: the look direction `look` is normalized or dropped; `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 27); on leaving, they hand their robot back to the AI; a new match starts 8 s after the end, in the next arena (`NetEvent` `arena`, before the props and the match; `zone` when a zone changes hands; the zones' state is in the snapshot). The room receives text and sends through a `Connection`: the tests run it without a network (`tests/network.test.ts`). Its arenas follow one another (`Room.rotation`): the game's (those whose files loaded, `RoomOptions.arenas`: `content/arenas/` and `ARENAS` in server/main.ts), then the community maps it is given (`RoomOptions.maps`, the `maps/` folder in server/main.ts: map files, and map packages as archives or folders). A community map is sent only once to whoever doesn't have it (`map` message, before the frame that puts it in place: on joining, and on each arena change); on joining, the client announces those it already has (`join.maps`, their identifiers, at most `KNOWN_MAPS`). **A package's resources** (prop models, ambience: see "Map packages") don't go through the WebSocket: the `map` message carries their SHA-256 and size (`MapFile.assets`, which the map's identifier covers: `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 27, a client that ignored them would not find the map the server names), and the game server serves them over HTTP by their SHA-256 (`MAP_ASSET_PATH`, `/map-assets/<sha256>`: `mapAssetHash`, server/staticFiles.ts; only the resources of the maps it plays, kept in memory; immutable caching, `access-control-allow-origin: *` for a page served by Vite on another port, a model gzipped once). Over HTTP rather than in the socket: a few hundred KB don't hold the match's frames back behind them, the browser downloads them in parallel and caches them (the address never changes), and the client doesn't need them to play (the game's model, a game arena's ambience meanwhile). The client downloads them from the origin of its socket (`httpOrigin`: `ws://host:8787` → `http://host:8787`, `wss` → `https`) as soon as it receives the map, or when its replica finds a map it already has (`resolveArena`, in case a resource was missing), bounded to the announced size and checked before use (`MapAssetStore`, client/maps/assets.ts).
- **Progression on the server (`server/progressBook.ts`):** each browser draws a secret token once (`playerToken`, client/progress.ts) and sends it when joining; the server keeps the credits and unlocked parts per token (`Ledger`), in memory (`ProgressBook`, with no disk, for the tests) and in a file (server/main.ts: read at startup, rewritten one second after each change, via a temporary file). A garage robot with parts that are not unlocked on this server is only accepted if the player has enough credits there: the parts are then unlocked (`unlockParts`, all or nothing), otherwise it is refused with the list and the price. Preset robots are always accepted. At the end of a match, each player earns their credits (`matchReward`: match played, victory, their kills, counted from `Bot.input`) and receives the `progress` message; the client turns it into a line in the feed. Local progression (matches against the AI) stays in the browser, separately.
- **Frames:** every `SNAPSHOT_EVERY` steps, a JSON header (`FrameHeader`: the `NetEvent`s since the previous frame, the player's robot and its current lock-on) followed by a binary snapshot (`snapshot.ts`: match, detection, state of the robots and their weapons (rate of fire, heat, ammo, reload of the current round; one deflection per row of control surfaces), part poses, wheels and hub carriers, debris, moving props, projectiles). An anchored prop isn't in it: its `NetEvent` `prop` gives its spot (`spot`, in `arena.props`, from which the replica gets its pose, `spotPose`, and its dimensions); once released (`propFree`), it enters the snapshots. A player who arrives first receives everyone (`describeWorld`), including wood that is burning or has burned (`propFire`: burning or not, its charred share) and the ongoing fire puddles (`groundFire`, `groundFireGone`); the replica knows about a prop's fire only from these announcements (`Prop.burn` is 1 while it burns). `World` also gives the props present (`props`) and the puddles (`groundFires`): an effect attached mid-match finds there what is already burning.
- **Encoder (`net/encoder.ts`):** translates `SimEvent`s into `NetEvent`s (entities named by their `id`). A robot that has appeared is described in full at send time (`BotDesc`: living blocks and their part, hit points, wheels, broken links); its structure is deduced from its design. **Any new structural change must have its `NetEvent`**, just as it has its `SimEvent`.
- **Replica (`net/replica.ts`):** rebuilds the same entities (with `newBot`, `newBlock`, `registerOrgans`, `addThrusters` from lifecycle.ts), with bodies without physics (`PoseBody`: pose, velocities, center of mass, mass) and the wheel poses (`WheelBody`); a mounted wheel has a hub carrier (`hub`), as in the simulation (this is how queries recognize a driven wheel). It replays the events when their frame is reached and emits the same `SimEvent`s. The arena of a `NetEvent` `arena`: a game arena, a received map (`addArena`), or a map the client already has (`resolveArena`, the browser's library); if unknown, it keeps its own and notes it (`missingArena`). `tests/network.test.ts` checks that it reproduces the server's world and that what the HUD reads works there.
- **Client:** `ArenaGame.startOnline(net)` binds the views to the replica (`bind`); the loop then replaces the physics with `replica.advance` and sends the commands (`INPUT_RATE` per second). A frame that puts in place a large game arena whose file this browser hasn't downloaded yet is held back, with everything that follows it, until the file is there (`NetClient.hold`; see "The game's arenas, played from their files"). Online, aiming goes through the Babylon scene (`WorldView.belongsTo` to aim through your own robot). An exception in a frame is reported once without stopping Babylon's loop (it wouldn't restart).
- **Solo in a Worker (`LocalSession`, net/session.ts; client/sim/simWorker.ts, workerLink.ts):** a solo game runs its simulation off the rendering thread, behind the same contract as the game server: the Worker receives commands (`SessionCommand`: a game in a mode and an arena, with the player's robot or imposed robots, and the arena's file given along: a community map, or a large game arena, which the Worker doesn't have in its bundle (`WorkerLink` sends each file once); the player's commands; the time that passes, which the game drives each frame, slow motion and pause included; a free-camera shot, the session looking for a homing target itself; an extra wave; freezing the AI; self-destruct; a console impact; measuring each step) and sends back frames like the server's (`encodeFrame`: events and a binary snapshot, as transferred `ArrayBuffer`s), which the game replays in a replica (`WorkerLink`), like a network game but with a short interpolation delay (`LOCAL_DELAY`, 2.5 steps instead of `INTERP_DELAY`). Solo frames carry a little more in their header: `reset` (a new world: the replica forgets the old one and shows it at once, even frozen behind the loading screen; `WorkerLink.arrived` and `ArenaGame.worldReady`, which `App.play` waits for), `arms` (the free camera's weapons, for its HUD), `profile` (the step times, for `npm run perf`). In the game, `net` is the replica's feed (`NetClient` or `WorkerLink`), `online` only a real network game, `inWorker` the solo game in the Worker; leaving a network game goes back to it. If the Worker cannot start or fails, the game goes back to the simulation on the page (`sim`, with its pose interpolation); `?sim=page` in the address forces it. The same session runs in Node for the tests (`tests/session.test.ts`: the replica reproduces the session's world, the free camera, the wave, the pause, the measurement, hover pads whose contact goes from the air to the ground). Measured (`npm run perf`, Depot reference battle, 1920 × 1080, Medium, GTX 1660 Ti Max-Q): 40.6 → 79 frames per second, CPU per frame 23.7 → 11.8 ms, 5% of frames over 16.7 ms; the step itself (~2.9 ms) runs in the Worker, the replica costs ~0.5 ms per frame on the page; ~7 KB per frame. The Worker's chunk embeds Rapier again (~4.7 MB, the page still has its own simulation).
- Removing a robot (`removeBot`) marks its blocks as destroyed: turrets, missiles or an AI that were targeting it don't read its freed bodies.

### Community maps (`shared/map/`, `client/maps/`, `maps/`)

The authors' guide, field by field, with importing from Gaea, World Machine, Blender, terrain.party and Unity: `docs/maps.md`.

- **Format (`MapFile`, map/format.ts):** versioned JSON data (`format: 1`, `.casse-map.json` files), in meters and degrees, in the game's frame. `meta` (name, author, description, license: text written by a person, displayed as is, with an optional name and description per language), `terrain` (a centered square grid: `cells` from 16 to 440, `step` 1, 1.25 or 2 m, 440 m per side at most, little-endian Uint16 heights from `min` to `max` in base64, in the order of Rapier's heightfield), `bounds` (only `half` required; the rest as a percentage of `half`, `airFloor` from the highest point of the scenery), `limits` (enclosure walls that follow the terrain, invisible boundary at `half` + 35 m by default), `sky`, `ground` (`preset`: the palette of a game arena, the Valley by default, `VALLEY_GROUND` in features.ts, and what you change in it), `water` (`level`, `deep`: you drown beyond it, `color`), `lava`, `vents`, `falls` (`dir` in degrees), `paths`, `urban`, `ambience` (that of a game arena: `ArenaLayout.ambience`), `nav` (nav grid: by default on a map with terrain, deep water or lava, 2.5 m cells, 26°; `false`: none), `navGraph` (a nav graph, `NavGraph`: points and links taken as given; a map that has one has no default grid), `boxes` (`StaticBox`: `size` up to 440 m, `yaw`/`pitch`/`roll`, placed on the ground when without `y`, a rock half buried), `props` (`PropSpot`: on the ground when without `y`; `size` for a slab, `scale`, `rests` as indices into `props`, `group` for a building, `diamond`, `centered`: `y` is its center), `prefabs`, `zones` (one to five, A to E), `places` (`ArenaPlace`: a translated place of the game's arenas, `place`, or a named one, `name`), `assets` (`MapAssets`: the SHA-256 and size of a map package's prop models and ambience, written by whoever reads the package; see "Map packages"). **Orientation (`turnQuat`, map/build.ts):** each angle given (even zero) is composed in the order yaw, pitch, roll, a single one exactly as `axisAngle` gives it; `quat` (`MapQuat`, a unit quaternion within `QUAT_SLACK`) is an exact orientation that the angles turn further. Unknown fields are ignored.
- **Reading (`readMap`, `readMapText`, map/read.ts):** untrusted input, copied field by field (types, finite and bounded numbers, bounded texts without control characters, lists bounded before being traversed, language codes and colors checked, booby-trapped keys refused); nothing in it throws an exception or does excessive work (a file of 4 MB at most, a million props refused without being read). Returns a clean `MapFile` (absent optional fields not written) or `MapIssue`s (24 at most; `path`: the faulty field, "props[3].type"). Migration: `upgradeMap` applies `MAP_MIGRATIONS[n]` (from n to n + 1) up to `MAP_FORMAT`; a map in a newer format is refused (`formatTooNew`).
- **Arena (`arenaFromMap`, map/build.ts):** the decoded terrain (`decodeTerrain`, codec.ts), the boxes and props placed (at the lowest point of the ground under their footprint, `lowestUnder`; a movable prop slightly above), the map's props first (their `rests` keep their indices), then the unfolded prefabs (`expandPrefab`, map/prefabs.ts, stopped beyond the allowed number of props), then the limits. **Prefabs:** `house` (the Valley's `house`, built at the origin then rotated by `yaw` and placed: its panels keep their supports and form a building), `tenement` (the City's `building`, which takes a `ground`), `fence` (panels of 2.5 m at most along a polyline), `woods` and `rocks` (sown by a fixed draw, `seeded`, away from paths, zones, spawn lines, water, lava and scenery; `Taken`: an 8 m grid), `ramp` (`incline`), `bridge` (deck, parapets, piers down to the ground), `containers`. After that, nothing distinguishes the map from a game arena: simulation, nav grid (computed on first request), rendering, minimap, network.
- **JSON Schemas (`schemas/`, `tools/schemas/`):** `schemas/map.schema.json` describes a map file, faithful to `readMap` (types, required fields, bounds, text lengths, list sizes, enums, colors and language codes; and two rules of the validation: one to five zones, a slab's `size`), and `schemas/robot.schema.json` a `RobotDesign` (block and wheel types, a facing as two perpendicular axes, the faces a wheel mounts on, track stacking, exactly one cockpit). `npm run schemas` writes them from the code: the catalogs (`PROP_TYPES`, `ARENA_IDS`, `BLOCK_TYPES`, `WHEEL_TYPES`, `AXES`) and the reader's own tables, which read.ts uses too (`MAP_RANGES`: the bounds of each number; `MAP_LISTS`, `MAP_CHOICES`, `MAP_PATTERNS`, `ZONE_LABELS`, `MAX_ANGLE`; `needsSize`, validate.ts). A map can reference the schema (`$schema`, for its author's editor): the reader ignores it, so the map's identifier doesn't change. What a schema can't say stays in the code (and in each schema's description): the terrain's data, bounds relative to another field (min < max, a waterfall's bottom below its top, a prop's `rests` below the number of props, a [min, max] pair), consistent dimensions, distinct and reachable zones, spawn lines, the counts once the prefabs are unfolded; for a robot, one block per cell, connected blocks, wheel supports, overlapping wheels, the budget. Texts are measured once trimmed by the reader, in code points by a schema. `tests/schemas.test.ts` checks them with a small validator (`tests/jsonSchema.ts`: only the keywords the schemas use; `unsupportedKeywords` keeps them to that subset).
- **Identifier (`mapKey`, map/key.ts):** `map-` and 16 hexadecimal digits, the hash (64 bits, in the manner of cyrb53) of the map written in a canonical form (`canonicalJson`: sorted keys); the same map has the same identifier everywhere, the slightest modification makes it a different one. A map from a package carries the SHA-256 of its resources (`assets`): the identifier covers them; a map without resources has no `assets` field, and keeps the identifier it had before packages. `ArenaLayout.id` is an `ArenaKey` (`ArenaId` or `MapKey`).
- **Validation (`checkMap`, `checkMapText`, `validateMap`, map/validate.ts):** the same in the browser, on the server and in the tool: reading, then what the built arena says: counts after the prefabs (`MAP_LIMITS`: 400 boxes, 900 props including 120 movable; the City has 152 and 530, the Harbor 78 movable; and, so that the worst case is checked in 2 s at most, 1,200 sown trees and rocks, 1,024 points for all paths, 512 for all lava, nav grid cells of 2 m at least), slabs without dimensions, terrain covering the playing area and the invisible boundary, consistent dimensions, nothing outside the map, zones (one to five, different letters, within the playing area, **reachable**: an open nav grid cell in their disc, or without a grid a point of their disc outside the scenery boxes: the City's zone C surrounds its monument), **spawn lines** (at least half of their spots free and passable, nothing dangerous within 8 m: lava, a prop that explodes). Returns `{ map, arena, key, issues }`: the checked arena is the one to play (its nav grid is already computed).
- **Imported terrain (map/heightmap.ts, png.ts):** `decodePng` (8- or 16-bit grayscale PNG, color or palette reduced to their average, row filters, no interlacing, 4,097 pixels per side at most; the decompression is supplied: `DecompressionStream("deflate")` in the browser, `zlib` in Node), `decodeRaw16` (square, little- or big-endian), `imageFromBytes` (an image passed through the canvas, 8 bits), `smoothImage`, `heightsFromImage` and `terrainFromImage` (bilinear resampling at the map's step, vertical scale `low`/`high`, smoothing of 8-bit images by default, mountains at the edge: `mountains`), `imageFromHeights` and `encodePng` (exporting a terrain). The image reads like the map: top to the north, left to the west. Errors have a code (`isHeightmapError`, `heightmap.<code>`).
- **Loading:** `registerArena` (arena/layout.ts) makes a map known to this process (`arenaById`, `communityArenas`); `registerMap(carte)` (map/load.ts) builds it once and makes it known; `Simulation.loadArena` takes a known identifier or an arena. In the browser, `MapLibrary` (client/maps/library.ts) holds the maps shipped with the game (`import.meta.glob` of `maps/`, loaded during the startup screen), those imported or made here (IndexedDB, `library` store: client/maps/db.ts; a map can weigh several hundred KB) and those received from a server (`cache` store), rechecked when loading them; `arena(key)` makes one playable; a package's resources are in the `assets` store (see "Map packages"). Menu: the arena choice lists the community maps after the game's arenas, and "Community maps…" imports (a checked file or package, its issues displayed: `importFile`), exports (`downloadMap`; a map with resources, its package: `downloadPackage`) or deletes. **Editor API (phase 2):** `blankMap`, `checkMap`, `arenaFromMap`, `mapKey`, terrain import (`heightImageFromFile`, client/maps/files.ts, then `terrainFromImage`), `MapLibrary.save`/`remove`/`list`/`importText`, `downloadMap`, `downloadHeightPng`, and `App.testMap(carte, { mode, pilot, back })` (a test match; returns the map's issues); in the console, `__casse.maps` and `__casse.testMap(carte)`.
- **Free spawn lines (`MapFile.spawns`):** optional (`format: 1` stays): for each team, a midpoint, the direction it faces at the start (`facing`, degrees: 0 toward the north, 90 toward the west) and a length (`width`, 4 to 160 m); both teams or neither. Carried by `ArenaBounds.spawns` (`SpawnLine`: radians and half-length, `mapBounds`); `spawnLineOf`, `spawnPoint`, `spawnLocal` (arena/layout.ts) give a team's line, a point on the line, and where a point is relative to it: without free lines, exactly the calculations from before (x along the line, z = ±`spawnLine`), so the game's arenas see no difference. They are taken into account by: `spawnTeams` and `respawnBot` (`placeInLine`, turned toward `facing`), planes (the half of their circuit that faces their line, `sideAngle`), sown woods and rocks (`sowable`), validation (each line within the playing area, 2 m from the edge, `spawnOutside`; clear and safe, like the north and south ones; the two at least `SPAWN_APART` (24 m) from each other, `spawnClose`). `tests/spawnLines.test.ts`. A map that has them doesn't play the same for an older client: `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 24.
- **Network:** see "Multiplayer" (`map` message, `join.maps`, `Replica.addArena`, `resolveArena`, a package's resources over HTTP); `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 22 (23 since the pilot controls, 24 since the free spawn lines, 25 since night: the City by Night, `sky.night`, headlights, beacons and the floodlight mast; 26 since the decorative lights; 27 since map packages, `MapFile.assets`).
- **The map editor (client/editor/):** a screen of its own, like the garage: its own scene on the game's engine (`EditorView`, created on entering, entirely freed on leaving; the arena on standby behind it). You enter it from the main menu (the current draft, otherwise a blank map) and from "Community maps…" ("New map", "Edit"). **State and commands (`MapEditor`, editor.ts):** the map (a `MapFile`), its history (`MapHistory`, shared/map/history.ts: the successive maps, with what doesn't change shared; at most 100 modifications and 48 MB of terrains; a brush stroke counts as one; undo and redo restore the selection each state had, `selections`), the tool, the selection (`ElementRef`s: a list of the map and an index; `spawns`: 0 the blues, 1 the reds), the tool settings; each modification goes through the pure operations of shared/map/edit.ts (`addElement`, `setElement`, `moveElements`, `rotateElements`, `duplicateElements`, `removeElements`, `movePoint`: the props' `rests` supports and `group` buildings follow along; `spawnsOf` gives the spawn lines, the north and south ones until they've been placed) then `commit`. **Drawing (`EditorView`):** the game's renderer (`ArenaView` in editable drawing: `showBoxes`, `showGround`, `reshapeTerrain`; props as instances of their models, `Assets.propSource`) from `arenaFromMap(carte, "map-editor", origins)` (`ArenaOrigins`: where each box and each prop comes from, so that a click on a wall panel selects its house); only what has changed is redone (`changesBetween`: the ground for terrain, water, paths, lava, palette, waterfalls; boxes and props for what places them or, if there are sown woods and rocks, for what they avoid); the decoded terrain is kept per map terrain (`decodeTerrain`, a `WeakMap`). Overlay draped on the ground (draping.ts: ribbons and discs, `alphaIndex` 2: after water, before smoke): playing area, AI area, invisible boundary, spawn lines and their handles (move; rotate, the ball at the tip of the arrow), zones, places, fumaroles, lava, paths, placed limits, the selection (a box, the handles of a trace's points, a wood's disc), the placement ghost (green or red: within the map, within the bounds of `MAP_LIMITS`), the brush ring, the trace in progress, the snapping grid (around the center of the view); projected HTML labels (zones, places, spawn lines, problems: a click leads there). Orbit camera without Babylon's controls (`orbit`, `pan`, `zoom`, `shift` on the keyboard, `toggleTop`: north up, `focus`), its center moved without changing the angle (`setTarget(…, true)`). **During a drag, only the element's meshes move** (`dragBy`); on release, the map is rebuilt. During a brush stroke, the field (shared/map/brush.ts: `applyBrush`, pure and tested) is reshaped live (`reshapeTerrain`, over the affected area: positions, normals by central differences, colors), and the terrain is re-encoded on release. **Panels (`EditorUi`):** top bar (name, "modified", where the map comes from, the "The map is playable" indicator or the number of problems, Test, Save, Leave), toolbar (`TOOLS`, editor/tools.ts: Select, Terrain, Place, Traces, Water and lava, Settings, File), tool panel, selection properties (built from `FORMS`, shared/map/fields.ts: each field of each kind of element with its bounds, those of reading; an optional field has its "auto"), list of problems, key help (`editorKeys`, keys.ts), notes. **Checking:** `checkMap` in a Worker (`MapChecker`, checker.ts; 350 ms after the last modification, only one at a time), on the page if there is no Worker; `issueTarget` (edit.ts) says where a problem is. **Testing:** `App.chooseTestMode` (the mode, the last one first; then the robot), `editor.suspend()` (scene, panels, listeners and Worker freed, state kept), `App.testMap`; the pause and end-of-match screens offer "Back to the editor" (`returnToEditor`, `editor.resume()`: in the same state, view included). A map that has problems doesn't start (the list opens). **Saving:** the map must be playable; `MapLibrary.replace` replaces its previous version (a map from this browser); a map shipped with the game or received from a server is saved as a copy ("(copy)" added to the name). **Draft:** kept in IndexedDB (`drafts` store, db.ts version 2; drafts.ts) 1.2 s after each modification, with where the map comes from and the view; the editor reopens it. Escape cancels what is in progress (a field, a drag, a trace, an import, the selection), then offers to leave (`App.leaveEditor`: save, keep the draft, discard it, go back). **Keys:** the "Map editor" section of the action table (`editor*`, context `editor`: never at the same time as the match or the garage, no gamepad, key combinations). Measured in Chrome without GPU on a loaded machine: opening Lake Vale ~200 to 400 ms (everything is built), moving a house or changing a field ~80 to 130 ms (the whole arena rebuilt, the ground kept: `arenaFromMap` ~12 ms, boxes ~23, props ~13, overlay ~18; redoing the ground would cost ~120 more), the end of a brush stroke ~70 to 110 ms (the ground, already reshaped live, isn't redone, unless there is lava), checking ~70 ms in the Worker; after six editor → menu round trips, two live scenes (the game, the garage) and no Worker (`Runtime.queryObjects`), the heap stable (~105 MB).
- **The example map, "Lake Vale" ("Val du Lac" in French, `maps/val-du-lac.casse-map.json`):** made through the format, as an author would: a terrain drawn as a 16-bit PNG (`tools/maps/val-du-lac.png`, computed by `tools/maps/example.ts`: hills, a deep lake where you drown, a shallow river), imported at a 2 m step over 320 m with mountains at the edge, then a village (brick houses, church and its bell tower), a farm (a barn you can drive through), a blockhouse on the western hill, a ruin on its mound to the east, an old stone bridge on the north-south axis, woods, rocks, five zones and six named places; symmetric about the east-west axis. 253 props including 9 movable, 32 boxes; 75 KB. `tests/communityMaps.test.ts` checks that it is indeed the one its tool makes. Measured (`MAP_PERF=1`, same machine loaded by other computations): a combat step with 12 robots costs 7.2 ms (Valley 7.1, Depot 6.4).
- **The game's arenas as maps (`mapFromArena`, map/export.ts; `content/arenas/`):** each arena of `ARENA_SOURCES` (its code, arena/sources.ts) written as a `MapFile` (`npm run map:export -- all`, tools/maps/arenas.ts adds its names and menu sentences in each language; not in `maps/`: the server would take them for community maps). The round trip is exact: read back (`readMapText`) and built (`arenaFromMap`), each file gives the code's arena to the bit, sign of zeros and terrain included (the code rounds its terrains to the format's 16 bits once, `quantizeTerrain`, and the export writes the encoding a decoded terrain comes from as is, `terrainSource`; `tests/arenaExport.test.ts`, field by field: boxes, props, zones, paths, bounds, sky, ground palette, terrain, nav grid or graph, places…), except the Volcanic Island's waterfall direction (an `atan2` angle no degree value gives back to the bit, read only by the renderer: within 1e-12 rad). A 2 s battle with twelve robots on each arena read from its file gives the same fingerprint as on the code's arena. What it took: everything written explicitly (each element's `y`, enclosure walls and invisible bounds as boxes with `limits.bound: 0`, `nav: false`, the small arenas' sky `PLAIN_SKY` and their own `ambience`, `airFloor: 0`); angles in degrees searched among the readable values and their floating-point neighbors so that `turnQuat` gives back the exact quaternion (`exactTurn`, `exactDegrees`), otherwise a `quat` (a rotation composed otherwise by the code, like the rocks' random orientations, or an angle no degree value gives back, like a full turn or the City's wrecks turned beyond one: 37 of the 797 turned elements of the ten files), and `quat: [0, 0, 0, 1]` for an upright prop that has an orientation (`spot.rot` present counts in sim/wave.ts); a prop's `centered` height when no base gives its center back (base + half-height rounds to even: 22 props); `-0` written as is by `mapText` (map/text.ts: one element per line, the terrain last; a map read then rewritten gives the same text); `peakLine: NO_PEAK` (300 m) instead of `Infinity` (the Canyon, the City: the same rendering, and valid JSON). Sizes: 2 to 3 KB for the small arenas, 169 KB (Valley), 572 KB (Canyon: its 1 m terrain is 506 KB of base64), 271 KB (Island), 199 KB (City), 172 KB (Harbor).
- **The game's arenas, played from their files (map/gameArenas.ts; client/maps/gameArenas.ts, smallArenas.ts):** the game, the solo Worker, the server and the tests play each game arena from its file in `content/arenas/`; the code that makes it (`ARENA_SOURCES`, arena/sources.ts, with valley.ts, canyon.ts, island.ts, city.ts, port.ts) only writes the files and is the reference of the round trip: it enters neither the client's bundles nor the server's (`tests/architecture.test.ts` follows the imports from their entry points). What a community map needs from the game's arenas stays in code, apart: the houses and tenements of its prefabs (arena/buildings.ts) and the ground palettes (arena/grounds.ts, `ARENA_GROUNDS`: a map's `ground.preset`, offered by the map editor). `ArenaId` stays the identifier (network, menus, translated names, the server's rotation): a game arena is built from its file by `arenaFromMap(carte, id)`, with its identifier and without `community` (its names are the game's, translated). **Registry (arena/layout.ts):** `ARENA_IDS` is a fixed list; `arenaById(id)` and `knownGameArena(id)` give a game arena if its file has been provided (undefined otherwise), `gameArena(id)` throws, and `ARENAS[id]` reads like the object it used to be (a getter on `gameArena`); an arena is built on its first request, once (the same object afterward: its nav grid is computed once). Where the files come from is plugged in by map/gameArenas.ts (`setGameArenaSource`): the shared code has neither `fetch` nor `fs`, those who run it provide each file's text (`provideGameArenaText`), an already-read file (`provideGameArenaFile`) or a synchronous reader (`readGameArenasWith`). **Browser:** the four small arenas (~3 KB each; the Depot is the arena of the first `Simulation` and of the first replica) are in the bundle (smallArenas.ts, an eager `import.meta.glob`, which the solo Worker imports too); each large arena is a chunk of its own (gameArenas.ts, a lazy `import.meta.glob` of `?raw`), downloaded once (`loadArena`) when it is played (`App.play` waits for it behind the loading screen, "Arena file…", and says so if it can't be loaded) or shown: the arena picker starts the six downloads when it opens (a card waits for its plan and its facts: `ArenaOption.layout` and `facts` may be promises), and the title demo plays the arenas already there and fetches the one it drew, for its next wave (`randomArena`, game.ts). **Solo Worker:** the `start` command carries the file of a large arena, which the session provides (`provideGameArenaFile`). **Network:** the protocol didn't change for them (`PROTOCOL_VERSION` stayed 26; 27 since map packages): the server only names the arena, and `NetClient` holds back, in order, the frame that puts in place an arena whose file isn't there and everything after it, until the file has loaded (if it can't, the replica notes the arena as missing, `missingArena`). **Server:** reads and builds the ten files at startup (`ARENAS_DIR`; an arena whose file is missing or unreadable leaves the rotation, the Depot is required; `ARENAS=valley,canyon`: only those, in that order). **Tests:** `tests/arenaFiles.ts` (vitest's `setupFiles`) reads them from disk on demand. Measured (a machine loaded by other computations): in Node, reading a large arena's file takes 5 to 20 ms and building it 4 to 27 ms (a small one: about 1 ms in all), against ~0.9 s to import the code that builds the Valley, the City, the Island and the Harbor at load time (which the page used to pay at startup) and ~0.8 s for the Canyon's getters (terrain 655 ms, nav graph 90 ms); the server reads and builds all ten in ~0.3 s (the Canyon 70 ms). In headless Chrome (served locally): a large arena downloads in 70 to 250 ms, is read in 7 to 22 ms and built in 8 to 52 ms (the Canyon); the six large chunks weigh 1.62 MB, ~770 KB gzipped (the Canyon 586 KB, 352 KB gzipped: its 1 m terrain compresses poorly; the others 53 to 95 KB gzipped). The bundles lost the arenas' code: main bundle 7,121,082 → 7,079,302 bytes (−42 KB, −19 KB gzipped; the small arenas' 11 KB of files included), solo Worker 4,744,058 → 4,718,684, map-checking Worker 109,271 → 51,969 (it used to take every arena along, through the ground palettes).
- **Map packages (map/pack.ts, shared/media/; `npm run map:pack`, `npm run map:unpack`; the authors' guide: docs/maps.md, "Map packages"):** a folder (the author's working form) with `map.json` (the map, its `terrain.image` naming the image instead of `heights`), `terrain.png` (16-bit grayscale, `cells + 1` pixels per side, each pixel the 16-bit code, north at the top and west on the left, like `imageFromHeights`), `LICENSE.txt` (`licenseText`), and optional **resources**: glTF models for existing prop types (`models/props/<type>.glb`, which replace, on this map only, the game's model of that type) and an ambient sound (`sounds/ambience.ogg`, an Ogg Opus loop, mono or stereo, 2 to 60 s, instead of a game arena's `ambience`); no new prop type (that needs code: catalog, collisions). **One file to exchange:** the folder as a zip archive (`.casse-map.zip`; `PACKAGE_EXTENSION`), read and written without dependencies (shared/media/zip.ts: `openZip` reads the central directory, then each wanted entry on demand, stored or deflated; `writeZip` writes the entries in a fixed order, dated 1 January 1980, names in UTF-8: the same files give the same bytes); the raw deflate is supplied like a PNG's (`DecompressionStream("deflate-raw")`/`CompressionStream` in the browser, client/maps/files.ts; `inflateRawSync`/`deflateRawSync` in Node). An archive made by any zip tool reads too, everything inside one folder included (`zipSource`), and a backslash counts as a slash (the old `Compress-Archive`). **Reading (`readPackage`, on a `PackageSource`: `zipSource` for an archive, `folderSource` for a folder through a small file-system interface, `FolderAccess`, which the tool and the server give node:fs):** untrusted input, everything bounded before it's read (`PACKAGE_LIMITS`, format.ts: the archive 24 MB and 128 files, `map.json` 4 MB, the image 2 MB, a model 4 MB and 16 MB for all, the ambience 2 MB; a file's size comes from the directory or the file system before anything is decompressed; an entry is decompressed at most to its announced size, `InflateRaw`'s `max`, so a "bomb" is abandoned on the way, then its length and CRC are checked; the image's PNG header is read before its data is inflated, and decodePng's inflate is bounded by the image's size too, `Inflate`'s `max`); names checked (`safeEntryName`: no `..`, no absolute path, no drive letter, no duplicate); only the expected files are read (`packageFileLimit`): notes and work files are ignored, anything else under `models/` or `sounds/` is an issue (`assetName`); each model passes the checks of `npm run models` (`inspectGlb`, shared/media/glb.ts, which now holds them and `GLTF_EXTENSIONS`; tools/models/glb.ts only says them in French), the ambience is checked page by page (`inspectOpus`, shared/media/ogg.ts: one logical stream, page CRCs, `OpusHead` and `OpusTags`, its duration from the last granule position minus the pre-skip); a faulty resource is a `MapIssue` (`archive`, `packagePath`, `packageMap`, `assetSize`, `assetName`, `assetModel` with its `GlbProblem`, `assetSound` with its `OpusProblem`; `mapIssue.<code>`, `glbProblem.<code>`, `opusProblem.<code>` in each language), and nothing throws. It returns the map, with **its resources' SHA-256 and size** (`MapFile.assets`, `MapAssets`: written from the files, whatever `map.json` says; shared/media/sha256.ts, a hand-written SHA-256, since `crypto.subtle` only exists on secure pages and the game server serves plain http on a LAN), the resources by their SHA-256 (`AssetShelf`) and the license. `packMap(carte, deflate, ressources)`, `packageEntries` and `writeMapArchive` write it back. **Identity:** `mapKey` hashes the map with its `assets`: a resource changed by one byte makes another map, and a map without resources has no `assets` field, so its identifier is the one it had before packages (the bundled Lake Vale, the ten files of `content/arenas/`). A package without resources and a map file convert into each other byte for byte (`tests/mapPackage.test.ts`); the PNG compresses the terrain: the Volcanic Island's package weighs 115 KB against 271 KB for its file; Lake Vale with a 44 KB model and a 203 KB ambience: a 245 KB archive (36 KB without resources), written in ~70 ms and read back in ~35 ms in Node, ~0.55 s to import in headless Chrome (archive, checks, SHA-256, IndexedDB; the SHA-256 alone: ~20 MB/s on a loaded machine); online, the crate model travels gzipped (45 → 6 KB), the ambience as is (204 KB), and the map's model replaces the game's ~0.35 s after the match starts (`tools/browser/` driving the built page and a game server whose `MAPS_DIR` holds the archive). **Where the resources live:** in the browser, `MapAssetStore` (client/maps/assets.ts, one per page: `mapAssets`): IndexedDB's `assets` store (db.ts version 3), by SHA-256, once for every map that uses them; in memory (48 MB at most) while in use; a resource is handed out only checked against the map (`assetMatches`: its size, its SHA-256, the checks of its kind). `MapLibrary.importPackage`/`importFile` import an archive (the menu's "Import a map…" takes `.zip` too), `remove` forgets the resources nothing uses any more (maps of the library, of the cache and the editor's drafts), `downloadPackage` exports a map with resources as its archive (the map screen and the editor). On the server, in memory (`MAPS_DIR`, see "Multiplayer"). **In the game:** a prop whose type the arena's map gives a model (`ArenaLayout.assets`, copied by `arenaFromMap`) takes it (`Assets.propSource(…, own)`, `PropModels`: a source of its own, keyed by the SHA-256, and its own materials; read like the game's models, `ModelReads` given where its bytes come from; the source drawn by code until it is read, the game's model if it can't be); the sound plays the map's ambience (`Sound.updateAmbience`: decoded once, one at a time; `mendForeignLoop` brings it to the level of the game's loops, −16 dB RMS, and crossfades its last 0.25 s into its start, then it loops from there to its end), the declared `ambience` while it loads or if it can't be read. The map editor: **File → Resources (package)** lists, adds (a `.glb` for a chosen prop type, an `.ogg`) and removes resources (`withAsset`: a map left without resources loses its `assets` field), opens a package and exports one; its draft keeps the map's `assets`. Tests: `tests/mapArchive.test.ts` (the zip, the SHA-256, the checks, a package written and read back, identity, refusals, random corruption that never throws), `tests/mapAssets.test.ts` (the browser's store, downloads checked, `withAsset`), `tests/propModels.test.ts` (a map's model), `tests/network.test.ts` (a package's map over the network).
- **Known limits:** the AI follows a skewed bridge poorly (its decks are read along the axes: arena/navgrid.ts, ai/navigation.ts); the boxes are those of the game's arenas (a package brings its own prop models and ambience, not new kinds of boxes or props); a structural error in a map hides its construction issues (you fix the first, then you see the next ones).

### Content catalog and element files (`content/`)

The game's elements are data, in two kinds of files: the **element files**, one per element, which the game reads (a modder tunes or adds an element there), and the **content catalog**, generated from the code, for other tools.

#### Element files (`content/<family>/<id>.json`)

One file per element, named after its identifier, in a folder per family: the block types (`content/blocks/<type>.json`, with the block's unlock `price`; not the shaped blocks, which are computed, see below), the wheel types (`content/wheels/<type>.json`, with the wheel's unlock `price`), the weapons (`content/weapons/<id>.json`, with their projectile), the melee weapons (`content/melee/<id>.json`) and the prop types (`content/props/<type>.json`). Tuning an element is editing its file, then `npm run schemas` (the content catalog, generated, carries its numbers) and rewriting the catalogs' snapshot (below), both checked by `npm run check`: no code changes.

- **What a file holds:** the element's definition, field by field as the code's type says (`BlockTypeDef`, `WheelTypeDef`, `Weapon`, `MeleeSpec`, `PropTypeDef`), in meters, kilograms, seconds, radians; plus what the element has outside its definition (a block's or a wheel's unlock `price`, which `splitFiles` sets apart into `BLOCK_PRICES` and `WHEEL_PRICES`, now in catalog/blocks.ts and catalog/wheels.ts, which design/progression.ts re-exports). What JSON can't write has a written form that the family's catalog turns back: a magazine that never reloads (the warhead, one shot per life) is `"reload": null`, which `weaponOf` (catalog/weapons.ts) makes `Infinity` again, as the content catalog writes it. The fields whose name starts with `$` are not data: `$schema`, the family's schema relative to the file (`../schemas/props.schema.json`: an editor such as VS Code then completes and checks the file as it is typed), and `$comment`, a note on why the values are what they are (in French, like the code comments it replaces). The game drops them (`fileData`, catalog/content.ts), and so does the bundle: the Vite plugin `contentData` (vite.config.ts, also in `worker.plugins`: the Workers' bundles have their own plugins) removes them before Vite turns the file into a module, and `json.namedExports: false` makes each file a single object (Vite's default, one export per field, wrote one constant per field into the bundle).
- **How they enter the code:** each family's catalog imports its files one by one, statically (`import crate from "../../../content/props/crate.json"`), lists them in the catalog's order (`FILES`) and reads them with `fromFiles` (catalog/content.ts). A static JSON import is read the same way everywhere: by Vite (the page, the solo Worker, the editor's checking Worker, the dev server), by `vite build --ssr` (the server, the tools compiled into `dist-tools/`), by Vitest, and TypeScript types it (`moduleResolution: bundler` implies `resolveJsonModule`); `src/shared` has neither `fs` nor `fetch`. Nothing is checked when the game starts (it costs nothing): the checks are the schemas' (below).
- **TypeScript keeps its checks:** the identifiers stay in TypeScript, as the import lines: `PropType = keyof typeof FILES`, `WheelType`, `WeaponId`, `MeleeId` likewise, and `BlockType`, the block files and the shaped blocks (`SHAPED`, catalog/blocks.ts), so every exhaustive `Record` of the client (a look, a sound, a name per element) is still checked when compiling. TypeScript also sees each imported file's structure (not its values: a text there is a `string`): an element's type is its definition where every optional field its file gives becomes certain (`Given<File, Def>`, catalog/content.ts), as when the catalogs were TypeScript literals (`WEAPONS.smoke.projectile.smoke` has its cloud, `WEAPONS.warhead.ammo` its magazine), and a union derived from the catalog still is (`MineWeaponId`, the weapons whose projectile has a `mine`: `MINE_MODELS` stays exhaustive over it). The other way, a script that generates a types file from the JSON files, was ruled out: adding an element already takes TypeScript (its look, its sound, its names: the exhaustive `Record`s demand them), so one import line costs nothing more, and there is no generated file that could go stale; `tests/contentFiles.test.ts` checks that a family's folder holds exactly one file per imported identifier. The values' types come from the family's schema, which the code's types write (`fields<T>()`, see below), and which checks each file.
- **Validation:** each family has its JSON Schema, `content/schemas/<family>.schema.json`, written by `npm run schemas` from the code's types (`familySchema`, tools/schemas/catalog.ts: the same definitions as the content catalog's, without the names, with `$schema`, `$comment` and what the file holds beyond the definition; English descriptions). `tests/contentFiles.test.ts` checks every file against it, with no field the schema ignores (a misspelled optional field), and names a malformed one by its file and field (`content/props/crate.json: hp: …`); it also checks that the game reads each file exactly as `JSON.parse` does (to the bit, without its `$` fields), and the rules a schema can't say (a steered wheel has the size, motor and suspension of its fixed wheel, `steeredVariant`; a `null` reload is `Infinity` in the game).
- **Nothing changed in the game:** `tests/reference/catalogs.json` is a snapshot of the code's catalogs (props, wheels and their prices, blocks and their prices, weapons, melee weapons, preset robots) taken before they moved into files: each entry on its line, every number to the bit (infinities and −0 marked), its fields in their order, and the order of the identifiers. One order changed, on purpose: `BLOCK_PRICES` now follows the order of `BLOCK_TYPES` (it was written in its own order; it is only read by identifier). `tests/catalogSnapshot.test.ts` compares the catalogs the code builds with it. A deliberate change of an element (a balance tweak) rewrites it in the same commit (`CATALOG_SNAPSHOT=write npx vitest run tests/catalogSnapshot.test.ts`): its diff shows what changed. The battles' fingerprints (the Depot, the Valley, the City; poses, velocities and hit points of every robot and prop after 10 to 12 s) are the same, bit for bit, before and after.
- **Bundle:** measured with `vite build`, before and after (all five families: main bundle 7,121,082 → 7,123,811 bytes, +2.7 KB; solo Worker 4,744,058 → 4,747,832, +3.8 KB; the map editor's checking Worker 109,271 → 110,139, +0.9 KB): the loader and the shared values (a material's resistances, a wheel size, a wing's profile) now written in each file that uses them.
- **What stays in TypeScript, and why:** what is computed: the block shapes (half-spaces clipped from the cube, the round shapes' facets computed by trigonometry), the shaped blocks (`SHAPED`, catalog/blocks.ts: `shaped(material, shape)` cuts them out of the armor files, their mass, hit points and cost in proportion to the shape's volume; their unlock prices, `SHAPED_PRICES`, next to them), constants and derived values (`CAR_HALF`, `springTuning`, `steerClearance`, `motorPower`…) and the functions; the preset robots (designs written as construction code: boxes, mirrored pairs, loops, a comment on each choice; as JSON, each would be a flat list of a few hundred blocks, and a design is already an open format: `schemas/robot.schema.json`).

#### Content catalog (`content/catalog.json`)

The game's elements as open, documented data, for other tools (an editor, a wiki, a balance spreadsheet, the compiled simulation of the engine plan) and for anyone curious: `content/catalog.json`, and its JSON Schema `content/catalog.schema.json` (draft 2020-12, English descriptions of every field, with units). It is generated from the code's catalogs (which read the element files), with their exhaustiveness checks:

- **What it holds:** `constants` (block size, plate thickness, gravity, build budget, generator power, a melee motor's efficiency, the heat at which a weapon fires again), `damageTypes`, `shapes` (each block shape: its share of the cube, whether its mirror image swaps its left and front, its convex pieces as faces in a cube of half-side 1), `blocks` (mass, hit points, cost, bond, resistances, shape, and the turret, thruster and its exhaust, wing, support, light, battery, repair, shield, radar, jammer, flare and melee specs; plus its unlock `price`), `wheels` (plus `price` and `kind`), `weapons` (with their `projectile`: guidance, explosion, mine, hull, repair, barrier…), `melee` (blade, spin, swing, plate, flail, jaw, thrust, field, bite, hit), `props` (collision shape, mass, hit points, resistances, explosion, burning), `robots` (each preset robot: its design, as the garage saves it, its cost and mass, color, speeds, behavior, mobility). Every entry has its display `names` in French and English, taken from the message catalogs (`block.<type>`, `weapon.<id>`… in i18n/locales/<language>/catalog.ts; a robot's `names` and `traits` in bots.ts). An infinite number (the warhead launcher's magazine, which never reloads) is written `null`. The file is indented, but an object or a list that fits on a line stays on it (a design's block, a vector): ~0.3 MB.
- **The schema follows the code's types:** each object is described by `fields<T>()`, which TypeScript forces to give every field of the type `T`, no more, optional (`may`) or not (`need`) as in the type; each enumeration by `all<U>()`, which must list every value of the union `U`. A field added to a catalog does not build until it is documented in the schema. The design of a robot reuses the robot schema's definitions (`schemas/robot.schema.json`).
- **Checked** by `tests/content.test.ts`: the catalog follows its schema with no field the schema ignores (`unknownProperties`), every field of the schema has a description, every entry of the code's catalogs is there with its values and its names in both languages, the shapes' points are in the cube, the preset robots' designs are those of the code and pass `validateDesign`.
- **Format:** `format` (1) goes up when a change breaks what tools read (a field renamed or removed, a unit changed); adding a field or an entry does not change it.

### Combat

- **Projectiles (`combat/projectiles.ts`):** every shot launches a projectile (`ProjectileSpec`: speed, share of gravity, flight time, damage and its type, penetration, impulse, explosion, `command`/`homing` guidance, proximity fuse, mine, fire, electromagnetic pulse, smoke, `pellets` spread, `fan`). It advances each step by a raycast between two positions, ignores the robot that fired it, pierces as long as it destroys what it hits, explodes, bursts (EMP, smoke) or settles (mine: the rest is in combat/mines.ts). A shotgun shot (`pellets`) launches nine pellets at once for a single `shot`; a salvo rocket (`fan`) leaves fanned out, then its guidance brings it back toward the aimed point (this is not inaccuracy: the crosshair circle ignores it). Fire rate is counted down on the fixed step, with a tolerance (`canFire`): without it, a leftover rounding error cost one extra step (the laser fired 8.6 times per second instead of 10).
- **Command-guided missiles (`command` guidance):** they follow the aiming axis of their missile launcher (`steer`, combat/projectiles.ts: the point on the line that starts at its muzzle, along the barrel axis, `LINE_LEAD` m ahead of them), like a real command-guided missile: turning the turret brings them back onto the new line; with the missile launcher destroyed, or from the free camera, they follow the aimed point. They are therefore fired straight at the target (`firingDirection`), without the elevation that seekers and the warhead take.
- **Launch along the axis, momentum, seeker head (`steer`, combat/projectiles.ts; `Guidance`):** every guided missile (command-guided, lock-on, anti-air, rockets, warhead) first flies straight along the weapon's axis for `Guidance.launch` s, then its guidance takes over. A homing missile has momentum: it turns no tighter than its lateral acceleration allows (`maxAccel`: the faster it goes, the wider its turn), each turn costs it a share of its speed (`bleed`, per radian), which its motor gives back (`recover`), and it has weight (`ProjectileSpec.gravity`). It must see its target (`sightBlocked`, combat/sight.ts: terrain, scenery, props and smoke block the view, robots do not; the cockpit or a point 0.8 m above it): with the target hidden, it flies on its momentum without turning (`Projectile.blind`), then loses the target after `SEEKER_MEMORY` (`lost`, it flies straight); nanites, which go to an ally, ignore this. Fired upward at a locked target behind a wall or a hill, it climbs on its momentum, finds the target again from above and dives onto it (a lob); fired straight, it hits the obstacle. Lock-on missiles: `maxAccel` 60, `bleed` 0.6, gravity 0.6: all the lobs of `tests/missileSight.test.ts` hit (wall at 20 and 40 m), a target moving straight at 10 m/s is hit, a sharp swerve at 10-15 m makes it miss (at 55 and 0.3, it missed the short lobs; at 80 and 0.5, nothing could dodge it anymore).
- **Turrets (`combat/turrets.ts`, `ai/gunner.ts`):** a head aimed in yaw and pitch, at limited speed, independently of the robot. The AI picks the nearest **spotted** enemy in range that suits the weapon, aims with lead and a ballistic angle, checks the line of fire (no teammate, no scenery, no smoke), then fires. Each turret scores targets (`targetScore`): the nearest, preferably damaged (`integrity`), vulnerable to its damage type (`vulnerability`, based on block resistances) and already engaged by its robot. It aims with an error (`Turret.wobble`, `AIM_BASE` plus `AIM_TRACKING` per m/s of crosswise relative speed): a maneuvering target is missed more often. Two teams (`Bot.team`) fight each other; the free camera is everyone's enemy.
- **Accuracy (`combat/accuracy.ts`):** `Weapon.spread` is a weapon's best spread (scoped, robot stationary); `Weapon.handling` says how it degrades: ×`hip` without the scope, +`move` rad per m/s of robot speed, +`turn` rad per rad/s of rotation (the robot turns, pitches, bounces). A plane in flight is a stable platform: only its rotation counts. `aimSpread` gives the spread of a player shot (at 2 m/s, the sniper strays by 3°: you have to stop to aim); the AI and the free camera fire with `hipSpread` (the former spread), the AI's aiming error already growing with speeds.
- **Lobbed fire (`lobbed`, `combat/ballistics.ts`):** for a slow weapon subject to gravity, neither guided nor laid (cannon, EMP shell, smoke shell), the player chooses the elevation: the barrel points straight at the crosshair (`lobDirection`, which only subtracts the velocity inherited from the robot), with no drop correction. To fire farther, you raise the mouse; the range follows from the elevation (and from the flight time `life`), and a crosshair in the sky no longer sends the shell off at 45°. Higher or lower than the turret can go, it fires at its stop (`aimTurret(…, reachable)`). `predictImpact` follows the trajectory as `projectiles.ts` integrates it (`ballisticPoint`) up to the first obstacle: that is the HUD's impact point. The AI keeps the ballistic solution (`firingDirection`).
- **Line of fire (`lineOfFireBlocked`, combat/turrets.ts):** projectiles ignore the robot that fires them; a turret therefore does not fire if its line of fire crosses its own robot. The line is traced from the head's axis, cell by cell in the robot's grid (no physics), until it leaves the robot's clearance: a block of its part or the head of another of its turrets masks it. Applies to the player and the AI; the HUD says "Shot blocked by your own robot" (red crosshair) when all the turrets of the selected weapon are masked, and the garage flags a turret buried under a block (`buriedTurrets`), which will never fire.
- **Identical weapons (`fireInTurn`, combat/turrets.ts; `stackRate`, combat/arsenal.ts):** the turrets of the same weapon on a robot fire in turn, on a shared rhythm (`Bot.nextShot`): each turret ready to fire (player: aimed, trigger; AI: target, clear path) becomes a request (`ShotRequest`), and each step, per weapon, only the one that has been waiting longest (`Turret.firedAt`) fires, if the rhythm allows. n weapons fire `stackRate(n)` times faster than one alone, with diminishing returns (each extra weapon adds `STACK_FALLOFF` = 0.8 times what the previous one added: ×1.8 with two, ×2.95 with four, never ×5); each therefore fires less often than alone, and its ammo and heat last that much longer. A lone weapon keeps its fire rate. No preset robot has two identical weapons: this only affects garage robots.
- **Lock-on (`combat/lock.ts`, weapon with `lock`):** an enemy must be kept in the weapon's cone (`lock.cone` around the aim) for `lock.time` s before firing; the missiles (seeker head) chase the locked target (`Turret.lockOn`, `lockTime`). Once acquired, the target stays held in a cone 1.8 times wider; out of the cone, the lock decays twice as fast as it builds, then moves to the enemy closest to the aim. Only enemies that are spotted, in range and in the weapon's sight can be locked (`sightBlocked`); a target hidden by scenery or smoke no longer advances the lock, and it is lost after `LOCK_GRACE` (1 s: a passing pole does not make it let go, a target that has just gone behind a hill stays locked long enough to fire a lob; `Turret.lockHidden`). Once acquired, the lock holds as long as the target stays within `LOCKED_HOLD` (~43°) of the aim: you can aim higher than or beside an obstacle to fire a lob. The player locks only through the scope (right click), and only what is under the crosshair (selected weapon only), and the lock drops when they leave the scope; the AI locks what it aims at; both fire only at their locked target. The HUD frames the target with tightening corners, red once the lock is acquired (`Instruments.showLock`); while locking, beeps that come closer and closer together (`LOCK_BEEP_SLOW` to `LOCK_BEEP_FAST`, game.ts), then a double beep; online, each frame's header carries the player's lock (`FrameHeader.lock`, applied by the replica).
- **Piloted warhead (`piloted` guidance, `pilotedWarhead`, `fuseNow`):** one shot per life (a one-round magazine that does not reload), a huge explosion (550 over 5 m). The AI does not fire it at an enemy closer than `minRange` (20 m): the explosion would hit it. While it flies, the player who fired it steers it: it turns (1.3 rad/s at most) toward the aimed point of their controls (`steer`, combat/projectiles.ts); their robot stops and its turrets stop aiming (`thinkPlayer` neutralizes its controls; a plane, which cannot stop, flies level at the target altitude, held by the autopilot, and the warhead launcher weighs only 5 kg so that a plane can carry it); a new click (trigger released since the shot, `Bot.triggerHeld`) detonates it on the next step, as at the end of its run. The AI command-guides it toward its target like a `command` missile. With no preferred range (`engage`), it counts neither in the AI's positioning nor in its retreats to reload. Client side, `ArenaGame` follows the player's warhead through its events (`projectileAdded`/`projectileRemoved`): the camera moves to its nose (the scope mechanism, without magnification, looking up to ±83°), you aim at the center of the screen (`Controls.centerAim`), a panel gives the remaining flight time, the altitude, the speed and the distance to the robot (`Instruments.showWarhead`); after the explosion, the camera watches it for 1.2 s from slightly behind, then returns to the robot. Does not go through the protocol: the controls (`aim`, `trigger`) are enough.
- **Bombs (`Weapon.dropped`, `bombBay` block):** a bomb bay is a fixed turret facing down (`TurretSpec.fixed`: `"back"` for the mine layer, `"down"` for the bay; its head, a rack with two bombs hanging from it, is below the block: negative `height`; the cell below must stay free, `buriedTurrets`). It releases its bombs without aiming (`released`: mines and bombs; `lobbed` excludes them), in a string as long as the trigger is held (eight, 60 plus 170 over 2.4 m); they fall keeping the plane's speed and explode on the ground (`bomb` look). The player sees where the next one would fall: a circle the size of the explosion and the fall time (`ArenaGame.bombing`, `predictImpact`, `Instruments.showBombing`). The AI drops when a bomb released now would fall on its target, where the target will be (`bombsOnTarget`, ai/gunner.ts, with `fallTime` from combat/ballistics.ts), never on a teammate; it only targets ground robots (`suits`).
