The brake (Space on the ground, `brake` from 0 to 1, bounded by the server) takes precedence over throttle, boost and sprint: the wheel motors are disengaged (their voltage still follows the command: released, the robot sets off again without waiting) and every wheel, tracks included, brakes (`Wheel.brake`, robot/wheels.ts: an implicit force that would stop the contact point in one step, capped at `BRAKE_GRIP` of what the tire carries: no lock-up, the wheel still holds sideways; it holds the robot at rest), as hard as the robot's stability allows (`PITCH_MARGIN` of `pitchoverAccel`, robot/steering.ts: the deceleration that would flip it over its front wheels; only the Stilt and the Crane are limited by it); pads and legs aim to stop, faster (`BRAKE_GAIN`), a pad gripping harder (`BRAKE_HOVER_TRACTION`); a flying robot stops on the spot (with the default keys, the brake only applies on the ground: in the air, Space makes it climb); in a plane, nothing (no airbrake: S reduces the throttle, X cuts it). Measured (`tests/brake.test.ts`, from cruising speed): stop in 0.8 s (Trike), 0.3 s (Tank, Spider), 0.9 s (Hovercraft), 1.5 s (Stilt), versus 1.1, 0.8, 0.6, 1.8 and 1.8 s with the throttle released, without the robot tipping. The AI does not brake; its boost (fleeing or charging, with a booster) goes through the same ratchet freewheel, without the sprint.
- **Matches (`sim/match.ts`):** `startMatch(sim, rules)` (Battle is played to 15 kills); each `botKilled` gives a point to the opponent; respawn on its team's side (`respawnBot`) after `respawn` s, or never (elimination); an AI robot immobilized too long (overturned without being able to right itself, without wheels, falling) self-destructs; end on score, on time, or when a team has nobody left (`matchOver`). **Zone Capture** (`MatchRules.capture`): kills no longer count; a team alone in a zone flips it (`ZoneState.progress` from -1, red, to 1, blue: first neutralized, then taken, √n times faster with n robots), both teams at once contest it; each zone held brings `rate` points per second (the score has fractions, displayed rounded); `zoneChanged` at each change of hands. **King of the Hill** (`CaptureRules.hill`): only one zone is in play (`ZoneState.active`), the center one first; every `hill` s (`Match.nextHill`), it moves to another, drawn at random, and the old one returns to neutral. Zones out of play cannot be taken, the AI only targets the hill (`objectiveZone`), the snapshot carries the state (one bit), the client hides zones out of play and grays out their badge, and the feed announces the move.
- **Righting (`robot/righting.ts`):** the cockpit's gyroscope sets an overturned robot that has been immobile for `RIGHTING_DELAY` s back upright (a small hop then a rotation toward vertical, by directly setting the velocities of the cockpit's part), for the AI as for the player; the same hop frees an AI robot stuck despite its maneuvers (`unstick`, after `UNSTICK_DELAY`). Not for planes.
- **Teams (`sim/wave.ts`):** `spawnWave` (teams alternating around the circle; inner circles when it is full, then a circle near the walls as a last resort: without it, a very large wave placed its last robots straddling their neighbors) or `spawnTeams` (two lines facing each other `SPAWN_LINE` m from the center, blue to the south and red to the north, about sixty meters apart: out of rifle range). `matchEntries` (modes.ts) composes the teams of a piloted match: the player at the head of the blue team, then the robots drawn at random, distributed from most to least expensive into the team that costs the least so far (the player's robot included): the two teams have a similar cost (`teamCost`), and a player who takes a small robot gets heavier teammates. The server fills its teams the same way. This was the real cause of unbalanced matches: replayed with sides swapped, the same compositions won in the north as in the south, in all three arenas.
- **Budget (`design/budget.ts`):** every block and every wheel has a cost (`cost`); `validateDesign` refuses a design over `ROBOT_BUDGET`.
- **Progression (`design/progression.ts`, `client/progress.ts`):** every type of block and wheel has an unlock price (`BLOCK_PRICES`, `WHEEL_PRICES`: the `price` of each block or wheel file, the shaped blocks' in `SHAPED_PRICES`; 0 = available from the start; a steered wheel costs more than the fixed one of its size). Parts unlocked in a previous version go through `upgradeUnlocked` (client/progress.ts, server/progressBook.ts): whoever had the heavy wheel also has its steered version. A piloted match earns credits (`matchReward`: match played, victory or draw, the player's kills, zones taken by their team, counted by `ArenaGame.matchStats`), displayed at the end of the match. The player keeps their credits and parts in the browser (`Progress`, key `casse-robot.progress`); in the garage, a locked part shows its price and is unlocked with a click; a robot that contains some can be tried out, but not played in a match (`lockedParts`). The "Every part" setting (sandbox).
- **Garage:** `client/garage/garage.ts` edits a `RobotDesign` (place, remove, orientation, mirror), saves it (`storage.ts`) and has it tried out (`trial` mode); **inventory** (right panel, `client/garage/inventory.ts`): parts by category (`partCategory`, tabs with their count, plus "All" and "Recent", kept in the browser, `casse-robot.garage.recent`), sorted by group (`partGroup`: direct-fire, explosive, guided, support weapons, mines, dropped; shapes; fixed, steered wheels, casters), an accent-insensitive search on the name, the category and the info sheet (`matchesSearch`, "/" goes there, the key of `garageSearch` named by the field: "!" on an AZERTY keyboard; Enter takes the first one), 3D thumbnails (`PartThumbnails`, render/partThumbnails.ts: a small off-page Babylon engine renders each part once, a few per frame, then keeps the image; released when the garage closes) and the info sheet of the chosen part (`partInfo`, client/garage/partInfo.ts, derived from the catalogs: what it is for, cost, mass, structure, resistances, and what is specific to it: damage, range, rate of fire, thrust, charge…; its rotating 3D preview, a single-part `RobotPreview`; the button to unlock it); 1 to 9 recall the recent parts, the middle click copies the part pointed at (`eyedrop`: it becomes the tool, and a copied block keeps its exact orientation, `copied`, which takes precedence over face orientation until the next part choice; O returns to it). **Robot direction:** on the ground, a "FRONT" arrow in front of it, "BACK", "LEFT" and "RIGHT" around it and the axis of symmetry dotted (`createMarks`, placed by `placeMarks` according to its clearance; drawn mirrored: the ground receives its texture flipped); an on-screen compass that follows the camera (`updateCompass`: the robot's axes projected onto the screen); **orientation:** a block that lends itself to it (`orientsToFace`: non-fixed turret, shape, flare launcher, radar, jammer; not engines, which push toward their top, nor wings, supports, fixed turrets) takes as its top the face it is placed on (`target` returns the face pointed at, the dominant axis of the normal: on a 45° slope, the top): under a block, a turret hangs, on a side it sticks out sideways, barrel at rest toward the front (`faceForward`), a shape has its solid underside stuck to its support; R rotates it a quarter turn around its top (`spin`), Shift + R tilts it (the top moves to the next axis, orientation becomes manual), O turns face orientation off or back on; a preview (`ghost`, `buildGhost`) shows the block as it will be placed: its shape, a turret's head, a yellow arrow for an engine (the direction of its thrust) or a flare launcher; a block's mirror counterpart is its mirror image (`mirrorFacing`, design/robotDesign.ts); `design/custom.ts` turns it into a playable profile (mobility and speed derived from the organs). Every change to the design goes through `edit()`, which makes it undoable (Ctrl+Z, Ctrl+Y). **Shortcuts:** all those of the garage (place, remove, copy the part pointed at, zone, rotate, tilt, face orientation, mirror (M), layer view, layer above and below, copy, paste, undo, redo, clear, search, recent parts; the keys quoted here are the defaults) are actions of the key table ("Garage" section, `garage` context: see "Keys and gamepad"), rebound like the others: `Garage.actionFor` finds the action of a key press or a click (`eventChords`, `chordCode`: the combination first, then the key alone), `run` performs it; its help texts (panel, help bar, notes, search) name the chosen keys (`Garage.keyName`, which the `App` connects to `Controls.keyName`), redone by `refreshKeys` when the keys or the language change (without redoing a panel with a focused field). Layer view (L, Page↑/↓): the grid rises to the working layer, the layers above are hidden, those below darkened; Ctrl+C / Ctrl+V copy one layer onto another. Zone: Shift + click on two blocks delimits a box (over several layers, yellow outline); Ctrl+C copies it relative to its corner, Ctrl+V pastes it into the cell under the pointer (free cells, without a second cockpit), Delete clears it. The palette sorts wheels by kind (fixed, steered, casters, tracks). A wheel is placed on the face pointed at (side or underside), offset by half a cell toward the edge pointed at (`WheelMount.shift`); a track roller can also be placed on another one (`stackOn`: on its top or underside, the track goes up or down a cell; on its outer face, it widens); a ghost shows where it will go. The fittings (`wheelFittings`, render/fittings.ts: fork of a caster or of a wheel placed underneath, bracket of a caster on a side) and the track belts are drawn as in the game. Outside layer view, the grid floor lowers beneath whatever hangs from the robot. A red ball (seen through the blocks) and a plumb line show the center of mass (`designCenterOfMass`), repeated in the panel. **Clearance (`design/clearance.ts`):** what each part occupies beyond its cell, in simple volumes in the robot's frame (`Solid`: box, cylinder along x, y or z, capsule; `Zone`: the part it belongs to, what it is (`ZonePart`, named in each language: `zonePart.<id>`), the cells it is allowed to encroach on), from the catalogs: a turret's head (`TurretSpec.head` around `headLift`, the height at which it is drawn) and its barrel at rest (a capsule from the head's axis to its muzzle, `barrel`, of caliber `caliber`, straight ahead, horizontal; its orientation `facing` followed), a mine layer's exit (the cell behind), the bombs' path under a bay (the cell below), a wheel (its disc, widened by what it sweeps when steering: `steerClearance`), a track's belt (a box around each group of rollers, `trackGroups`), blades (`BLADES`: tilt rotor, propeller, tail rotor of an engine laid on its side, chosen by `bladeKind` as in the game), a turbojet's nacelle (`JET_NACELLE`), a leg at rest (`restLeg`, outward from the center of mass). A turret only reserves its rest pose, not the circle its barrel sweeps: a block next to its head only limits its field of fire (`lineOfFireBlocked`), and reserving the circle would forbid almost any compact robot; a wheel does not reserve its suspension travel either. A full cell counts whole (the grid remains the truth), except a plate (wing, control surface: a slab, thickened by what a control surface sweeps, `plateBody`): a wheel passes under a wing. A part may encroach by `CLEARANCE_SLACK` (4 cm) on a neighboring cell; two zones get in each other's way if a point is 4 cm inside each (less for a thin volume, like a blade disc); two wheels, by the rule of `validateDesign` (`wheelsOverlap`). `placementConflicts(plan, pièce)` says, in both directions, what a placement would obstruct (a block in a part's zone, or a part whose zone would enter a block or another zone), with the reason (`ConflictReason`, a code the client states in one line, `conflictText`: "In the way of the barrel (Machine gun turret)", "The wheel would hit the propeller (Propeller)") and what is in the way on the other one (`Conflict.solids`); `designConflicts` all those of a design (one line each: `conflictLine`). `validateDesign` ignores them: a design from before stays playable (the panel flags "Parts in each other's way"). No preset robot has parts in each other's way (`tests/clearance.test.ts` checks it for all of them): the Dragster, the last one, had the air intakes of its four turbojets 18 cm into the chassis in front of them; they are now one cell further away (see "Propellers and turbojets"). In the garage, the preview of the part to place (block or wheel) is green if it fits, red otherwise (occupied cell included), with its clearance shown transparently over the blocks and what it would obstruct in red (`ClearanceView`, garage/clearanceView.ts), and the reason under the chosen part (`.g-clash`); a click on an impossible placement places nothing, plays the error sound and states the reason (`refuse`). In mirror mode, the mirror counterpart is checked after the part (if it gets in the way, the part is placed alone and this is stated; an already occupied mirror cell remains silently ignored); pasting a zone or a layer only pastes what fits, part by part, and says how many were left out (`pastePieces`). What the game draws in addition to a block or a wheel (turret heads and munitions, blades, nacelles, legs, pad glow, forks, brackets and steering arms) is also drawn, still, in the garage, in the robot picker's preview, a part's info sheet and the inventory thumbnails (`stillBlockParts`, `stillWheelParts`, render/stillParts.ts), with the same sources and the same dimensions as in the game; the grid floor lowers beneath the feet of a legged robot. `design/flight.ts` estimates a design's lift, thrust and flight endurance (the same one that `liftToWeight` measures in the game).

### Vehicles

- **AI driving (`ai/brain.ts`):** three rays in front of the robot (range depending on its speed) make it slow down and steer around scenery, props and robots (except the enemy it is charging). With steered wheels, it never takes a turn tighter than its stability allows (`rolloverAccel`), builds up momentum to turn around and backs up while counter-steering when it is stuck. Its boosters, if any, fire at full power when fleeing or charging, well aligned, or when an enemy is in their jet (`foeInJet`), never if they would burn its own robot (`scorchesItself`).
- **Driving, all organs (`drive` in robot/propulsion.ts):** wheels and tracks (`driveWheels`), and supports (`driveSupports`), with the same commands on top of throttle and steering (`DriveOptions`: boost, sprint, brake; see "Boost and brake").
- **Wheelless supports (`robot/supports.ts`):** a hover pad or a leg (`SupportSpec`) measures the ground beneath it and pushes like a spring-damper tuned for its rated load; it also pushes horizontally toward the command, within the limit of its grip (pad: slides, consumes energy; leg: grips). The renderer animates the legs (inverse kinematics, alternating gait).
- **Propellers and turbojets (`ThrusterSpec.look`, `topSpeed`):** each engine has its look (`look`: flame by default, `rotor`, `propeller`, `jet`), which decides how it is drawn and how it sounds (no longer `gimbal`). The **propeller** (block `propeller`): a three-blade disc nearly 2 m across in front of its block (`Assets.propellerSource`, `LocomotionView.syncBlades`: spin of `PROPELLER_RPM`, painted tips, anti-strobe); its thrust drops with the airspeed along its axis (`propellerFactor`: all of it at a standstill, none at `topSpeed` = 45 m/s; `t.thrust` remains the engine's spin, which the blades show). The **turbojet** (block `jet`): 700 N (five boosters), slow to spool up (1.4 s), 0.6 kW (two run on the generator, beyond that batteries are needed), a nacelle that sticks out of its block (`WorldView.nacelles`, `jetNacelleSource`) and a flickering afterburner flame more than 2 m long (`jetFlameSource`, from the nozzle `JET_NOZZLE`). Both can be placed in any direction, like the other engines: facing forward they propel (on the ground, when sprinting: Shift), facing up they lift (vertical takeoff; a turbojet, being slow, lifts poorly when hovering), sideways they turn. Sounds: `jet-engine` (in flight, or on the ground when they push), `propeller-big`. A garage plane flies faster the harder it pushes (`cruiseSpeed`, design/custom.ts: `PLANE_SPEED`, from 11 to 20 m/s; it used to aim for 5 m/s). Robots: the Plane has a propeller on its nose, the Bomber one on each wing, the Fighter two turbojets under its wings (18 m/s), the Dragster four at the back of a wheeled chassis (when sprinting, it goes much faster than its wheels can; one cell behind the chassis, held by a spine in the middle, on two levels, so that the cell in front of each stays free: the nacelle's air intake sticks out there 0.43 m from the block's center, `JET_NACELLE`; a second row of heavy armor at the bow counterbalances the 24 kg of the moved-back engines: the front carries 40% of the weight at rest (39% before, 34% without it), its front wheels no longer leave the ground when setting off at a sprint, it steers as before; 109 kg instead of 100, hence a sprint 7% less lively, 24.7 m/s after 2 s instead of 26.5: `tests/engines.test.ts`). Thrusters, boosters and turbojets spit a flame jet that burns whatever is behind their nozzle (see "Engine flame jets").
- **Tail rotors:** a thruster laid on its side (`Thruster.role === "trim"`) is drawn with a two-blade propeller, without a flame (`LocomotionView.syncBlades`, like the tilt rotor's blades). The blades turn like a real rotor: at a nearly constant spin as long as the engine is powered (connected to the cockpit, robot not stunned), a little faster at full thrust, with inertia (`ROTOR_RPM`, `TAIL_RPM`, `SPOOL_UP`, `SPOOL_DOWN`); the rotation shown per frame is capped (`STROBE_LIMIT`) so that the eye does not see them stop or turn backward.
- **Tracks:** rollers (`WheelTypeDef.track`) on a side, inside a belt drawn by `LocomotionView` (and by the garage): neighboring rollers on the same side (less than two cells apart along the side, one cell apart in height, or one on top of the other outward; `trackGroups`, shared/design/tracks.ts: clearance uses it too) form a single track, whose belt follows the convex hull of their centers (`beltShape`, `beltPath`: straight lines from one roller to the next, arcs around the corner ones, at the roller's radius plus `BELT_CLEARANCE`: the tip of the grousers, which bites 1 cm into the ground). **Rendering (render/models/tracks.ts, render/belts.ts):** all around, raised shoes (`trackShoe`: plate, grouser, hinge, steel connectors, one guide per row of rollers, which passes between its twin wheels), one thin instance per shoe every `SHOE_PITCH` (12 cm; `BeltShoes`, placed by `placeShoes`, render/trackShape.ts, from the normal of the path: they pivot in the arcs like links); under them, a thin belt (`beltMesh`), seen between the shoes and from the inside; the rollers are banded twin wheels (`TRACK_MODELS`) and the rear roller of each run (the highest one if they are stacked: `sprocketRollers`) carries a toothed sprocket (`sprocket`, which follows its pose). The belt (its texture) and the shoes scroll by however much the rollers have turned around their axle, read from their pose (the bottom moves back, the top moves forward): online too, since the replica does not have the wheels' rotation speed. Per frame, only the shoes' matrices are rewritten (one buffer per track, no mesh rebuilt). The shoes and the sprocket cast no shadow (the belt's has the same silhouette). **Levels of detail:** beyond `ROLLER_DETAIL_RANGE` (20 m) from the camera, the rollers switch to a simple model (band, flange, hub: `addLODLevel`, which Babylon also applies to instances and shadow maps); beyond `TRACK_DETAIL_RANGE` (35 m; `LocomotionView.shoeRange`, adjustable from the console), the track no longer has shoes or a sprocket, but the old thick belt (`beltMesh(…, false)`), and nothing to place. A Tank: 2 × 80 shoes of 144 vertices, a roller of 1,250 vertices (~150 from afar), a sprocket of 1,590. Measured in Chrome without a GPU: an ordinary battle (12 robots including the Tank and the Bastion) runs as before (3.2 vs. 3.3 fps following the Tank, 4.3 vs. 4.2 from afar); twelve tracked robots packed near the camera cost ~20% more in frames per second, and 0.8 ms of placement per frame for 24 tracks. The garage draws the same shoes and sprockets, motionless (`showBelts`). Taller (`rise`), only its bottom rollers touch the ground (the rays of the others do not reach it) and the robot sits that much higher; wider (`layer`), it has twice as many rollers, hence twice the motors and rated load. Differential steering. **Progressive damage:** each roller carries and pulls on its own; a roller torn off (or carried away by a part that breaks off) does not make the belt disappear: it is rebuilt on the remaining rollers of the same part (`buildBelts`, grouped by part then by `trackGroups`), and splits into two runs if the gap is too large.
- **Energy (`robot/energy.ts`):** thrusters, boosters and hover pads draw (`power`) on the batteries of the cockpit's part; the generator (`GENERATOR_POWER`) recharges more slowly than a flying robot consumes; with empty batteries, the engines only have the generator. A flying robot's AI lands to recharge (`landing`).
- **Damaged engines (`wearEngines`, robot/propulsion.ts, once per step and per robot):** below `ENGINE_WORN` (50%) of its hit points, an engine loses thrust, down to `WORN_OUTPUT` (60%) of its full thrust when spent, and misfires (it cuts out for 0.18 to 0.5 s, at random via `rand`, up to `MISFIRE_RATE` times per second when spent): `Thruster.output` (not transmitted: the replica sees its effect in the thrust) caps each thrust axis, and the hover pilot distributes according to what remains. At 45% when spent, planes fell too often (in battle, twice as many deaths by impact for the Plane and the Bomber). A spent engine that is hit can catch fire (see "Fire").
- **Loop (`client/game.ts`):** accumulator × `timeScale` (slow motion), at most 4 `sim.step()` per frame; `readInput` fills `sim.input` from the keyboard, the mouse, the touch controls and the camera. **Pose interpolation in solo (`PoseInterpolation`, render/interpolation.ts):** the simulation steps at 60 Hz, the screen draws at its own rate (120, 144 Hz…); without anything, a robot would only move on frames where a step falls (every other frame at 120 Hz, irregularly at 144 Hz). After each step, the position and orientation of everything that moves are recorded (parts, wheels and their hubs, debris, mobile props: two Rapier calls per body; projectile positions); while a frame is drawn, the entities carry poses interpolated between the last two steps (`ShownPose` in place of their body, at `alpha` = the share of the next step already elapsed; velocities and mass are read from the real body when asked, the center of mass is placed on the interpolated pose so the camera agrees with what it shows), then get their bodies back after `scene.render()`. The frame is one step (16.7 ms) behind, but everything glides: in slow motion (fewer steps than frames, like a 144 Hz screen), 0 frozen frames out of 147 for a moving robot, against 125 out of 185 without. Cost: ~0.14 ms per step, ~0.12 ms per frame (Depot battle). Rule: nothing may change the simulation while a frame is drawn (`begin` to `end`); what does (a new wave of the title demo, `simulate`) calls `poses.reset()` first. In multiplayer, the replica already interpolates (its `PoseBody`).
- **Mouse look (`client/input/controls.ts`, `input/cameraRig.ts`):** while piloting, a click in the arena captures the mouse (`requestPointerLock`, via `captureMouse`): the mouse turns the camera (`CameraRig.lookBy`, sensitivity and inverted axes in the settings), you aim at the crosshair (`Controls.aimPoint`; the aiming ray starts from the robot's plane: scenery between the camera and the robot does not count, `ArenaGame.aimAt`), and Esc releases the mouse and opens the pause menu (`onRelease`). Holding the right button (or toggling it: `Controls.scopeToggle`) goes to the scope (`PlayerInput.scope`): the camera glides into the selected weapon (just above its turret; with several turrets of the same weapon, in the middle of the highest ones, chosen in the robot's frame so that the choice does not change when it pitches: `ArenaGame.scopeEye`, `CameraRig.setEye`) and zooms in a little (more for the sniper); on entering, it turns toward the last point aimed at from behind (`CameraRig.lookFromEye`, with `ArenaGame.backAim`: the point of the switching frame is already taken at the center of the screen, before the camera turns, and does not count), and on leaving, it turns so that the crosshair from behind falls back on the point aimed at in the weapon (`CameraRig.lookFromBehind`, `scopeAim`): you aim at the same spot from behind and through the scope, and it looks around there as freely as from behind (`CameraRig.eyeBetaRange`): the turret follows up to its stop, as from behind (when limited to the turret's travel, measured against the horizon without accounting for the robot's tilt, it could hardly aim at the ground from a plane); from behind, the camera only looks up 30°, but, behind and below the robot, it aims higher than it looks: on leaving the scope, a view raised higher comes back down smoothly; the mouse wheel does nothing there; in the weapon, your own robot is no longer drawn (`WorldView.hidden`, `LocomotionView.hidden`), nor its muzzle flashes, nor its projectiles within `OWN_NEAR` m of the lens (fired from neighboring barrels, they blocked the view on one side then the other) and labels keep their size on screen; the spread of the player's shots is the weapon's best (see "Accuracy"), and a `scopeOnly` weapon (the sniper) only fires through the scope. While piloting, the camera aims above the top of the robot (`OVER_TOP`, from `Bot.top`): you see over it, even a tall robot; once the robot is destroyed, it keeps that height above the wreck (`ArenaGame.pilotLift`): lowered to ground level, it dove into the wreck when you looked up. It can go below the robot to aim at the sky (`PILOT_BETA_MAX`): its target rises and it moves closer so as not to go under the ground (`CameraRig.update`, zoom requested with the wheel in `zoom`). A piloted plane, as long as it flies, lifts this limit (`CameraRig.setFreePitch`, `freeLook`): the camera turns with no pitch stop, goes through the vertical and upside down (Babylon's `allowUpsideDown`, β wrapped by `betaWithin`), without raising its target (`LOOK_UP_RISE`): the crosshair follows the gaze, and the plane with it, all the way around a loop; `chaseDirection` brings it back behind a direction (the nose), by whichever of its two ways of looking at it is closer. Nor does it go behind scenery: a wall, a pillar, a ramp, a hill between its target and itself, and it moves closer to the robot (`CameraRig.setOcclusion`, with `rayArena`, shared/arena/ray.ts: a raycast on the scenery boxes and the terrain, without physics, which also works online); it comes in at once and goes back out smoothly (`held`). It does not go below the terrain, and the free camera does not go beyond the arena walls (`CameraRig.setArena`, through `ArenaGame.showArena`). The mouse is released as soon as you stop piloting (menu, end of match). With the mouse captured, its buttons are keys like any others (`Mouse0` to `Mouse4`; by default, left: fire, right: scope), read from the `buttons` mask of each event (`Controls.syncButtons`): a second button pressed or released while the other is held only emits a `pointermove`, not a `pointerdown` or a `pointerup`. Headless Chrome refuses the capture: the tests simulate it.
- **Crosshair and scope (`client/hud/reticle.ts`):** each weapon has its crosshair (SVG centered to the pixel on `Controls.aimPoint`: 45% of the height from behind, the exact center through the scope) and its magnifications (`SIGHTS`); through the scope, the edges darken (a round scope for the sniper and the railgun) and the mouse wheel switches from one magnification to another (sniper ×2.5, ×4, ×8). The player's shot subtracts its robot's velocity (the projectile inherits it): it reaches the aimed point even while driving. The crosshair traces the circle the shots will go into (`aimSpread` converted to pixels: it opens up when the robot moves) and, for a precision weapon that movement makes shake, invites you to stay still; the scope has optics (thick posts, fine cross, graduated marks). A gauge surrounds the crosshair (`Gauge`, from `readiness`, combat/arsenal.ts, which only reads the state of the weapons: it also works online): on the right, an arc fills from bottom to top until the next possible shot (white: rate of fire, at the shared rhythm of identical weapons; amber: a round is reloading, magazine empty; red: overheating; blue: batteries of an electric weapon), then fades in a flash; a rate of fire under 0.3 s is not shown. On the left, the magazine (one segment per round, the next one filling during its reload; a single bar beyond 12 rounds), or the heat of an automatic weapon (mark at `RESUME_HEAT`). **Instruments (`client/hud/instruments.ts`):** for a weapon that fires in an arc, a panel next to the crosshair gives the barrel's elevation and the one it is moving toward (with a gauge), the heading, the range and the flight time to the point of impact (or "Out of range": the shell ends its course in the air), and a diamond marks that point of impact on screen (`ArenaGame.gunnery`, `toScreen`); for bombs, a dotted circle on the ground, the size of the explosion, where the next one would fall, with its fall time (`ArenaGame.bombing`); for a flying robot, an artificial horizon (pitch, bank), the altitude, the speed, the vertical speed and the heading.
- **Recoil (`client/render/recoil.ts`):** the simulation pushes the robot opposite each shot (`Weapon.recoil`, applied at the muzzle), but a rifle only moves a robot by a few cm/s. So the client shows the kick, based on `Weapon.recoil` (nothing under 1 N·s: laser, plasma, flamethrower, mines, bombs): on `shot`, it finds the turret whose muzzle is at the shot's origin (`firingTurret`, also works online); its head moves back along the barrel then returns in 0.18 s (`WorldView.kicks`, from 2.5 cm for the rifle to 16 cm for the cannon), and, for a player's shot, the view jumps upward (a little sideways, at random) then returns to where the mouse had left it (`CameraRig.kick`, from 0.3° for the rifle to 2.5° for the cannon through the scope, half as much from behind). The aimed point follows the view: a burst climbs a little.
- **Hit feedback (`client/hud/hitFeedback.ts`):** the simulation emits `hit` (victim, shooter, weapon, destroyed blocks) for each robot hit by a weapon; the game turns it into a marker at the crosshair (stronger if blocks fall, and for a kill), an arc toward the shooter when the player's robot is hit, and interface sounds (`Sound.ui`). The scoreboard (Tab) shows the frames per second (the "Frames per second" graphics setting puts them in a corner).
- **Camera-relative steering** (the "Steering" setting, Controls tab, `Settings.drive`: `camera` by default, as in Robocraft, or `tank`, keyboard; `PlayerInput.relative`): on the ground, ZQSD give a direction on screen, the robot turns toward it, or reverses if it has its back to it (`cameraDrive`, sim/player.ts); a flying robot turns toward where the camera is looking. Keyboard: tank steering, and a flying robot only pivots with yaw. Settings saved before keep their value (`tank`, the old default).
- **Keys and gamepad (`client/input/bindings.ts`, `gamepad.ts`, `controls.ts`):** all commands go through an action table (`ACTIONS`: label, section of the key screen, context where it applies, default keys; pure logic, `tests/bindings.test.ts`); the player chooses their own (`Settings.bindings`: two per action on keyboard or mouse, the mouse wheel included for a one-shot action, one on the gamepad; read by `parseBindings`, which restores the default key for any missing or invalid slot: settings from before therefore have the keys from before). A key is designated by its physical position (`KeyboardEvent.code`: ZQSD on AZERTY, WASD on QWERTY), and named according to the player's keyboard (`codeLabel`: `navigator.keyboard.getLayoutMap()`, otherwise learned while playing, `Controls.learn`; digits remain digits). Each action applies in a context (`InputContext`: always, while piloting, on the ground, in flight, in free camera, in the garage; `Controls.contextOn`; the garage only overlaps itself): two actions that never apply together share a key (Space: brake on the ground, climb or pull up in flight; E: mines on the ground, yaw in flight; F: flares while piloting, next robot in free camera; R: new wave in free camera, rotate in the garage; Page Down and Page Up: next and previous music track in a match, layer up and down in the garage). The music actions (`musicNext`, `musicPrevious`: Interface section, `any` context, Page Down and Page Up, plus the `.` and `,` keys of a QWERTY keyboard for keyboards without page keys; no gamepad button) skip tracks in a match; in the menus and the garage, the "now playing" buttons do it (see "Sound", the music player). Assigning a key already taken in the same context (`assignBinding`) removes it from the other action, which receives in exchange the old key of the action being set if that creates no other conflict (otherwise its slot stays empty): the screen says so under the row (`displaced`). Esc (pause, cancel) and the gamepad's Menu button are reserved; pause and the sticks are fixed. **Combinations** (in the garage only: `chordCode`, `isChord`): modifier keys (`MODIFIERS`: Ctrl, Shift, Alt) then a key or a button ("Control+KeyZ", "Shift+Mouse0"); with Ctrl, a letter is read from the typed character (`eventChords`: Ctrl+Z undoes on AZERTY as on QWERTY), the rest from its position. `Controls` only reads the table: `holding(id)` for an action you hold (forward, boost, brake, fire…), `trigger` for a one-shot action (at the moment of the press, `press`); a game key does nothing else in the page (`preventDefault`). **No text writes out a key:** help texts, HUD reminders, part sheets (the flare launcher), robot traits (the Dragster), feed lines (the flight mode), the garage and the key screen cite a key through a parameter (`{key}`) filled in from the table (`keyLabel(action)`, which `Controls` has named by `keyName`: `setKeyNamer`; outside the browser, the default keys in QWERTY), and are redone when the keys or the language change (`ArenaGame.refreshKeyHelp`: the controls table, `keys.ts`; the weapon keys, `Hud.weaponKey`; the HUD reminders, `[data-key-action]` in index.html; "F: flares", mines, scope; `Garage.refreshKeys`); on the gamepad, its buttons (`Controls.keyName`). `bindingName` picks, of an action's two keys, the one that types the letter of its default key on QWERTY: the map and symmetry (M) have the position of the M on a QWERTY keyboard and that of the M on an AZERTY one, and the help says "M" on both keyboards. `tests/keyMessages.test.ts` rejects a key name hard-coded in the message catalogs, and checks that made-up keys appear in the part sheets, the traits and the help texts, in French and in English. **Gamepad** (Gamepad API, standard layout; `GamepadInput`, polled each frame by `Controls.poll`, connection and disconnection detected when polling, indicator at the top left: `PadBadge`): left stick, movement (analog), right stick, camera (`CameraRig.turn`; sensitivity, inverted axes, settings separate from the mouse), adjustable radial dead zone (`stickDeadzone`), RT and LT triggers: fire and scope, then buttons (A: brake on the ground, climb in flight, pull up in a plane; B: descend, push down; X: flares; Y: mines; LB, RB: previous, next weapon; L3: boost, full throttle; R3: cut the throttle; D-pad: map, flight mode, yaw; View: scoreboard; Menu: pause; in a plane, the left stick sets the throttle and turns, the right stick steers it); it aims at the crosshair (`Controls.reticleAim`); behind a menu, it navigates it (`Menu.navigate`: the neighboring control in that direction, or the focused control receives the keyboard arrow: robot list, tabs, sliders; A confirms, B and Menu go back, LB and RB switch tabs; `body.pad-nav` makes the focus visible). Keys screen (`App.showBindings`: a screen of its own, opened from the Controls tab, "Choose your keys"; it used to fill that tab; Back, Esc or B return to the tab, and abandon a reassignment in progress): one row per action, by section (on the ground, in flight, weapons, interface, free camera, garage, without gamepad), two keyboard-or-mouse slots and one gamepad slot (`SettingRow` `binding`); click a slot, then the key, the mouse button or the gamepad button (`App.listen`: listens in the capture phase, before everything else, and swallows the press and its release; for a garage action, the combination, a modifier key released on its own counting for itself; Esc or Menu cancel; right-click clears); ↺ resets an action's keys (`resetAction`), "Reset all" resets all of them. **Migration:** the table has a version (`BINDINGS_VERSION`, 2; that of the saved settings: `Settings.bindingsVersion`, absent: 1); `migrateBindings` gives the new default keys to the slots (keyboard, gamepad) of an action that the player had left at the old ones (`FORMER_DEFAULTS`), keeps those they had changed, and leaves empty a slot whose new key already belongs to one of their actions in the same context. Version 2: Ctrl no longer descends (in flight, Ctrl held with the throttle key made Ctrl+W, which closes the tab). The new actions (those of the garage) take their default keys.
- **Touch controls (`client/input/touchPad.ts`, `touchLook.ts`):** as soon as you pilot with a finger (`pointer: coarse`, or a first touch), a stick on the left and buttons on the right (fire, scope, climb, descend, turbo); you aim at the crosshair, in the center of the screen (`Controls.aimPoint`). **Swiping turns the camera** (`TouchLook`, input/touchLook.ts, pure logic tested by `tests/touchLook.test.ts`): every finger on the game screen is followed by its `pointerId`, and only one turns the camera, the first one down; the others are followed without turning anything (their position kept up to date), and when it lifts, the oldest remaining finger takes over where it is, without a jump. Before, the swipe was tied to no finger: a second finger landing on the screen (next to a button, to aim while firing) replaced the first, then the moves of both fingers alternated and each gap between their two positions became a rotation (several radians per event): the camera, and with camera steering the robot that follows it, spun around. While piloting, the swipe aims as in a mobile shooter (finger to the right, the view turns right; up, it looks up: `swipeAngles`, `TOUCH_YAW` 0.0036 and `TOUCH_PITCH` 0.0029 rad per CSS pixel at sensitivity 1, about 85° for half a phone held sideways; it used the free camera's "grab the scene" orbit, twice as fast and reversed horizontally), through `CameraRig.turn` (slower through the scope, in proportion to the zoom); in free camera, it still grabs the scene (`orbit`), and a short tap fires. A finger turns nothing below `DRAG_THRESHOLD` (8 px, a tap); a single event moves it by `MAX_STEP` (240 px) at most; the swipe reaches the camera at each frame through a light smoothing (`LOOK_SMOOTHING`, 16 ms: two thirds at the first frame at 60 Hz), which loses nothing (`take`) and is dropped in pause or behind a menu. The mouse (right-button drag outside capture) and the gamepad don't go through it. **Aim stick** ("Touch" tab: swipe or stick, `Settings.touchAim`): a second stick on the right turns the camera at a speed, like the gamepad's right stick (`stickRates`: radial dead zone `STICK_DEADZONE`, the remaining travel to the power `STICK_CURVE`, `STICK_YAW` 2.6 and `STICK_PITCH` 1.7 rad/s fully pushed, its own sensitivity); released, it returns to the center and the camera stops. With it, swiping elsewhere on the screen still turns the camera: the stick for big turns, the swipe for fine corrections, and a thumb that just misses the stick isn't lost (each stick can also be touched in a margin around it, `.tp-stick::before`). **The Fire button can be dragged**: the finger on it is one of the `TouchLook` fingers, so you aim while firing, in both modes. The scope button toggles by default (`Settings.touchScopeToggle`: holding it would block the aiming thumb). Layout (style.css, `.touchpad`): sizes relative to the screen (`--stick`, `--btn`: at most a share of its width and height, so a small phone tightens them), times the "Size" setting (`--tp-scale`, 70 to 140%), and an "Opacity" setting (`--tp-alpha`; a held button stays opaque); with the aim stick (`.aim-stick`), sideways, the stick in the corner, Fire on its left, the scope above it, turbo, climb and descend farther from the thumb, and the weapon bar moves toward the left stick; upright, on two columns, Fire above the aim stick; left-handed (`.lefty`), everything mirrored (`row-reverse`, the buttons' grid in `direction: rtl`). Between the buttons, a finger reaches the game screen (it turns the camera). Measured in headless Chrome emulating a phone held sideways (844 × 390, real multi-finger gestures through CDP `Input.dispatchTouchEvent`, the Tank in the Depot): a 200 px swipe to the right turns the view by 0.72 rad to the right (before: 1.2 rad to the left); a second finger landing 320 px away during a swipe and staying still changes nothing (before: a jump of 2.16 rad at once), and when both fingers move in turn, only the first one counts (before: ±1.9 rad at each event); the aim stick pushed fully to the right turns the view at 2.60 rad/s and leaves it in place once released; a 100 px drag from Fire turns it by 0.36 rad while firing. **Playing on a phone (`client/hud/touchHud.ts`, `TouchHud`, created by `App`):** the page gets the `touch-ui` class (a coarse pointer, or a first touch); in a match, a **pause** button (there is no Esc key on a phone; it does what Esc does, `App.pause`); full screen is not on the game screen but in the pause menu and on the title screen, which have a "Full screen" entry wherever the browser allows it (see "Screens"; by touch, it also turns the screen sideways where it can). A phone held upright in a match is invited to turn sideways (`.rotate-hint`, CSS only: `orientation: portrait`, fades after a few seconds, does not block the game). While playing, the page neither zooms on a pinch nor scrolls nor selects text on a long press (`touch-action: none` on the body outside the menus, `overscroll-behavior`, Safari's `gesturestart`); buttons have `touch-action: manipulation` (no double-tap zoom). The HUD tightens around the stick and the buttons (style.css, `body.touch-pilot`): upright, the weapon bar and the robot status sit above the stick and the kill feed is hidden; sideways (a screen less than 560 px high), the minimap shrinks to 116 px above the buttons, the stick and buttons shrink (with the screen's height), the gunnery panel keeps only its gauge, the flight panel moves under the pause button; the buttons under the minimap don't name keys; the music note of a new track goes on the left, under the pause button (and under the score, upright), which it used to hide. The garage and the map editor are made for a mouse: by touch, a note says what works (`TouchHud.note`, shown on entering them through `App.enter`, closed by the player for the visit): in the garage a tap places the chosen part, dragging turns the view and pinching zooms, but removing or copying a part and the shortcuts need a mouse and keyboard; in the editor you can look at a map, try it and leave. Their "Back" and "Quit" buttons work by touch. The engine never renders more than two pixels per CSS pixel (`applyRenderScale`, game.ts: a phone often has three).
- **HUD (`client/hud/hud.ts`, `matchHud.ts`, `minimap.ts`):** on screen, only the essentials: at the top the score, the time and the zones (`MatchHud`), the kill feed below; at the bottom (`.bottom-dock`) the weapon bar (one key per weapon, ammo or heat; in free camera, the whole catalog as small buttons) then the status of the piloted robot; at the top right the minimap; in a plane piloted with the mouse, a nose marker (`#noseMark`, `ArenaGame.showNoseMark`, `NOSE_MARK_RANGE` m ahead of it) shows where it is going, the crosshair where it will go. The rest is in the **scoreboard** (`Hud.pressTab`/`releaseTab`: holding Tab shows it for as long as it is held, a single press pins it until the next one; by touch, the button under the minimap): the robots of both teams (status, locomotion, structure, and kills and deaths of each slot, the best at the top: a slot passes to the robot that respawns in the same team with the same robot, based on `botAdded`/`botRemoved`, which also works online; reset at each new match), "you" and "player" for the humans, the statistics, the Free Arena tools and the controls (`keys.ts`). **Minimap (`MiniMap`):** north (+z) at the top, west (+x) on the left, like the 3D view; its background (ground, terrain shaded with the colors of `ArenaView.groundColor`, water, paths, static scenery) is computed once per arena (`setArena`), buildings, roofs, tanks and standing trees are on a separate layer, redrawn when one of them falls (based on `propAdded`/`propRemoved`/`propReleased` and the spots), then, ten times a second, the zones (in their team's color, only the hill in play), the robots (an arrow in the team's color, hollow if it flies; only spotted enemies while piloting) and the camera's view cone. In a large arena, it shows the player's surroundings (`MINI_RANGE`, 60 m); M opens the whole map, with the named places (`ArenaLayout.places`). Pause and a new match close the scoreboard and the map (`ArenaGame.closeOverlays`).
- **Shadows (`render/scene.ts`):** cascaded (`CascadedShadowGenerator`, if WebGL 2 is available): several shadow maps along the camera's field of view, fine up close, coarse in the distance; three cascades up to 240 m in a large arena, two up to 110 m in a small one (`applyArenaLook`). A single map framed on the whole Valley was blurry; framed around the robot, it left everything seen in the distance without a shadow. Without WebGL 2, a single map that Babylon frames on everything that casts a shadow (sun 250 m away along the axis of its rays). **Each cascade only draws what can cast a shadow into it** (`cullCascades`): the meshes whose bounding sphere touches its frame seen from the sun (an object outside that frame cannot shadow it: a shadow falls along the sun's direction); Babylon drew every caster in every cascade. The sorting is put back on each new shadow map (Babylon remakes it when the cascade count or its size change). **Static scenery drawn in few passes:** in game (not in the map editor, which picks boxes one by one), the arena boxes that share a material are merged into one mesh (`ArenaView.mergeBoxes`: the City's 86 edge buildings were 258 shadow passes per frame), and the anchored props that don't move are in thin-instance batches per source (`PropBatches`, render/propBatches.ts, used by WorldView): a prop that changes (it sways, falls, leaks, is destroyed) leaves its batch for an instance of its own (`ownMesh`), a tree that has finished swaying goes back (`rejoinLot`), a prop that gets charred moves to the batch of its new source; a source with several materials (a tree, a car, a glTF model) gives its batch its parts (`SubMesh`: the copied geometry only had one, and the batched trees were drawn all in bark), and a batch whose source takes another geometry (its glTF model arrives, see "3D models of props") rebuilds itself on it (`reshape`). Measured in the City (`npm run perf`, reference battle): shadow passes per frame 857 → 281, draw calls 1,020 → 475, Babylon preparing a frame 16.5 → 12.1 ms, GPU 15 → 11.7 ms; at the Depot, little static scenery: draw calls −30%, −5%.
- **Loading screen (`client/menu/loading.ts`, `LoadingScreen`, markup `#loading` in index.html):** opaque, above everything (the scene being prepared behind it cannot be seen), a progress bar and the current step. **At startup** (`App.boot`, launched by main.ts): physics, the scene, then, in parallel, all sounds decoded and the title-screen music prepared (`Sound.preload`) and the warm-up of the effects (`ArenaGame.prewarm`); the title screen and its demo wait behind; finally, if the browser waits for a gesture before playing sound (`Sound.needsGesture`), "Click to start" (a click, Enter or Space), and the title-screen music, already prepared, starts with that gesture (it was waiting for the audio context to resume). **Before each match** (`App.play`: local, "Restart", garage trial; and the online match, `connect`: the replica first receives the world, `waitForOnlineWorld`): the arena and the robots are built, the match stays frozen (`ArenaGame.hold`: no simulation, no commands; online, the replica keeps receiving), what it will show and its music are prepared (`prepareMatch`), then it begins (`beginMatch`: `release`, music, mouse). A loading is abandoned by a return to the menu or a dropped connection (`loadTicket`, `cancelLoading`). **Warm-up (`ArenaGame.prewarm`):** one instance of each projectile, mine and indicator light, tracer (`ProjectileView.warmUp`), block fire, engine and turbojet flame, prop fragment of the arena (`WorldView.warmUp`), smoke cloud (`SmokeView.warmUp`), energy wall of each team (`BarrierView.warmUp`), explosion of each look, muzzle flash of each weapon and small effects (`Effects.warmUp`), particle, streak and mark of each kind (`Fx.warmUp`) is placed in front of the camera, a few frames are drawn, we wait for the scene to be ready (`whenReadyAsync`, at most `READY_WAIT` s), then everything is removed (`Effects.clear`, `Fx.clear`). Without this, the first shot of each weapon, the first explosion, the first effect built their model and compiled their shaders in the middle of the match: measured with a GPU, the first shot of each of the 23 weapons caused 7 frames of more than 50 ms (worst 125 ms); afterward, like a second pass. **No top-level `await` on startup in main.ts** (`start()` is launched without being awaited): in production, the shader code chunks, loaded on demand, import the main module, and would wait for it to finish evaluating while it waited for the shaders (the loading screen never finished).
- **Screens (`client/app.ts`):** main menu (Play, Multiplayer, Garage, Map editor, Settings, Full screen; a demo battle behind it, `startDemo`), choice of mode, then of the arena (see "Arena picker"), then of the robot, match (`startMode(id, pilot)`), pause (`frozen`), end of match, garage (the arena is on standby: `suspended`). **Full screen (`client/hud/fullscreen.ts`, `App.fullscreenEntry`):** the main menu and the pause menu have a "Full screen" entry wherever the browser allows it (`fullscreenSupported`: a computer, Android, an iPad; not the iPhone, whose Safari only puts videos in full screen), by touch or not; it is no longer a button of the game screen. Its label says what it will do ("Full screen", "Exit full screen"), and the menu is redrawn when the page enters or leaves full screen, through the menu or through the browser (Esc, the back gesture: `onFullscreenChange`). By touch, entering it also turns the screen sideways where it can (`screen.orientation.lock`: Chrome on Android, in full screen only), and the entry has its one-line explanation; on a computer it has none, and the main menu's entries are a little tighter on a screen 780 px high or less: its six entries fit in 1280 × 720 without scrolling. A screen that has a `back` returns to it with Esc (`Menu.goBack`). A screen can carry content built elsewhere (`MenuView.content`, in the widest panel, which places the focus itself) and release it when it goes away (`onClose`, called when the screen is replaced or closed). A menu panel is centered when it fits on the screen, and starts at the top when it doesn't (`margin-block: auto`: centered by the box, its top went out of reach of the scroll on a phone held sideways). **Tiles (`MenuView.tiles`):** the screens seen most often (main menu, choice of mode, pause, end of match) lay their entries out as tiles across the width on a small screen (style.css: narrower than 760 px or lower than 560 px): two columns upright, the main entry on a whole row; as many columns of 160 px as fit sideways (four on a phone), the main entry on two; each entry's explanation on two lines at most, a smaller title, no footer sideways. The main menu then fits without scrolling (390 × 844 upright, 844 × 390 sideways); on a computer, nothing changes. The gamepad and the keyboard move through them as before (`Menu.navigate` goes to the nearest control in a direction, in two dimensions). On a small screen, the settings and keys screens keep their entries (Back, defaults) at the bottom of the screen while their rows scroll (`position: sticky`).
- **Robot picker (`client/menu/robotPicker.ts`, `App.showPicker`):** the same screen everywhere a robot is chosen (match, "Change robot", multiplayer, and "My robots" in the garage, where you also open an unfinished robot or one still to unlock). Filters by locomotion (wheels, tracks, legs, pads, flyers, planes, and "My garage", each with its number of robots, only those that have any), a search (name, description, weapons, locomotion, accent-insensitive) and a sort (game order, cost, name), kept for the duration of the visit; a **grid of cards** that uses the width of the screen (`CardGrid`, menu/cardGrid.ts, shared with the arena picker: as many columns as fit; one card per robot: thumbnail with its cost and its tags, name, locomotion, weapons; keyboard: the arrows move in two dimensions (`gridMove`, menu/gridNav.ts, pure and tested by tests/gridNav.test.ts: left and right follow the reading order, down from a short last row goes to its last card; the number of columns is read from the page, `columnsOf`), Home, End, Page Up/Down, Enter; the gamepad sends the same keys (`Menu.navigate`), and at the edge of the grid it passes to the neighboring control; mouse: click to select, double-click to choose; by touch: a tap selects, a second tap on the selected card chooses); the selected robot's sheet: a rotating 3D preview (you turn it with the mouse), description, cost against the budget, mass, locomotion, speed, weapons (×n for several turrets of the same one), parts to unlock, and the button that picks it. The robot chosen last time is selected from the start and marked (`lastPick`, key `casse-robot.lastRobot`). Everything is derived from the design (`robotCard`: `locomotionOf`, `weaponsOf`, `designCost`, `designMass`): a new preset robot or a new weapon finds its place there on its own. **Thumbnails (`render/robotSketch.ts`):** drawn in 2D (canvas, orthographic projection from the front right, shaded faces, faces hidden by a solid neighbor omitted, wheels, track belts (`beltPath`: the facets turned toward the eye, drawn right after the rollers), turret heads and barrels, and what sticks out of the blocks, in the place its clearance reserves for it (`protrusions`: translucent blade disc, nacelle, leg); colors `blockColors`, `wheelColors` from materials.ts), a few milliseconds for the whole list. **Preview (`render/robotPreview.ts`):** its own Babylon engine on its own canvas (the game continues behind it on its own), created at the first robot shown, released with its WebGL context (`loseContextOnDispose`) when leaving the screen; the blocks and what sticks out of them (`stillBlockParts`, render/stillParts.ts: turret heads, blades, nacelles, legs, hover pad glow), the wheels and their fittings (`stillWheelParts`) and the tracks (`stillBelts`, render/belts.ts: belt, shoes and sprocket, motionless), the same functions as in the garage, framed on their bounding sphere (`FRAMING`), and further away if the bounding box of their clearance (`designZones`) would leave the image at one of the angles the preview turns through (`fitDistance`: a 2 m propeller, legs). **Layout (style.css, `.picker-*`):** on a large screen (wider than 760 px and taller than 560 px), the panel takes the whole height: the grid and the sheet beside it each scroll on their own (the button that chooses stays at the bottom of the sheet), the title, the filters and "Back" stay in view; on a low screen (a phone held sideways), the page scrolls and the sheet beside the grid stays in view, without the stats; on a phone held upright, the sheet, compact (the preview on the left, the name and weapons on the right, the button below), stays at the top while the page scrolls, the grid in two columns below. A selected card is brought into view by scrolling the grid alone when it scrolls (`CardGrid.reveal`), the page only when moving with the keyboard or the gamepad: on opening, the title and the filters stay in view. Measured: 6 columns at 1920 × 1080, 4 at 1280 × 720, 2 on a phone 390 px wide, no horizontal scroll from 360 px up.
- **Arena picker (`client/menu/arenaPicker.ts`, `App.pickArena`):** after the mode, the same grid of cards (`CardGrid`): the game's arenas, then the community maps (`MapLibrary.list`), then "Random" (a die, drawn in SVG), with "Community maps…" and "Back" under the grid. Each card: the arena's top-down plan, its name, its sentence (`arenaInfo`; a community map: its author and description) and its tags (last chosen, community map, night). The sheet of the selected one: its plan in large, its full description (a community map: author, license, description), a few facts (small arena or large map, size, number of capture zones, day or night: read from the game arena's file once loaded, or from the community `MapFile`, without building the arena) and the button that chooses it (a community map then becomes playable, `MapLibrary.arena`). The last arena chosen is selected and marked (`lastArena`, key `casse-robot.lastArena`), otherwise the one on screen. **Plans (`menu/arenaPreview.ts`):** the minimap's drawing (`hud/minimap.ts`: `paintTerrainRows` for the terrain, `drawFlatGround`, `drawBoxes`, and `standingOf`/`paintStanding` for the buildings, roofs, tanks and trees standing at the start, read from `ArenaLayout.props`), plus the spawn lines in the team colors and the capture zones with their letters, darkened at night (`drawArenaPlan`, a 400 px square; a card shows its middle at 5:4, the sheet all of it). Drawn once per arena and kept for the visit (`PlanQueue`: one after the other, the selected arena first, `hurry`; leaving the screen drops the rest). The terrain, colored pixel by pixel by `groundColor`, is the expensive part: it is computed at half resolution (200 px, then enlarged) in slices of 8 ms between two frames; measured (busy time, a loaded machine): small arenas 1 to 4 ms, Valley 126 ms, City 198 ms, Canyon 213 ms, Volcanic Island 330 ms (at 400 px and in one go: 0.6 to 2.8 s of frozen menu). A large game arena waits for its file (the picker starts the six downloads when it opens), then is built in one go (8 to 52 ms). Measured (headless Chrome, a loaded machine, served locally): the selected arena's plan in ~0.35 s, the twelve cards' plans in ~6 s.
- **Settings (`App.showSettings`, `client/settings.ts`):** Graphics (the renderer first: see "Technical choices already made"), Controls (mouse, steering on the ground and when hovering, plane piloting, gamepad, then the entry to the keys screen and to the summary of all controls: see "Keys and gamepad"), Touch, Sound and Profile tabs (the language first: see "Languages (i18n)"; "Default settings" keeps it, like the name and the server). The tabs have identifiers (`SettingsTab`; the last one opened is remembered). **Touch tab** (`App.touchRows`; only when playing by touch, `TouchHud.active`, and then first and opened first, with Controls last: keyboard, mouse and gamepad stay reachable): aiming by swiping or with the aim stick (`touchAim`), the swipe's sensitivity (`touchSensitivity`) and, with the stick, its own (`touchStickSensitivity`), inverted vertical axis (`touchInvertY`), scope button toggled or held (`touchScopeToggle`, toggled by default), size (`touchSize`, 70 to 140%) and opacity (`touchOpacity`, 30 to 100%) of the on-screen controls, left-handed layout (`touchLeftHanded`), and steering (`drive`, the same setting as in Controls, explained for the stick); applied by `Controls.setTouch` (see "Touch controls"). Settings saved before keep their values and take these defaults. **Sound tab:** sound, master volume, played sounds, music volume, the **music player** (`musicPlayer`: Shown, Shrinks, Hidden, see "Music player"; applied by `NowPlaying.setMode`), then the track playing and the previous one, with their buttons. The music player's default depends on the machine (`defaultMusicPlayer`: it shrinks by touch, a coarse pointer, and stays shown on a computer), also for settings saved before it existed (`parseSettings` takes the machine, `deviceInfo`, as its second argument: a phone that already had settings gets the pill). One row per setting (`SettingRow`: toggle, choice, slider, text, button, information, keys of an action). Each change is saved then applied by `applySettings`, which passes all the settings to the game (methods with no effect if nothing changes: `setShadowQuality`, `setRenderScale`, `setReducedEffects`, `setFxLook`, `setGlow`, `setDynamicLights`, `setHeatHaze`, `CameraRig.setFov`, `Sound.setVolume`, `FpsBadge`); the graphics presets also set the smoke and flames, glow, light flashes and heat haze (low: simple effects, nothing else; medium, the default: realistic effects, glow and light flashes; high: realistic effects and all three; settings from before this setting switch to realistic effects, except those on the Low preset, `parseSettings`); the screen redraws and the focus stays on the control. **First launch** (nothing saved, `loadSettings`), and "Default settings": the defaults for this machine (`defaultSettings`): the graphics preset of `firstRunPreset` (pure, tested in tests/settings.test.ts), the lightest on a phone (a coarse pointer and a screen whose smaller side is at most 600 px), on a modest tablet (4 GB of memory or 4 cores at most, `navigator.deviceMemory`, `hardwareConcurrency`) and on a very small machine (2 GB or 2 cores), medium elsewhere; and the music player of `defaultMusicPlayer`. A new setting: a field in `Settings` (with its default value and its validation in `parseSettings`), a row in the tab, its application in `applySettings`.
- **Ramps (`arena/layout.ts`):** inclined slabs thickened downward, whose underside goes below the ground (no gap where a robot could get stuck).
- **Robot frame:** forward is local +z, left is +x (steering > 0: to the left). Babylon uses a right-handed frame to match Rapier.

## Architecture rules

- `src/shared` never imports Babylon, the DOM or `src/client`. This is checked by `src/shared/tsconfig.json` (no DOM lib) and `tests/architecture.test.ts`. Whatever depends on the browser (the `performance` clock, storage) is provided by the client.
- **No displayed text in `src/shared` or in `src/server`** (see "Languages (i18n)"): a catalog only has identifiers, and what the shared code or the server tells the player is a code with its parameters (`DesignIssue`, `ConflictReason`, `RewardLine`, `Refusal`), which the client translates (client/i18n/names.ts). A new server message: one more code in its type (protocol.ts, `PROTOCOL_VERSION` +1), its text in each language, and its translation in names.ts. Messages for developers (console, errors, server logs) stay in French.
- **Displayed text (recipe):** never a hard-coded string in the client code: a key in the file of its zone, in French (`client/i18n/locales/fr/<zone>.ts`, the source language: the text exactly as it should be displayed, parameters in braces, plurals as `{ one, other }` chosen by `n`) and in every other language (same file, same keys, same parameters: TypeScript checks it), then `t("zone.clé", { … })` where it is displayed; a number goes through `num`, `fixed`, `pct` or `measure` (format.ts, i18n.ts), never `.replace(".", ",")`; a text in index.html, through `data-i18n` (or `data-i18n-title`…). Whatever is built once (a button, a texture) and must follow a language change subscribes to `onLocaleChange` (i18n/state.ts). A string that looks like French without being displayed text (an internal key) is marked `// i18n-ignore`: `tests/i18nSource.test.ts` rejects the rest.
- **Language (recipe for a translator):** copy `client/i18n/locales/en/` to `locales/<code>/` (ISO 639-1 code: `de`, `es`…), translate the values (never the keys or the `{paramètres}` (the parameters); the plural forms the language requires: `zero`, `one`, `two`, `few`, `many`, `other`, following the CLDR rules of `Intl.PluralRules`), rename the exports if needed and check each file against French (`satisfies ZoneOf<typeof fr>`); add the code to `LOCALE_IDS` (i18n/state.ts) and the language to `LOCALES` (locales/index.ts: its name in its own language, `complete(…, true)` for a complete language; without `complete`, a partial language is displayed in English where it is missing); `npm run check`. The units (`unit.*` of `common`) adapt too (symbols, space before the symbol). The language then shows up on its own in the settings, and `detectLocale` picks it for a browser that speaks it.
- `erasableSyntaxOnly` is enabled: no `enum`, no `namespace`, no constructor parameter properties (so that Node can run the server's TypeScript without compiling it).
- Game data lives in typed catalogs, and each entry has its client-side look in an exhaustive `Record`. TypeScript therefore rejects an incomplete addition. The blocks (but the shaped ones), the wheels, the weapons, the melee weapons and the props are element files (`content/<family>/<id>.json`, see "Element files"): tuning one is editing its file, and `npm run check` says which file and which field is wrong:
  - **block type:** its file `content/blocks/<type>.json` (with `cost`, `bond`, `resist` and its unlock `price`; `$schema` for your editor) and one import line in `shared/catalog/blocks.ts` (`FILES`: its name makes the `BlockType`; a light: `light`, its model in `LIGHT_MODELS` and its lens in `LIGHT_LENSES`, render/models/lights.ts, its info-card sentence in `LIGHT_SUMMARIES`, garage/partInfo.ts; a new color, `LIGHT_COLORS`, a new pattern, `lampLevels` and `LAMP_INTENSITY`, render/lightSources.ts; see "Night") + `BLOCK_LOOKS` (`client/render/materials.ts`) + its name in each language (`block.<type>`, i18n/locales/<language>/catalog.ts) and its info card in the garage (its sentence in `parts.ts`, based on what it is: garage/partInfo.ts). A thruster is a block with a `thruster` field (tilt rotor: `gimbal`; flame engine: its jet, `exhaust`), a support a block with `support`, etc. The garage palette builds itself. **3D model (optional):** without code, a glTF file (`public/models/blocks/<type>.glb`, a turret's head in `heads/`, then `npm run models`: see "3D models of props, blocks, turret heads and wheels" and [`models.md`](models.md)) takes precedence over everything below; with code, a block can have its own instead of the textured cube (`BLOCK_MODELS`, render/blockModels.ts, which gathers one file per family in render/models/: propulsion.ts, weaponsDirect.ts, weaponsGuided.ts), and a turret its head (`HEAD_MODELS`); a model is made of vertex-painted primitives (render/modelKit.ts: `box`, `cylinder`, `ring`, `sphere`, `fuse`), merged into one mesh with a single material (thin-instance batches only have one; each instance's wear tint multiplies their colors), in the block's frame (±0.25 m, up +y, forward +z); collisions remain the cube, and in the garage it is targeted through an invisible box of its cell (`Assets.pickBoxSource`). The propulsion parts all have their own (thruster, booster, rotor, propeller engine, turbojet cage, hover pad, leg hip); guided weapons, mines, drop weapons and support: `GUIDED_HEADS` and `GUIDED_BASES` (render/models/weaponsGuided.ts); a mine layer has a symmetrical head (a drum), because it turns half a turn in game but not in the garage, and its chute, facing backward, is in the base; the muzzle of each head falls `barrel` m in front of its axis. A block with a model has no per-instance color (`registerInstancedBuffer`): Babylon does not blend it with vertex colors on regular instances (garage, previews); wheels too (`WHEEL_MODELS`, with its type `WheelModel`: axle along x, symmetrical; render/models/wheels.ts: tire with chevron lugs, rounded sidewalls, spoked rim, nuts, hub cap, orange rim for a steered wheel, deep lugs and a bead ring for a heavy one, smooth tire for a caster; the track rollers in render/models/tracks.ts); **visible ammunition**: a head can show its rounds separately (`HEAD_ROUNDS`, `Rounds`: a round and its slots in the head, in firing order; missiles, anti-air, rockets, lock-on missiles, warhead, bombs, smoke canisters), placed under the head by `Assets.addRounds`; in game, `WorldView.syncRounds` only shows the share of the magazine that remains (`visibleRounds`, render/rounds.ts: at least one as long as a round remains, the first slots go first, based on `Turret.arm.ammo`, which also goes over the network), and a reloaded round slides back into its slot (`ROUND_RETURN`); the head has a dark muzzle on the front face of each tube (the model kit's tubes are solid); then `npm run schemas` (the robot schema lists the block types);
  - **block shape:** an entry in `SHAPE_SPECS` (`shared/catalog/shapes.ts`: half-spaces that cut the cube, one or two convex pieces, and its mirror symmetry, `swap`), then block types `shaped(matière, forme)` in `SHAPED` (catalog/blocks.ts; mass, hit points and cost in proportion to volume; their unlock price in `SHAPED_PRICES`; their name in each language: `block.<type>`); collider, rendering and mass follow from it; its inventory subgroup (`SHAPE_GROUPS`, garage/partInfo.ts) and its info-card sentence (parts.ts); then `npm run schemas`;
  - **wheel type:** its file `content/wheels/<type>.json` (its definition and its unlock `price`; `$schema` for your editor: see "Element files") and one import line in `shared/catalog/wheels.ts` (`FILES`: its name makes the `WheelType`), + `WHEEL_LOOKS` (`client/render/materials.ts`) + its name in each language (`wheel.<type>`). Four kinds (`wheelKind`): fixed, steered (a `steering` field), caster (`caster`), track roller (`track`); the garage palette sorts them by kind; then `npm run schemas` (the robot schema lists the wheel types). Its model: `WHEEL_MODELS` (code), or without code a glTF file (`public/models/wheels/<type>.glb`, then `npm run models`; not for a track roller: [`models.md`](models.md));
  - **weapon:** its file `content/weapons/<id>.json` (with `engage`, its preferred range for the AI, and `handling`, how it handles; `$schema` for your editor) and one import line in `shared/catalog/weapons.ts` (`FILES`, in the order of the weapon keys: its name makes the `WeaponId`) + `MUZZLE_FLASH` (`client/render/effects.ts`) + `SHOT_TONES` and `SHOT_SOUNDS` (`client/audio/sound.ts`: fallback synthesized sound, and recorded sound, a sound from `SAMPLES`) + `SIGHTS` (`client/hud/reticle.ts`: crosshair, scope zoom levels, scope lens or darkened edges) + its name in each language (`weapon.<id>`, catalog.ts; the kill cause in the feed follows from it); the projectile's look follows `projectile.look` (a new look: its light in `PROJECTILE_LIGHTS`, client/render/lightSources.ts), and that of its explosion `explosion.look` (effect in `EXPLOSION_STYLES` and, with realistic effects, in `FIREBALLS` (render/fx/blastFx.ts), noise in `EXPLOSION_SOUNDS`, ground marks and embers in `CRATERS` (render/fx/blastFx.ts), light in `BLAST_LIGHT` (render/lighting.ts): exhaustive `Record`s). A turret is a block type with `turret` + its head in `TURRET_LOOKS` (`client/render/models.ts`); its head and its base (the block) can also have a 3D model (render/models/, by family: `DIRECT_HEADS` and `DIRECT_BASES` for direct-fire and explosive weapons, in weaponsDirect.ts; or, without code, a glTF file, `public/models/heads/<type>.glb` and `blocks/<type>.glb`, which takes precedence over both), which takes precedence over the box and tubes of `TURRET_LOOKS`: the barrel's muzzle falls `TurretSpec.barrel` in front of the head's axis, and the base rises as a pivot up to just under the head (`TurretSpec.height`), two values read from the catalog; `TurretSpec.head` (head dimensions) and `caliber` (barrel diameter) give its hitbox, to be kept in agreement with the model; a weapon without an aimable turret (mines, bombs) sits on a fixed turret (`TurretSpec.fixed`); a mine has its 3D model in `MINE_MODELS` (`client/render/models/mines.ts`, exhaustive over `MineWeaponId`, which TypeScript infers from the catalog: ribbed disc with a pressure plate, coils and dome of the EMP mine, tanks of the incendiary mine, vented canister of the smoke mine, mound and antennas of the bouncing mine, missile tube and radar of the anti-air mine, box on legs and antenna of the remote mine, tripod and lens of the sensor mine, dome on suction cups of the sticky mine; origin at the laying point) and its indicator light separately (`light`: color, position, `Blink` pattern), a glowing bead (`Assets.mineLightSource`) attached to the mine by `ProjectileView`, lit only once the mine is laid or stuck, which blinks at its own pattern (`blinkOn`: short flash, pulse, double flash, fast; offset from one mine to the next);
  - **prop:** its file `content/props/<type>.json` (`burns` if it is wooden; `$schema` for your editor: see "Element files") and one import line in `shared/catalog/props.ts` (`FILES`: its name makes the `PropType`; a lamp, its night light in `PROP_LIGHTS`, client/render/lightSources.ts, and its glass in `PropModels.setNight`) + `PROP_LOOKS` (`client/render/propModels.ts`: painted, with staves and a conical roof for the water tower, building material, tree (fir, oak, palm, kapok), cactus, car (whole or burned-out shell) or street lamp; blackened in steps) + `BREAK_SOUNDS` (`client/audio/sound.ts`) + its name in each language (`prop.<type>`), and its spots in the arenas (`ARENAS[id].props`, `shared/arena/layout.ts`); then `npm run schemas` (the map schema lists the prop types, the content catalog has its numbers). **glTF model (optional, no code):** a file `public/models/props/<type>.glb` (meters, +y up, origin at the bottom center of the footprint, a material named `glass` for a lamp's glass: [`models.md`](models.md)), then `npm run models` (it checks the file and writes the manifest, render/modelFiles.ts): it replaces the look drawn by code, in game and in the map editor; the collision shape stays the catalog's;
  - **arena:** its identifier in `ARENA_IDS` (`shared/arena/layout.ts`) and its code in `ARENA_SOURCES` (`shared/arena/sources.ts`, or a file of its own like valley.ts; at night: `sky.night`, and lamps, see "Night"), its name and its menu sentence in each language (`arena.<id>`, `arenaInfo.<id>`, i18n/locales/<language>/world.ts) and its map places (`places`: a `PlaceId`, a new one if needed, and its name `place.<id>`), nothing else on the client side (except its ambience, `AMBIENCES` in client/audio/sound.ts; the Canyon reuses the Fortress's, Volcanic Island the Valley's, the City the Trenches', the Harbor the Depot's): the menu offers it on its own, and the server adds it to its rotation. Its ground palette (`ground`: its constant in `ARENA_GROUNDS`, arena/grounds.ts, where a community map's `ground.preset` finds it), the look of its boxes (`StaticBox.look`, `BoxLook`: a material in `ArenaView.lookMaterial` — the harbor's in `HARBOUR_LOOKS`, a drawn texture and its tiling — and a color in `LOOK_COLORS` of the minimap), its navigation (`navGrid` or `nav`), its lava and its waterfalls are optional; they are drawn from its data (ArenaView: `groundColor(…, arena)`, the lava as emissive terrain triangles under a scrolling crust, the waterfalls as scrolling curtains, `update` every frame; minimap: darker deep water, orange lava) and `terrainFx` (render/fx/terrainFx.ts) makes the fumaroles smoke (the crater's plume can be seen from everywhere), the lava bubble and the waterfalls spray; then `npm run schemas` (the map schema lists the arenas, for a map's `ground.preset` and `ambience`) and `npm run map:export -- all` (its file in `content/arenas/`, the one the game plays: the tests check that each arena gives the same arena back through its file, and that the files are up to date; a small arena of a few KB can go in the bundle: its name in the patterns of client/maps/smallArenas.ts and gameArenas.ts; a new `ArenaLayout`, `StaticBox` or `PropSpot` field must be written by `mapFromArena`, read and built by the format: `tests/arenaExport.test.ts` lists them all, TypeScript demands a new one);
  - **community map:** a file `maps/<nom>.casse-map.json` (docs/maps.md), and nothing else: the menu, the server, the tests (`tests/communityMaps.test.ts`: validation and a battle on each one) pick it up on their own. A map package (an archive `.casse-map.zip`, or a folder with its `map.json`) in `maps/` is read by the server and `npm run map:check`, not shipped in the page (a player imports it, or receives it from a server). **Package resource:** a new kind (another family of models, another sound) is a path in `PACKAGE_FILES` and `packageFileLimit`, its check (like `modelIssue`, `ambienceIssue`), its field in `MapAssets` (layout.ts) read by `readAssets` (read.ts) and written by `readPackage`, its entry in `assetEntries`, its bound in `PACKAGE_LIMITS`, its place in the map schema (`npm run schemas`), and what the client does with it (client/maps/assets.ts gives its bytes). **Format field:** its type in `MapFile` (map/format.ts), its bounded reading in read.ts (a malformed field is a `MapIssue` `field`, never an exception), its conversion in build.ts, its check in validate.ts if needed, its paragraph in docs/maps.md, its place in the map schema (`mapSchema`, tools/schemas/schemas.ts; a bound in `MAP_RANGES` or `MAP_LISTS`, a choice in `MAP_CHOICES`, read.ts: the reader and the schema share them), then `npm run schemas` (a new field or enum value); a change that makes existing maps unreadable bumps `MAP_FORMAT` to the next version, with its migration (`MAP_MIGRATIONS`, read.ts). **Prefab:** a case of `MapPrefab` (format.ts), its reading (`readPrefab`), its expansion (`expandPrefab`, prefabs.ts: bounded, built at the origin then rotated and placed), its paragraph in docs/maps.md, its entry in the map schema (`mapDefs` and the `prefabs` list of `mapSchema`, tools/schemas/schemas.ts), then `npm run schemas`. **Map issue:** a `MapIssue` code and its text in each language (`mapIssue.<code>`, i18n/locales/<language>/maps.ts; `mapIssueText`, names.ts).
  - **AI mode:** `AiMode` (`shared/sim/entities.ts`) + its shade in the scoreboard (`MODE_TONES`, `client/hud/hud.ts`) + its name in each language (`aiMode.<mode>`, world.ts);
  - **game mode:** an entry in `GAME_MODES` (`shared/modes.ts`), and its name and its menu sentence in each language (`mode.<id>`, `modeInfo.<id>`, world.ts): the menu lists it on its own (`listed`).
  - **preset robot:** an entry in `PRESETS` (`shared/design/presets.ts`, without a name: its identifier makes up `PresetId`) + its name and its short description in each language (`bot.<id>`, `botTrait.<id>`, i18n/locales/<language>/bots.ts);
