  - **kill cause** (`KillCause`, other than weapons), **design flaw** (`DesignIssue`), **what a part reserves** (`ZonePart`), **credits line** (`RewardLine`), **server refusal** (`Refusal`): one more code, and its text in each language (`cause.<id>` in world.ts; `designIssue.<code>`, `zonePart.<id>` in design.ts; `reward.<kind>`, `refusal.<code>` in net.ts);
  - **player action (key):** an entry in `ACTIONS` (`client/input/bindings.ts`: section, when it applies, conflict-free default keys, checked by `tests/bindings.test.ts`) + its label in each language (`action.<id>`, and if needed its detail `actionDetail.<id>`, i18n/locales/<language>/keys.ts), then its effect in `Controls` (`trigger` for a press, `holding` for a held action); the key bindings screen shows it on its own. A key that does not type a character has its name in each language (`NAMED_KEYS`, `key.<code>`);
  - **effects system:** a file in `client/render/fx/` (an `FxFactory`) and a line in `FX_SYSTEMS` (fx/systems.ts).
  - **map editor palette item:** an entry in `PALETTE` (shared/map/palette.ts: its tab, its group, the element it places with its default values, or `trace` if it is traced point by point) and its name in each language (`editor.palette.<id>`, `NamedPaletteId`; a prop or a box take `prop.<type>`, `editor.boxKind.<sorte>`); a new kind of element in the format (a list of `MapFile`): its list in `ElementList` and `ElementOf` (shared/map/edit.ts), its form in `FORMS` and `formOf` (shared/map/fields.ts; its fields with the bounds of the reader, which tests/mapEditor.test.ts checks; their names `editor.field.<clé>`, `editor.kind.<formulaire>`), and its overlay if it does not show up in the game's rendering (`showOverlay`, editor/editorView.ts); a new field: one line in its form;
  - **map editor tool:** an entry in `TOOLS` and `TOOL_ACTIONS` (client/editor/tools.ts), its action (`editorTool<Nom>`, at the end of the "Map editor" section of `ACTIONS`), its name and its help text in each language (`editor.tool.<id>`, `editor.toolHint.<id>`), its panel (`EditorUi.renderPanel`, editor/editorUi.ts: TypeScript demands it) and what a click does with it (`MapEditor.onPointerDown`).
  - **melee weapon** (catalog/melee.ts, see "Melee weapons"): its file `content/melee/<id>.json` (`MeleeSpec`: motion, axis, hub, blade or arm, hit, `engage`; `$schema` for your editor) and one import line in `FILES` (catalog/melee.ts: its name makes the `MeleeId`); a block type with `melee`, its file in `content/blocks/` (mass, including that of the moving part, hit points, cost, `resist`, a solid `bond`, its unlock `price`) + `BLOCK_LOOKS`; its name and that of its block in each language (`melee.<id>`, `block.<type>`); its moving part in `MELEE_MODELS` (render/models/melee.ts: in the block's frame, origin at the hub, at angle 0, along `e1`; its dimensions read from the catalog) and its base in `MELEE_BASES`; its sight in `SIGHTS` (hud/reticle.ts); its sounds in `MELEE_SOUNDS` (client/audio/sound.ts: sounds from `SAMPLES`, or a borrowed sound played at another pitch, `MeleeSample`; null: the game synthesizes it, as it does while a file is not loaded). TypeScript demands these `Record`s (and `BLOCK_LOOKS` for the block); a new look (`look`) adds to `MELEE_ZONES` (clearance.ts) if what it reserves is made of several pieces, its name in each language (`zonePart.<look>`, design.ts), what sets it apart in the weapon bar (`hud.meleeTag.<allure>`, i18n/locales/<language>/hud.ts), and its synthesized hit in `energyHit` (sound.ts) if it is not a clang of steel, its effects in `meleeFx` (by look). A weapon that glows (an energy weapon) has its glowing part in `MELEE_GLOWS` (render/models/meleeEnergy.ts), and the voice of its loop in `MELEE_SOUNDS[id].tone`. Massless: `gyro`, `unbalance`, `mass`, `reaction` at 0 and `fling`, `recoil` at 0; one that goes out without power or in water: `quench`; one that sets fire to what it hits: `hit.ignite`; one that stuns locally: `hit.stun`. A new look also adds its pattern spacing (`STROBE_GAP`, meleeView.ts) and, for a rotor, its info-card sentences (`parts.meleeSays.<allure>`, `parts.meleeGyro.<allure>`, i18n/locales/<language>/parts.ts, read by garage/partInfo.ts). A blade with arms (`SweptRing.arms`) has a hitbox that rotates and, like an energy blade or a biting blade, the blur disc; a solid blade, a cylinder (or boxes, if hollow: `inner`); a ring around the robot (`inner` ≥ 0.8 m, `allAround`) and its web (`web`) are reserved as a tube and a disc. A rotor that bites the ground (an upright disc): `hit.ground`. A rotor driven by its block's engine (motion `driven`, `drive`) has no moving part of its own (`MELEE_MODELS` null: the engine's is enough) and is neither wielded nor commanded (`wielded`). Nothing else for a rotor (`spin`, `driven`), an arm (`swing`), a point (`thrust`) or a field (`field`): spin, hits, ricochet, gyroscope, imbalance, hitbox, network, AI, controls, gauge, rendering, effects, clearance, still drawing (`stillMelee`) and info card follow. A weapon that grinds instead of striking: a rotor with `bite` (`BiteSpec`: cutting power, feed direction `radial` or `axial`, grip, gear reduction, depth); the bite, its progression, its forces, the snapshot, the AI (charge, `meleeSkim`, `chargePoint`), the continuous effects, the grinding sound (`RotorTone.grind`, optional; `MELEE_SOUNDS[id].bite`, the recorded cutting loop) and the info card follow; a drill bit is a blade longer than it is wide (`isBit`). A new motion (like the flipper, the flail and the jaw, in combat/mechanics.ts): one more case in the `switch` of `updateMelee` (exhaustive over `motion`) with its functions in combat/melee.ts or a file of its own (build on `meleeFrame`, `stepRegime`, `powerOk`, `pathContact` (`headContact`, `tipContact`), `rotorContact`, `landHits`, `effectiveMass`, `precess`), its command in `operateMelee` (ai/brawl.ts) and `operatePlayerMelee` (sim/player.ts), its pose in `placeMoving` and `MeleeRig.pose` (render/meleeView.ts), its clearance in `meleeSolids`, its gauge in `meleeReadiness`, its rest position in `restSwing`, and the state the replica needs in the snapshot (snapshot.ts) if `spin`, `phase` and `swing` are not enough (a block and numbers per weapon: `mechanicsState`, `applyMechanics`); its extra moving pieces in `MELEE_PIECES` (render/models/melee.ts) and their still pose in `stillMechanics`. One test per rule in tests/melee.test.ts (tests/meleeEnergy.test.ts for energy weapons, tests/meleeBite.test.ts for the biting ones, tests/meleeMechanics.test.ts for mechanical strikes, tests/spinners.test.ts for the spinners, the propeller and the hitboxes).
  - **part that sticks out of its cell** (a head, blades, a nacelle, a leg, a wheel of a new kind…): its clearance in `blockZones` or `wheelZone` (shared/design/clearance.ts; what it reserves has a `ZonePart` and its name in each language, `zonePart.<id>`), with its dimensions as shared constants (like `BLADES`, `JET_NACELLE`, `headLift`) that the renderer reads too, and its still drawing in `stillBlockParts` or `stillWheelParts` (render/stillParts.ts): the garage then rejects whatever would get in its way, and the drawing matches what it reserves. Otherwise, the garage lets a block be placed through it.
- A robot design is JSON data (`RobotDesign`): `spawnBot` rejects a design that does not pass `validateDesign` (a single cockpit, all blocks connected, wheels on an existing block, a wheel underneath on a free cell, rollers stacked within their limits, no overlapping wheels, budget). A design from a previous version (a steered wheel was a fixed wheel with `steer: true`) is read back with `upgradeDesign`: the garage (storage.ts) and the server (room.ts) do so when loading it; `validateDesign` rejects a leftover `steer`. Two overlapping wheels push each other apart: one stays lifted, its suspension crushed (a standard wheel is 1.1 m in diameter: two wheels on the same side are at least three cells apart).
- Randomness goes through `shared/math/scalar.ts` (`rand`, `randSign`), so that it can be made deterministic later.
- **Deterministic math (`shared/math/det.ts`):** in `src/shared`, sines, cosines, logarithms and powers go through `dsin`, `dcos`, `dlog` and `dpow`, never `Math.sin`, `Math.cos`, `Math.log`, `Math.pow` or `**` (checked by `tests/architecture.test.ts`): their last bit depends on the JavaScript engine (V8 computes sin and cos like glibc, Rust's libm like FreeBSD: they differ on one value in two hundred, `log` on one in seven hundred, `pow` on one in seventy). Ours are FreeBSD's algorithms (fdlibm), written twice, here and in `crates/casse-sim/src/det.rs`, operation by operation, and give the same bits in both (`tests/native.test.ts`, 200,000 values each); `dpow` gives the simulation's exponents (2, 3, 1, 0, ½) exactly and the others as `exp(y·dlog(x))`. The other functions of `Math` the simulation uses (tan, asin, acos, atan, atan2, exp, sqrt, hypot, round, floor, ceil, min, max, sign) give the same bits in V8 and in the compiled module (`crates/casse-sim/src/jsmath.rs` reproduces V8's `Math.hypot`, a Kahan sum of squares scaled by the largest, and `Math.round`).

## Physical model of a robot (to be kept)

- Block grid of 0.5 m. Integer grid coordinates, keys `"x,y,z"`.
- Each block type has a nominal mass and hit points (`BLOCK_TYPES`). Armors form a scale: light (40 HP, 0.5 kg), normal (100 HP, 1 kg), heavy (250 HP, 2 kg, resists bullets); reactive armor (90 HP) stops explosions but is vulnerable to piercing. The cockpit has 800 HP: it is the structure rule that takes a wrecked robot out of the fight. Reference values (tuned with `npm run battle`): rifle 22 damage (two bullets for light armor), sniper 220 piercing, cannon 40 plus 120 splash over 1.2 m, missile 70 plus 150 over 1.4 m, laser 16 (`energy`, 10 shots per second, 0.15 kJ each), railgun 380 piercing (10 kJ per shot), shotgun 9 pellets of 20 (180 at point-blank range, scattered at a distance), rockets 20 plus 55 over 0.9 m (salvos of 8), plasma 13 `energy` 20 times per second up to ~27 m (3.5 s before overheating), bombs 60 plus 170 over 2.4 m (strings of 8, four per second), horizontal spinner 288 cut from a clean hit at full spin (plus 100 to each neighbor), pneumatic hammer up to 330 impact, circular saw 450 cut per second at full spin and ~234 under load, drill 480 and ~200 (both bore from one block to the next), vertical spinner 240 cut from a clean hit at full spin, drum 89 (231 on a wheel), shell spinner 173, cutting propeller 119. The standard wheel has 50 HP: three rifle bullets rip it off.
- **One rigid body per part** (`part`), with merged colliders. Never one body per block.
- All the parts of a robot share the same local frame. When a part is split off, the new body is created at the same pose as the parent, and the blocks keep their local coordinates.
- On destruction (or when a link breaks): the connected components are found (6-neighborhood, ignoring broken links), and the component that contains the cockpit keeps the body (otherwise the largest one). The others become new bodies, with the parent's velocity at the point of their center of mass.
- **Wheels:** a wheel is mounted on a face of a block (`WheelMount`: block, side `dx`/`dz`, or underneath `dy` = -1, offset `shift` of half a cell along the face; its axle always follows the x axis); a wheel that pivots (steered, caster), on a left or right side, is moved away from it so it can pivot without entering it (`steerClearance`); a track roller on a side stacks: `rise` (-2 to 2 cells higher or lower, with no block behind it) and `layer` (0 to 2 rollers outward); `wheelLocalPos` gives its position, and everything (physics, replica, overlaps) goes through it. A caster placed under a block touches the ground at the level of a heavy wheel placed on its side. A track roller hangs 12 cm below its block (`WheelTypeDef.drop`): without this ground clearance, the edge of the Tank's hull touched the slightest rise in the terrain and carried the robot. **Raycast wheel (`robot/wheels.ts`):** when mounted, a wheel has neither a body nor a joint. At each step (`stepWheels`, before the physics), a ray goes from the top of its suspension toward the bottom of the part and only sees the ground and the static scenery (nothing that moves: the wheel would climb onto it all at once); a ray starting inside an obstacle carries nothing. If it hits, a spring-damper (tuned for `ratedLoad`, with a bump stop at the end of travel) pushes the part at the contact point, and the tire applies there the motor's traction and its lateral grip, together within the limit of `grip × appui` (grip × load; friction circle: a pivoting track no longer holds sideways). Suspension and lateral grip are **implicit**: each wheel computes its force from the part's velocity at the end of the step and its effective mass at the contact point (mass and inertia of the part), then leaves this velocity to the following wheels, like a constraint solver; when explicit, the damper of a light part with widely spaced wheels (the Tank) reversed the motion from one step to the next. The forces of a part add up and go to Rapier in two calls. Steering angle (`steer`), travel (`travel`), rotation (`spin`, `spinRate`) and load (`load`) are plain numbers, from which `placeWheels` deduces, after the physics, the pose of the wheel (`Wheel.rb`) and of its hub carrier (`Wheel.hub`), `WheelPose`s read like bodies by the renderer, the shots and the network. The wheel's collider, attached to the part at its rest position, carries its mass, receives shots and pushes crates and robots. Ripped off (`releaseWheel`), it becomes a real body again (`Wheel.body`), which rolls and bounces. **Wreck:** once ripped off, it keeps a share of its hit points (`WHEEL_WRECK_HP`) and gives way under hits and explosions (`discardWheel` at the end of the damage batch: its body disappears, `wheelRemoved`, a burst of fragments); it also disappears on its own after `WHEEL_WRECK_LIFE` (30 s, `expireWheelWrecks`): no more invincible wheels cluttering the field. Once removed, it no longer has a part or a body, keeps a frozen pose (a freed body is never read again), and leaves the snapshots; the replica unmounts it when it receives `wheelGone` (ripped off and destroyed between two frames, it still thought it was mounted). A wheel follows the part of its supporting block (`moveWheel`), and it is ripped off if that block is destroyed (`releaseWheel`). Only the wheels of the cockpit's part are powered and steered.
- **Three kinds of wheels (`catalog/wheels.ts`):** fixed, steered (same size, `STEER_EXTRA` more hit points and more expensive to unlock: `steered`, `smallSteered`, `heavySteered`, given by `steeredVariant`), caster (`caster`). These are separate types (no longer a "steered" checkbox). **Steered wheels (`robot/steering.ts`):** the steering angle reaches the setpoint at the rate `steering.rate` (`aimWheel`); unpowered, a steered wheel keeps its steering angle and slides sideways (`LOOSE_STEER_GRIP`). `driveWheels` follows Ackermann geometry: center of rotation on the line of the fixed wheels (without a fixed wheel, level with the center of mass; casters do not count); each driven wheel turns at the speed of its own path (differential). Without a powered steered wheel, differential steering (`BotProfile.turn`). **Caster:** a free-swiveling wheel with no motor (`stallTorque` 0: never `engaged`, it rolls freely), which pivots toward the velocity of its contact point (`swivel`, robot/wheels.ts: the faster it rolls for its trail `caster.trail`, the faster it swivels, at most `SWIVEL_RATE`; in reverse, it turns around; only on the ground) and barely holds sideways (`CASTER_SIDE_GRIP`); its long downward travel keeps it in contact. It serves as a support: tail wheel of planes, a two-wheel-drive robot that pivots on the spot. Its swiveling goes through `Wheel.steer`, like a steering angle (snapshot, replica). The cruise speed of a garage robot does not count its casters (`cruiseSpeed`).
- **In-wheel motor:** torque = `stallTorque × (tension − ω / freeSpeed)`, capped at `stallTorque`, the wheel rolling without slipping (ω = contact point speed / radius); disengaged (`engaged` false: freewheeling, stun, brake), a slight drag (`FREEWHEEL_DRAG`); in ratchet freewheel (`overrun`, boost), torque that would go against the command is replaced by this slight drag; braked (`brake`), see "Boost and brake". The voltage follows the command with a ramp (`DRIVE_RAMP`). The suspension is tuned for the nominal load of the wheel type (`ratedLoad`) and sags beyond it.
- **Block orientation:** a block can have a `facing` (its up and its forward among the robot's 6 axes, 24 orientations). An engine pushes toward the top of its block: toward the top of the robot it is a lift rotor (`lift`), toward the front a booster (`cruise`), sideways a tail rotor (`trim`). A turret aims in its block's frame (`aimAngles`, `lineOfFireBlocked`, `buriedTurrets`: everything follows its up): placed on a side, it sweeps that side; under a block (`HANGING`), the underside. The mirror image of a block (`mirrorFacing`, `DesignGrid.pair`) reflects its up and its forward; for a shape whose symmetry swaps its left and its front (the corners), its forward becomes the reflection of its left, otherwise the mirror image would be the same corner rotated.
- **Shaped blocks (`catalog/shapes.ts`):** wedge (prism, 45°), corner (two slopes that meet, like a hip roof), inner corner (two crossed wedges: two convex pieces), round (quarter cylinder with six facets), round corner, low slope and high slope (a 27° slope over two blocks), half block; each shape is the intersection of the cube and half-spaces (block frame: left +x, up +y, forward +z), its underside solid, its slope facing up and forward; `shapePieces` turns them into polyhedra (faces, vertices, volume, computed once), whose convex hull the physics takes (`ColliderDesc.convexHull`) and whose faces the renderer takes (`shapeVertexData`, materials.ts: flat-shaded; an axis-aligned face takes the panel like the cube face that carries it, a slanted face takes it stretched). In light and normal armor (and heavy for the wedge and the corner, the glacis pieces): mass, hit points and cost in proportion to volume (`shapeFraction`); same material, same resistances. A ray that hits a slope finds its block through the grid cell (`hitTargetOf`). The grid remains the source of truth for everything else (links, lines of fire, splits): a shape occupies its whole cell.
- **Aerodynamics (`robot/aero.ts`):** flat-plate lift and drag per wing or control surface; the mixer `steerSurfaces` converts the stick into deflections based on the actual effect of each control surface. **Runs (`robot/surfaces.ts`):** control surfaces end to end along their span (same type, same up, same forward, linked: same part, link intact) form a single control surface (`flapRuns`, `flapGroups` for the cockpit's): the mixer reasons per run, with the combined effect of its blocks, and all its blocks deflect by the same angle (a run that has just formed starts from the average of its blocks). A block destroyed in the middle cuts the run in two, a part ripped off takes its own with it (and the mixer only deflects the cockpit's). Two runs one behind the other remain two control surfaces: the mixer uses the difference between their lever arms (with a single set per aileron over two chord rows, the Plane could no longer hold its altitude in a tight turn and crashed in `tests/aircraft.test.ts`). Per run rather than per block, a one-piece elevator no longer helps with roll: the Plane rolls at 96°/s instead of 101 (stick fully over, manual flight), the Bomber at 97 instead of 101, the Fighter at 143 instead of 147; pitch unchanged (74, 68, 94°/s); the AI's planes do not crash more (three planes per team, six 150 s battles, the same flight time: 52 fatal impacts instead of 68 at the Depot, 133 instead of 127 in the Valley). The hinge (`hingeChord`, drawing only): at the leading edge if a block precedes the run, at the middle of its chord otherwise (single-block aileron); `carrierCell`: the run of control surfaces just ahead, which carries the second one. The network snapshot gives only one deflection per run, named after one of its blocks; the replica finds the same runs from its live blocks and their parts.
- **Plane pilot (`ai/pilot.ts`):** instruments (`instruments`), desired bank (L1 guidance on the circuit for the AI, or on its attack circuit for a bomber, `bombingRun`; `bankToward`), then flight (`fly`: attitude, stick, integral elevator trim, throttle). The AI only goes through that; the player, through `flyMouse` or `flyManual` (see below), and `holdPlane` while their warhead is in flight. **Turns:** it does not bank more than its wings can carry at its speed (`sustainableBank`: maximum lift of the surfaces in level flight, before stalling, relative to weight, with a margin; the Plane at 13.5 m/s holds ~45°, a large wing or more speed allow more), at most 43° for the AI; in a turn, the elevator pulls extra based on the load factor (`TURN_PULL`); too far below the desired altitude, the bank decreases (`LOW_START`, `LOW_SPAN`: a turn held at its tightest ended on the ground), likewise near the ground (the ground below: the terrain ahead of the plane makes it climb, but no longer limits its bank) or when it sinks. The control surfaces deflect 29° (at 20°, the Plane only rolled at 17°/s; it now rolls at ~45°/s, with full-chord ailerons at the wingtip). **Limits:** the autopilot takes over beyond the walls, but at least `CIRCUIT_ROOM` (18 m) beyond the circuit: in a small arena, the circuit runs along the walls, and it took over as soon as you turned outward. An arena can impose a floor altitude on the AI (`ArenaBounds.airFloor`: 23 m in the Valley, above the chimney and the trees; planes spawn there at that height, and the player can climb 12 m higher than elsewhere). Without an engine it glides (`gliding`). **Weakened engines** (`Instruments.power`, the share of their full thrust that its propulsion engines can deliver): below `WEAK_SLOW` of its cruise speed, speed comes before altitude (it gradually takes the gliding attitude instead of pitching up until it stalls); low (`GROUNDED` m) without the thrust to climb back (`TAKEOFF_POWER`), it lands: throttle cut, wings level, nose slightly up (`FLARE`), then it stops, stranded (`stranded`: the match self-destructs it if it stays there). Otherwise it skimmed the ground at full speed and hit the scenery, or rolled along its circuit until it hit a wall (the Plane with its engine at 25% crashed seven times out of eight; `tests/engineWear.test.ts` lands it on the Valley's runway). **Manual flight (`flyManual`):** the player holds the stick and the throttle, with no help except damping on each rotation (`ROLL_DAMP`, `PITCH_DAMP`, `YAW_DAMP`: stick released, the plane stops turning and keeps its attitude, the elevator trim adjusting itself, `AUTO_TRIM`); loops, rolls, flat turns with the rudder, gliding with the throttle cut; beyond its limit, the autopilot takes over (`awayFrom`, as tight as its wings allow: `returnBank`, `RETURN_BANK`) and holds the altitude it was flying at (`ai.alt`, at least `RETURN_ALT`). **With the mouse (`flyMouse`, `MouseFlight`):** as in Robocraft, the plane goes where you look. An instructor (`instructor`) judges the deviation on the flight path (the velocity), not on the nose (in level flight, the wing lifts with the nose slightly up: a direction straight ahead seemed to be below the nose, and it rolled the wrong way, in a spiral): almost straight ahead (less than `LEVEL_OFF` rad), it corrects with the elevator and the rudder, wings level (`PATH_GAIN`, trim `TRIM_RATE`); beyond that, it rolls to put the desired direction above the nose and pulls (fully at `LEVEL_OFF + BANK_SPAN`); a direction below the nose, within `PUSH_MAX`, is taken by pushing; a roughly horizontal direction, to the side or behind, that would require rolling more than `ROLL_FREE`, it takes in a banked turn without exceeding what its wings can carry (`sustainableBank`, between `MIN_HARD_BANK` and `HARD_BANK`, less near the ground, `low`, and less if it sinks below the desired direction, `SINK_SPAN`): it no longer lies on its side; but a direction above it (the raised view that keeps going) is taken by pulling, without rolling: a loop, on its back, then it comes back. It does not pull (or push) beyond `AOA_LIMIT` of angle of attack (`AOA_GAIN`): pulling more made it stall. The rudder follows the turn (coordinated turn) and, almost straight ahead, aims a little (`RUDDER_AIM`). The player's throttle (`power`) sets a desired speed, from `SLOW_SPEED` to `FAST_SPEED` times its cruise speed (raw throttle meant nothing from one plane to another: at 60%, the Fighter went three times faster than the Plane), more when climbing (it does not stall in a loop); below `ENGINE_CUT`, engine cut, it glides. With the keyboard, without a look direction (`mouseTarget`): the target direction is the flight path, turned by `KEY_TURN` toward the side of the turn, raised by `KEY_PULL` above the nose (pull up: held, a loop) or lowered by `KEY_PUSH` (dive); with nothing pressed, it flies straight and level; the rudder, a flat turn. **Limit:** the instructor checks it where the plane will be in `BOUNDARY_LEAD` s (`awayFrom(f, true)`), and beyond it, instead of following the look, it aims for the planes' circuit, a little above the horizon (`RETURN_CLIMB`), at cruise throttle (`CRUISE_SETTING`), taking full control halfway: the autopilot, which gives priority to altitude at the foot of the mountains, turned too wide, and the plane hit the Valley's invisible limit. Measured (`tests/mouseFlight.test.ts`, in the Valley): looking 69° to the left and 11° up, the flight path gets there (67°, 12°), likewise to the right; the view raised continuously at full throttle, a full loop (360° of pitch, on its back, never lower than 48 m), then it levels off toward the center; pull up held on the keyboard, a loop too; turning with the keyboard, 93° in 4 s; full throttle 19 m/s, idle 13, without changing its path. `fly` splits into `flightControls` (stick and throttle of the autopilot) and `applyControls`. A plane's propeller loses thrust only with airspeed (`propellerFactor`): a thrust "hole" at high speed comes from the autopilot reducing the throttle to hold `profile.speed`.
- **Hover (`flyToward`):** desired force and torque using the body's actual inertia, distributed among the thrust axes of the thrusters by bounded least squares (`boundedLeastSquares`) with priorities: attitude > thrust > yaw. When the player pilots it (`yaw`: desired yaw rate and assistance), yaw is actively held (`YAW_KP_PILOT`), the propellers may become more unbalanced for it (`YAW_SHARE_PILOT`, `WEIGHT_YAW_PILOT`), and the cockpit's gyroscope makes it pivot as long as A or E is held and for a moment afterward (`GYRO_KP`, `GYRO_ACCEL`): the reaction torques of a Drone's propellers only give ~0.2 rad/s² (it needed 7 s to stop turning). Outside these moments, the gyroscope does nothing: a helicopter that loses its tail rotor still spins on itself. A tilt rotor (`gimbal`) adds two tilt axes (swashplate, response `TILT_TIME`); a tail rotor (`trim`) makes yaw firmly controllable. No assistance: with 3 thrusters out of 4 the Drone can no longer hold its altitude; with 2, it falls; without a tail rotor, the Helicopter spins on itself. A helicopter's rotor must be directly above its center of mass, otherwise its tilt saturates.

## Roadmap

Announced goals: menu (done), robot editor (done), block toughness (done), armors (done), weapons (done), steered wheels and tracks (done), wings (done), boosters and rotors (done), legs and hover pads (done), piloted robot and matches (done), sound (done), combat AI (done), flyable plane and touch controls (done), multiplayer (first version done).

1. Multiplayer, continued: compress the snapshots (~8 KB per frame today, 160 KB/s per client: 16-bit positions, "smallest three" rotations, send only what moves), client-side prediction of the player's robot, send each player only what their team has spotted (`sim.detected`), several rooms, WebTransport.
2. Balancing, continued (`npm run battle`): with equal-cost teams, matches are close (8 battles, 29 preset robots: 4 wins on each side, scores from 15-12 to 7-15). **To examine:** on October 1, 2026, with 34 robots, the reds win 7 battles out of 8 (scores from 15-13 to 4-15), and this was already the case before the effects and their gameplay changes (damaged engines, engine fires, burning wood, fire puddles): the cause predates them. These changes raise deaths by fire from 13 to 17 over 8 battles, with no other difference beyond the noise. Big robots remain the safest (Truck: 24 kills for 5 deaths) and small ones die a lot, but relative to their cost, a small robot kills as much as a big one. The robots of the new weapons: Warhead Carrier 16 for 7 (the AI guides its warhead perfectly: the warhead launcher costs 90), Brawler 13 for 16 (it charges), Tracker 7 for 7, Furnace 4 for 6; the Swarm kills little, its rockets often get lost. The Bomber often hits (one pass over a stationary tank destroys it), but flies over the enemy weapons and does not live long (in 8 battles: 2 kills for 5 deaths, average life 33 s, mostly shot down: its tail or a wing gets ripped off, and it dives). Remaining: the Stilt often tips over (that's how it is designed), the Plane and the Bomber still crash when a wing or the tail is damaged; the AI could dodge missiles.
3. Garage, continued: unify wheels and blocks in a catalog of components (the shape, as a property of the placement rather than of the type, would open all materials to all shapes); rotate or flip a copied region. Done: copying a region, wheels offset by half a cell, center of mass, shaped blocks, face-based orientation and an oriented preview.
4. Bundle: Rapier "compat" embeds its WebAssembly in base64 (4.2 MB of the 5.8 MB bundle); `@dimforge/rapier3d` (separate WebAssembly, loaded in parallel) would reduce it further, at the cost of configuring Vite and the server.
5. Performance: on the rendering side, thin-instance batches brought a battle from ~1,070 down to ~290 meshes, and Babylon's work on meshes from ~6.5 to ~1.4 ms per frame (measured in Chrome without a GPU). On the simulation side, a combat step with 12 robots costs ~3.2 ms, of which ~1 ms is physics and ~0.5 ms wheels (`npm run bench`); with jointed wheels, it was ~4.6 ms, of which ~2.4 ms was physics. Memory measured as stable: server, 40 minutes of back-to-back matches at ~17 MB of JS heap and ~205 MB of RSS; client, ~40 MB of heap after the first battle (ephemeral effects are capped at `MAX_PUFFS` and share one geometry). Jointed wheels accounted for 87% of the physics cost (44 wheels = 97 bodies and 97 joints): they are now raycasts (see "Physical model of a robot"). Still to gain: the AI (~1.1 ms).
6. Community maps (see "Community maps"): the engine is done (format, terrain import, validation, loading, multiplayer, the example map Lake Vale, `docs/maps.md`). Measured on October 14, 2026 (`BATTLE_ARENA=val-du-lac npm run battle`, machine loaded by other computations): 2 blue wins, 4 red, 2 draws, matches from 196 to 240 s (almost all ended on time), ground robots stuck 16.3% of the time (like Volcanic Island; the Valley 5 to 8%), average step 9.6 ms, 2 drownings in the lake. The map editor is done (step 2: see "The map editor"; terrain imported and sculpted, the palette, the traced paths, the water, the settings, the issues, the test match against the AI, the draft), and free spawn lines too. Remaining: less time stuck in Lake Vale (the riverbanks, the village?); in the editor, a rectangle selection, a box's handles (to resize it with the mouse), an exact preview of what is about to be placed rather than a box, editing `rests` and `doors`, a piecewise check of the map (on Lake Vale, a change rebuilds the whole arena: ~80 ms); slanted bridges the AI can follow (their decks are read along the axes); boxes per map rather than those of the game's arenas (an ambient soundscape and prop models per map: done, in map packages); a place to share maps between players beyond a server's rotation and a file passed by hand (map packages travel as one `.casse-map.zip`).
7. Content: the Canyon (October 6, 2026; `BATTLE_ARENA=canyon npm run battle`, on a machine loaded by other computations): the reds win 7 battles out of 8 (as at the Depot these days), matches from 156 to 240 s (three ended on time), ground robots stuck 20.5% of the time (Depot measured at the same time and in the same way: 14.6%), average step 22 ms versus 11.6 at the Depot; over 20 s of battle, 14.4 ms versus 10.3 in the Valley under the same load (physics +1.2 ms: the terrain at 1 m resolution; AI driving +1.5 ms; the nav graph itself ~0.1 ms, the avoidance rays ~0.4 ms, measured separately: `ia › réseau`, `ia › rayons`). The Plane and the Bomber die there mostly from impacts (7 out of 15, 14 out of 18), but six planes without combat crash more often in the Valley (5 out of 6 in 120 s, 3 in the Canyon): this predates it. Coming up from the canyon floor to the arch (November 12, 2026): the AI stalled at the bend of the Mesa's ledge trail, a wheel or a track on its edge (the links of the nav graph and the robot's shortcuts ran along the trail's edge: a 1 m margin, judged on two lines only; the trail was 6.5 m wide, flat over ~5 m; its bends had a step on their inner edge), and under the viaduct it hit one of the two legs of a bent (the Buggy lost a wheel there). Rounding the terrain to 16 bits changed no link of the graph: it only tipped runs that got through by a hair. Now (`walkable` judges the whole band, links at 2 m, the robot's own half-width, smooth trails, an 8 m ledge trail, one post per bent): from six starts in the bed and three seeds, the Buggy, the Trike and the Tank reach the arch 51 times out of 54, in 30 to 120 s (before: 8 out of 18 with seed 6); from the plateau, 9 out of 9 from three starts (before: 7). In eight battles there (`BATTLE_ARENA=canyon npm run battle`, same seeds, a machine loaded by other computations), ground robots are stuck 24.0% of the time against 25.9% before, the step costs no more (15.9 against 17.6 ms, the latter under a heavier load), self-destructs 8 and 8: the climb was not what kept them stuck in battle. To do: less time stuck; a robot that crosses the fill of a trail at an angle instead of entering it at its end can still sit on its belly on the crest (the Buggy coming from the east, at the foot of the north wall trail: the 3 failures out of 54), and the rocks of the bed near the foot of the trails break a wheel of the Buggy when it makes a U-turn into them; the cost of driving, a recorded desert ambience. Volcanic Island: the AI's planes sometimes hit its invisible limit (as in the Valley: the pilot does not take it into account), the AI backs up there more often than elsewhere (6.5% of the time versus 2.1% in the Valley, around the hangar and the village), and it needs its own ambience. The two terrain-based navigations (the Island's grid, the Canyon's graph) could become a single one. The City: the Buggy stays stuck at the top of the south embankment of the expressway, the Truck does not reach the roof of the parking garage (the Tank takes more than a minute); it needs its own ambience, and full battles to balance it (38 trips from a spawn line to a zone out of 40 succeed). More arenas and modes; a screen to spend one's server credits without going through a garage robot; accounts rather than a browser token; a minimap for the Valley. Done: eight arenas (including four large maps: the Valley, with its destructible buildings and trees, the Canyon, its cliffs and its trails, Volcanic Island, its lava and its sea, and the City, its streets, its buildings that collapse floor by floor and its cars that explode), Zone Capture and King of the Hill, twenty-eight weapons (including bombs, nine mines, the repair beam that drains life, nanites and the barrier projector) and fifteen melee weapons (the horizontal spinner and the pneumatic hammer, on the melee weapon foundation; the laser blade, the plasma whip, the shock lance and the crusher field, energy-based; the circular saw and the drill, which bite continuously; the flipper, the flail and the crusher jaw, mechanical; the vertical spinner, the drum, the shell spinner and the cutting propeller), flares and missiles that can be shot down in flight, the regrowth of lost blocks, 46 preset robots (including the Reaper, the Pounder, the Duelist, the Urchin, the Sawyer, the Borer, the Catapult, the Crusher, the Thresher, the Tumbler, the Drummer and the Whirlwind, with melee weapons, the Bomber, classes: Sapper, Mechanic, Scout, Guardian, Angel, the jet Fighter and the Dragster, and three ships made of shaped blocks: Frigate, Manta, Bastion), shaped blocks (wedges, corners, rounds, slopes, half blocks) and face-based orientation in the garage, the propeller and the turbojet, credits and unlocks, progression kept by the server in multiplayer.
8. Melee weapons, continued (see "Melee weapons" and the recipe in "Architecture rules"): the proposal's weapons are all done (the laser blade and the crusher field could also bite continuously, `BiteSpec`); their recorded sounds are done (see "Sound": nobody has listened to them yet, they were chosen based on their analysis; to be judged in game: the base volumes, the flail's chain that only has treble, the jaws' snap a bit thin); remaining: a hitbox for the tip of a lance, the plate of a flipper, the ball of a flail and the jaws, an AI that presents a side-mounted weapon. Spinners: measured on October 7, 2026 (`SPINNER_BATTLE=1`, six battles at the Depot, a Tumbler, a Drummer and a Whirlwind per team, the rest drawn at random; three wins on each side): Whirlwind 12 kills for 12 deaths, 359 hits on robots (1.3 kJ on average), 12 wheels ripped off, 81 s flipped over, 3 self-destructs (before its ring scraped the ground when upside down: 6 for 21, 364 s flipped over, 13 self-destructs); Drummer 7 for 25, 448 hits (0.38 kJ: small repeated hits), 11 wheels ripped off (a previous battle: 21 for 30, 18 wheels); Tumbler 6 for 24, 228 hits (0.66 kJ), 159 s flipped over (it flips itself over when its disc bites the ground); no propeller hit a robot (the AI's planes fly high); the "contact" step: 0.13 ms of a 2 ms step. The Tumbler and the Drummer die three to four times more than they kill, like the Reaper; a tilted ring that rolls along the ground (see "Spinners and cutting propeller"); the vertical spinner barely tilts its robot in a turn with physics alone (see "Spinners and cutting propeller"); an AI that approaches with its vertical spinner from below and keeps its disc away from the ground; the mass of a ring distributed over the ring (the robot's inertia); the propeller's gyroscopic effect (neglected: it disturbed the autopilot). Energy weapons: the Duelist and the Urchin die twice as much as they kill (like the Reaper and the Pounder); the whip hits too early in its arc (an AI that waits until its target is in the middle of the arc, or a head energy less dependent on how much of the arc has been traveled); a local stun of the cockpit (the whole robot, more briefly?); an AI that aims at its target's wheels or turrets with the lance; a field that arms on the trigger rather than on its own; the cost of the field in the step (~6% with two Urchins). Biting blades: the Sawyer and the Borer die four times more than they kill (see "Melee weapons"); a saw that bites while skimming mostly gashes its target's skin (it rarely kills: aim it toward the cockpit like the drill?), a cut that depends on the pressing force, a drill bit that does not pass through intact blocks. Mechanical strikes: the Catapult, the Crusher and the Thresher kill little (see "Mechanical strikes"); the Crusher, being slow, does not catch a fast robot that keeps its distance, and its prey often dies of something other than its jaw (aim for the cockpit, like the drill? bite harder?); an AI that places the flipper under a fleeing robot, that looks for a post to get an enemy's chain wrapped around it, or that drops its prey in front of a wall or at the water's edge; the flail's chain only wraps around static scenery (not around a robot), the ball does not hit its teammates; the plate and the jaws have no collider (what is on top gets lifted, nothing pushes them). Balancing: the Reaper and the Pounder die two to three times more than they kill (see "Melee weapons"); leads: an approach under cover (they cross open ground under fire), more armor on the front, a Pounder less prone to rearing up.
9. **Engine plan (November 2026): 120 Hz in the browser, a lighter simulation, open content formats.** Measured with `npm run perf`: the game is bound by the CPU, on a single thread (Babylon preparing each frame, the simulation, the effects), not by the GPU. Five phases, three columns in parallel (rendering, simulation, content); the Worker comes before the compiled code, and the catalogs become data before the compiled code. **1. Guardrails** (done): `npm run perf`, `npm run render:check` in CI, WebGL context loss handled, reference battles and determinism test, JSON Schemas of maps and robot designs. **2. Smoothness:** interpolate poses in solo (done: see "Loop"), lighten Babylon in WebGL (done: static scenery merged and batched, shadow casters sorted per cascade, see "Shadows"; the glow lightened rather than replaced by a bloom, see "Optional lighting"; the HUD no longer reads the layout back; next, measured by the CPU profile: the shadow maps, 13 to 19% of the page's thread, could keep the static scenery's shadows instead of redrawing them every frame); the TypeScript simulation in a Worker behind the multiplayer contract (done: see "Solo in a Worker"; 79 frames per second in the reference battle); content in open formats: glTF models (Blender; done for the props, the blocks, the turret heads and the wheels, without code: see "3D models of props, blocks, turret heads and wheels" and `docs/models.md`; still to do: what moves on its own, the melee weapons' moving parts, the blades of rotors and propellers, the track rollers and their belt, and the visible rounds of a head, which stay the code's), the game's elements as open, documented data (done as an export: `content/catalog.json` and its schema, generated from the TypeScript catalogs, see "Content catalog"; started as files the code itself reads, one per element: the blocks, the wheels, the weapons, the melee weapons and the props, `content/<family>/<id>.json`, their schemas generated from the code's types, the identifiers and their exhaustiveness checks kept in TypeScript, see "Element files"; what is computed stays computed: the shapes, the shaped blocks; still to do: reading them in the compiled simulation, and the preset robots if they ever become data). Target: 60 frames per second in the reference battle on a GTX 1660 Ti Max-Q laptop, solo smooth at 144 Hz. **3. WebGPU:** the WebGPU engine with WebGL 2 as fallback (done: the "Renderer" setting, see "Technical choices already made"; our GLSL shaders converted by Babylon; WebGL stays the default, measured faster on the CPU), checked by `npm run render:check -- --renderer=webgpu` and measured by `npm run perf -- --renderer=webgpu`; the game's arenas exported as map files and played from them (done: `content/arenas/`, an exact round trip, terrain included since the code's terrains are rounded to 16 bits once; the game, the solo Worker and the server play these files, the large arenas downloaded on demand: see "The game's arenas, played from their files"), map packages (done: JSON and a 16-bit PNG terrain, license, prop models and an ambient sound of the map's own, one zip file to exchange; read by the tool, imported, played, edited and exported by the game, played and served by the server, resources over HTTP by their SHA-256: see "Map packages"; still to do: a package's own block, wheel and turret-head models, music, new kinds of boxes). **4. Compiled code:** the simulation in Rust (native Rapier) compiled to WebAssembly for the Worker and the Node server, behind the contract of phase 2, validated by the ported tests and against the reference battles (started: `crates/casse-sim`, see "Compiled simulation"; ported module by module from the leaves up rather than in one go, each one checked to the bit against its TypeScript, so that every intermediate state ships; the simulation that owns its Rapier world drives 21 ground robots and crashes them into each other and into the props to the bit in the probe: first milestone (driving), second milestone (collisions and damage: `resolveCollisions`, `inflict`, the weak links, destroyed blocks and their debris, splits, torn-off wheels, which free and create bodies and colliders) and third milestone (the props: crates, barrels, gas cylinders, anchored walls and trees, the breach of an anchored prop, explosions and their blast in a chain, the fire of blocks and of wood) and fourth milestone (repair: modules that mend blocks and wheels and regrow lost blocks, those left on a torn-off part included; the ground fire puddles) and fifth milestone (the AI's driving without combat, in the four small arenas: patrols, navigation around the scenery and up the ramps, avoidance rays, getting unstuck, fleeing and charging after a hit; the detection) done; next, in that order: turrets and projectiles, with the AI's tactics (`planCombat`), which come with them (with combat on, the turrets draw at random every step), and with them the repair beam, the nanites, and the flamethrower and the fire mine, which light puddles in the game; supports, engines and flight; melee weapons; the large arenas (their terrain and rocks, the nav grid and graph, bridges, tall ramps, the city); then the whole step behind the Worker's contract, against the reference battles); full WebGPU (recorded draw lists, particles computed on the GPU, WGSL shaders). **5. Then:** client-side prediction (Rapier is deterministic).
